Algorithm Design & Analysis · part 6 of 7

Shortest Paths, MST & Flow

The weighted-graph problems are the three questions a graph with numbers on its edges is asked most: the cheapest route between vertices (shortest paths), the cheapest connected skeleton of the whole graph (the minimum spanning tree), and the most that can be pushed from a source to a sink through capacitated edges (maximum flow) — with the bottleneck that limits it (the minimum cut). This page builds each from one primitive, relaxation, and one invariant per algorithm: Dijkstra and exactly where it needs non-negative weights, Bellman-Ford and the negative cycle, one pass over a DAG, Floyd-Warshall and Johnson for all pairs, A* and what an admissible heuristic buys; the cut and cycle properties and the three MST algorithms with union-find built and its inverse-Ackermann bound explained; residual graphs, augmenting paths, the max-flow min-cut theorem, Ford-Fulkerson's pathology, Edmonds-Karp's bound, push-relabel, and the reductions — matching, disjoint paths, segmentation — that make flow the workhorse it is. Every count a figure shows is measured by running the real routine under a counter, and every answer is cross-checked against brute force in its readout.

Data Structures & Algorithms Relaxation · optimality conditions Dijkstra · Bellman-Ford · DAG · A* Floyd-Warshall · Johnson Cut property · Kruskal · Prim · Borůvka Union-find · inverse Ackermann Max-flow min-cut · Edmonds-Karp · push-relabel Matching · disjoint paths · min-cut segmentation 23 visualizations

01 What the weighted-graph problems are

A weighted graph is a graph G = (V, E) together with a weight function w : E → ℝ; the weight of a path is the sum of the weights of its edges; and the three weighted-graph problems are to find, from those sums, the cheapest path between vertices (shortest paths), the cheapest edge set that keeps every vertex connected (the minimum spanning tree), and the largest amount that can be routed from a source to a sink when each edge's weight is read as a capacity (maximum flow), together with the bottleneck that limits it (the minimum cut). Everything on the Graph Representations & Traversal page treated an edge as a unit step, so that "distance" meant edge count and BFS found it. One number per edge changes the questions, not the graph: the representations are the same adjacency lists with one more field, the traversals are the same loops with one more comparison, and the whole of this page is what that comparison — relaxation — does in the hands of a dozen algorithms.

the objectsa path p = ⟨v₀, v₁, …, vₖ⟩ with every (vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) ∈ E; its weight w(p) = ∑ᵢ₌₁ᵏ w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) shortest-path weight δ(u, v) = min { w(p) : p is a path u ⇝ v } if some path exists δ(u, v) = ∞ if v is not reachable from u δ(u, v) = −∞ if a cycle of negative weight lies on some u ⇝ v walk (§03: the min does not exist) a SHORTEST PATH u ⇝ v is any path with w(p) = δ(u, v). Several may exist; every algorithm here returns one, through predecessor pointers π. the three problem families, one line eachSINGLE-SOURCE SHORTEST PATHS given s: compute δ(s, v) for every v ∈ V, and a shortest-paths tree rooted at s (§02–§11, §15) ALL-PAIRS SHORTEST PATHS compute the V × V matrix D[i][j] = δ(i, j) (§12–§14) MINIMUM SPANNING TREE G undirected, connected: find T ⊆ E with (V, T) a tree and w(T) = ∑ₑ∈T w(e) minimum (§16–§20) MAXIMUM FLOW / MINIMUM CUT capacities c(u, v) ≥ 0, source s, sink t: find a flow f of maximum value |f| = ∑ᵥ f(s, v) − ∑ᵥ f(v, s), and the s–t cut (S, T) of minimum capacity c(S, T) = ∑ᵤ∈S ∑ᵥ∈T c(u, v) — which are EQUAL (§22) (§21–§26) the one primitive every shortest-path algorithm is built fromRELAX(u, v): if d[v] > d[u] + w(u, v) then d[v] ← d[u] + w(u, v), π[v] ← u ("can the edge (u, v) improve v's estimate?") every algorithm of §04–§15 is INITIALISE (d[s] = 0, all other d = ∞) followed by relaxations in SOME order — the algorithms differ ONLY in the order (§02)
the template — every algorithm on this page is an instance of itSTATE what is maintained: a distance estimate d[v] and predecessor π[v] per vertex; or a growing edge set X; or a flow f and its residual graph INVARIANT the one property every step preserves and the answer is read from when the loop ends: Dijkstra "every vertex in the finalised set S has d[v] = δ(s, v)" §04–§05 Bellman-Ford "after round i, every vertex whose shortest path has ≤ i edges has d[v] = δ(s, v)" §07–§08 DAG pass "when v is processed, every predecessor of v in the order is final" §09 Floyd-Warshall "after round k, D[i][j] is the shortest i ⇝ j path using intermediates from {1 … k} only" §13 A* "with a consistent heuristic, when v is expanded d[v] = δ(s, v)" (Dijkstra's invariant, reweighted) §15 Kruskal/Prim "the edge set X is contained in SOME minimum spanning tree" (the cut property) §16–§19 augmenting "f is a valid flow; when no augmenting path remains, the reachable set S is a minimum cut" §21–§24 ORDER the sequence of relaxations / edge choices / augmentations — by tentative distance, by rounds, by topological order, by weight, by BFS COST (number of steps) × (cost of choosing the next step) — a priority queue's log V, a sort's log E, a union-find's α(V), a BFS's V + E

Where it sits

This page sits on three others and repeats none of them. The traversals — BFS's queue and its shortest-path theorem for unit weights, DFS and its parenthesis structure, topological order, strongly connected components — are Graph Representations & Traversal, §10–§24; Dijkstra's algorithm is BFS with the queue replaced by a heap and the assignment d[v] = d[u] + 1 replaced by RELAX, and that page's §28 states the hand-off in exactly those terms. The cut property — the one lemma that makes every minimum-spanning-tree algorithm correct — is proved as the greedy-choice lemma on Greedy Algorithms, §20, which also explains in what sense Dijkstra is greedy; §16 restates it and adds the cycle property, and §17–§20 build the algorithms on it. The DP view — Bellman-Ford as "shortest walks with at most k edges", Floyd-Warshall as "intermediate vertices from {1 … k}", and the one-pass solution when the graph is a DAG — is the hand-off in Dynamic Programming, §21; here the same recurrences are run as graph algorithms with their proofs, their negative cycles, and their measured costs. What is new on this page is the theory that ties the three together: relaxation and its six properties (§02), from which every single-source proof follows in a few lines; the cut and cycle properties, from which every spanning-tree proof follows; and augmenting paths, residual graphs and the max-flow min-cut theorem, from which everything about flow follows.

The thesis: one primitive, one invariant per algorithm

The shortest-path algorithms differ far less than their reputations suggest. Each initialises d[s] = 0 and every other d[v] = ∞, then performs relaxations — nothing else ever changes a d value — and each is characterised entirely by the order in which it relaxes edges: Dijkstra relaxes the out-edges of vertices in increasing order of final distance, which its greedy extraction achieves only when weights are non-negative; Bellman-Ford relaxes every edge, V − 1 times over, in any order at all; the DAG algorithm relaxes each edge once, in topological order of its tail; A* relaxes in order of d + h. Because relaxation only ever lowers an estimate and never below the truth (§02, the upper-bound property), and because relaxing the edges of a shortest path in sequence forces the estimates along it to their true values whatever else happens in between (the path-relaxation property), a correctness proof for any of these algorithms is a proof that its order relaxes the edges of every shortest path in sequence. The spanning-tree algorithms have the same shape with a different lemma: each grows an edge set that is always contained in some minimum spanning tree, and the cut property says which edge is safe to add next. The flow algorithms have it too: each maintains a feasible flow and improves it along an augmenting path in the residual graph, and the max-flow min-cut theorem says that when no such path exists the flow is maximum. So the page has three invariants, not thirty, and each algorithm is an invariant plus a schedule plus a data structure that makes the schedule cheap.

What "weight" means, and what it does not

A weight is any real number attached to an edge; the algorithms do not care whether it is a distance in kilometres, a latency, a cost, a log-probability or a capacity, but they care a great deal about its sign and its type. Non-negative weights license Dijkstra (§04–§05); negative weights force Bellman-Ford (§07) or a DAG (§09) or Johnson's reweighting (§14), and a negative cycle makes shortest paths undefined (§03). Small integer weights admit bucket queues that beat every comparison-based heap (§11). Capacities are weights read as upper bounds on how much can pass, and flow is a different problem from shortest paths even though it is solved with the same traversals (§21). Undirected graphs are the directed graph with both arcs; a negative undirected edge is a negative cycle of length two, which is why the shortest-path sections are stated for directed graphs and the spanning-tree sections for undirected ones. Nothing here requires weights to be distinct, integer, or bounded, except where the text says so, and each such assumption is named where it is used: the Fibonacci-heap bound is amortised (§06), Ford-Fulkerson is guaranteed to terminate only for integer or rational capacities (§22) and terminates on any capacities once a shortest-path rule is imposed (§23), Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) is for shortest augmenting paths only (§23), and union-find's inverse-Ackermann bound needs both of its heuristics (§18).

The catalogue, and how this page walks it

  • Single source — relaxation and the six optimality conditions every proof rests on (§02); what negative edges and negative cycles do to the definition of δ (§03); Dijkstra's algorithm stepped (§04), its proof and exactly where w ≥ 0 enters (§05), and its cost under an array, a binary heap, a Fibonacci heap and lazy deletion, measured (§06); Bellman-Ford stepped with its early exit and the negative cycle found and reported (§07), and its proof through the path-relaxation property and the DP view (§08); shortest and longest paths in a DAG in one pass, with critical paths (§09); systems of difference constraints solved as a shortest-path problem (§10); 0-1 BFS and Dial's buckets when the weights are small integers (§11).
  • All pairs — V runs of Dijkstra, and repeated matrix squaring (§12); Floyd-Warshall stepped, with path reconstruction and the transitive closure (§13); Johnson's reweighting for sparse graphs (§14).
  • A heuristic — A*: admissible and consistent heuristics, optimality, and what it saves (§15).
  • Minimum spanning trees — the cut property and the cycle property (§16); Kruskal's algorithm stepped (§17); union-find built, with union by rank, path compression and the inverse-Ackermann bound (§18); Prim's algorithm stepped — Dijkstra's skeleton with a different key (§19); Borůvka, bottleneck and second-best trees, the MST against the shortest-path tree, and MST clustering (§20).
  • Flow — flow networks, residual graphs, augmenting paths and cuts (§21); Ford-Fulkerson and the max-flow min-cut theorem (§22); Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) bound proved and measured (§23); push-relabel with preflows, heights and the O(V²E) generic bound (§24); maximum bipartite matching as a flow, with König and Hall (§25); what flow solves — disjoint paths, project selection, image segmentation, circulations (§26).
  • Beyond — weighted-graph algorithms in machine learning (§27); choosing the algorithm, as a decision procedure (§28); pitfalls (§29); the cheat sheet, with the load-time audit that re-checks every family of routine against brute force (§30).
Where the neighbouring material lives. Graph representations, and what a weight costs in each — one more field per adjacency entry, one more parallel array in CSR — are Graph Representations & Traversal, §05–§08; BFS and the proof that its arrival order is the distance order for unit weights, which is the fact Dijkstra generalises and the fact Edmonds-Karp relies on, are §12; topological order, the evaluation order of §09, is §20–§21; and the union-find hand-off that §18 fulfils is §27. The priority queue Dijkstra and Prim are built on — sift-up, the index map that makes decrease-key possible, the Fibonacci heap and why its bound rarely wins — is Heaps & Priority Queues, §16 and §23. Amortised analysis, the language in which the Fibonacci-heap and union-find bounds are stated, is Algorithm Analysis, §18. The sort Kruskal begins with is Sorting & Searching. Optimal substructure — the cut-and-paste argument that a subpath of a shortest path is a shortest path, and the case where it fails for longest simple paths — is Dynamic Programming, §02, and is the reason §03 can say that "shortest simple path" with negative cycles is a different, and hard, problem.

02 Relaxation and the shortest-path optimality conditions

Relaxing an edge (u, v) asks whether the best known way to reach u, extended by that edge, beats the best known way to reach v — and if it does, lowers v's estimate to match and records u as its predecessor. That test is the whole of the single-source machinery: every algorithm from §04 to §15 initialises one estimate per vertex and then performs relaxations in some order, and nothing else ever changes an estimate. The reason this is enough is a short list of properties that hold for any sequence of relaxations whatsoever: estimates never drop below the truth, an estimate that reaches the truth stays there, and relaxing the edges of a shortest path in order — with anything at all happening in between — drives the estimate at its end to the true distance. Each correctness proof on this page is one of those properties applied to one schedule.

initialisation and the primitiveINITIALISE(G, s): for every v ∈ V: d[v] ← ∞, π[v] ← NIL; d[s] ← 0 RELAX(u, v, w): if d[v] > d[u] + w(u, v): d[v] ← d[u] + w(u, v); π[v] ← u (with ∞ + w = ∞, so an unreached u never relaxes anything) d[v] is the SHORTEST-PATH ESTIMATE of v: the weight of the best s ⇝ v path found so far (the π-path), always an upper bound on δ(s, v) the PREDECESSOR SUBGRAPH G_π = (V_π, E_π): V_π = { v : π[v] ≠ NIL } ∪ {s}, E_π = { (π[v], v) } — the tree the answer is read from the six properties — for any sequence of relaxations after INITIALISE, in a graph with no negative cycle reachable from sTRIANGLE INEQUALITY for every edge (u, v): δ(s, v) ≤ δ(s, u) + w(u, v) UPPER-BOUND PROPERTY d[v] ≥ δ(s, v) at all times; and once d[v] = δ(s, v) it never changes again NO-PATH PROPERTY if no s ⇝ v path exists, d[v] = δ(s, v) = ∞ at all times CONVERGENCE PROPERTY if s ⇝ u → v is a shortest path and d[u] = δ(s, u) at any time BEFORE (u, v) is relaxed, then d[v] = δ(s, v) at all times AFTER PATH-RELAXATION PROPERTY if p = ⟨v₀ = s, v₁, …, vₖ⟩ is a shortest path and its edges are relaxed in the order (v₀, v₁), (v₁, v₂), …, (vₖ₋₁, vₖ) — with ANY other relaxations interleaved, before, between or after — then d[vₖ] = δ(s, vₖ) afterwards PREDECESSOR-SUBGRAPH once d[v] = δ(s, v) for every v, G_π is a shortest-paths tree rooted at s (and G_π is a tree at ALL times, negative cycles aside)

The proofs, each a few lines

Triangle inequality. If v is unreachable the right side is what it is and the left is ∞ only if u is unreachable too, so take both reachable. A shortest s ⇝ u path followed by the edge (u, v) is a walk from s to v of weight δ(s, u) + w(u, v); with no negative cycle reachable, any walk contains a simple path of no greater weight (cut out each cycle — its weight is ≥ 0), and δ(s, v) is the minimum over paths, so δ(s, v) ≤ δ(s, u) + w(u, v). Upper bound. Induction over the sequence of relaxations. After initialisation d[s] = 0 = δ(s, s) (no negative cycle reachable means no negative cycle through s, so the empty path is shortest) and every other d[v] = ∞ ≥ δ(s, v). A relaxation of (u, v) that changes anything sets d[v] = d[u] + w(u, v) ≥ δ(s, u) + w(u, v) ≥ δ(s, v), the first step by the induction hypothesis on u and the second by the triangle inequality. The second half follows: relaxation only ever lowers an estimate, and it cannot go below δ, so an estimate that equals δ is stuck. No path. δ(s, v) = ∞, and d[v] ≥ δ(s, v) by the upper-bound property. Convergence. Suppose d[u] = δ(s, u) before (u, v) is relaxed. It still equals δ(s, u) at the moment of relaxation, by the upper-bound property. Afterwards d[v] ≤ d[u] + w(u, v) = δ(s, u) + w(u, v) = δ(s, v) — the last equality because the s ⇝ u part of a shortest s ⇝ u → v path is itself a shortest path (Dynamic Programming, §02: splice a shorter one in and the whole gets shorter). Combined with d[v] ≥ δ(s, v) that is equality, and it persists. Path relaxation. Induction on i: after the i-th listed relaxation, d[vᵢ] = δ(s, vᵢ). For i = 0 this is d[s] = 0 from initialisation, never changed thereafter. For the step, every prefix of a shortest path is a shortest path, so s ⇝ vᵢ₋₁ → vᵢ is one; d[vᵢ₋₁] = δ(s, vᵢ₋₁) holds before (vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) is relaxed by the hypothesis, and the convergence property gives d[vᵢ] = δ(s, vᵢ) afterwards, whatever other relaxations intervened. Predecessor subgraph. Two facts. First, G_π never contains a cycle: if relaxing (vₖ₋₁, vₖ) closed a cycle ⟨v₀, …, vₖ = v₀⟩ of π-pointers, then just before it, for each earlier edge on the cycle, d[vᵢ] ≥ d[vᵢ₋₁] + w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) — equality held when π[vᵢ] was set and d[vᵢ₋₁] can only have dropped since — while for the closing edge d[vₖ] > d[vₖ₋₁] + w strictly; summing around the cycle, the d terms cancel and leave 0 > w(cycle), a negative cycle reachable from s, which was excluded. So G_π is a forest in which every vertex has at most one parent, connected through s — a tree. Second, once every d[v] = δ(s, v), take the π-path ⟨s = v₀, …, vₖ = v⟩; the same inequality d[vᵢ] ≥ d[vᵢ₋₁] + w now reads w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) ≤ δ(s, vᵢ) − δ(s, vᵢ₋₁), and summing telescopes to w(path) ≤ δ(s, v) − 0. A path cannot weigh less than δ, so it weighs exactly δ: the π-path is a shortest path.

What the properties buy: correctness is a statement about order

Put the path-relaxation property next to the upper-bound property and the shape of every proof to come is visible. To show that an algorithm computes δ(s, v) it suffices to show that its schedule relaxes the edges of some shortest s ⇝ v path in path order; the estimates it happens to produce in between are irrelevant, because none of them can overshoot below the truth and the listed relaxations force the truth regardless. Bellman-Ford's proof (§08) is exactly this: a shortest path has at most V − 1 edges, and a schedule that relaxes every edge in each of V − 1 rounds relaxes the path's i-th edge no later than round i. The DAG algorithm's proof (§09) is the same with "round" replaced by "topological position". Dijkstra's proof (§05) is subtler because its schedule relaxes each edge once, so the order must be right the first time — which is what non-negative weights guarantee and what the figure of §05 breaks. The properties also say what a wrong answer must look like: since d[v] ≥ δ(s, v) always, an algorithm can only fail by leaving an estimate too high, never too low — it can miss a shortcut but never invent one. That is why an independent check against brute force is a check that every d[v] came down far enough.

Worked in full: five vertices, eight edges, four schedules

The figure's graph has vertices s, a, b, c, t and directed edges s→a (6), s→b (2), b→a (3), a→c (1), b→c (7), c→t (2), a→t (5), b→t (9). Enumerating every simple path from s (there are 12, 13 with the empty path at s) gives the true distances δ = ⟨s 0, a 5, b 2, c 6, t 8⟩, and the shortest path to every vertex lies on one chain: s→b→a→c→t, so the shortest-paths tree is that single path and every δ is determined by relaxing its four edges in order. The table follows the schedule that relaxes the chain's edges in reverse: c→t, a→c, b→a, s→b, then the other four in edge-list order, pass after pass until a pass changes nothing.

passrelaxations that improved something (in order)d[s a b c t] after the passvertices at δ
1s→b (∞→2), s→a (∞→6), b→c (∞→9), a→t (∞→11)0 6 2 9 112 (s, b)
2a→c (9→7), b→a (6→5), a→t (11→10)0 5 2 7 103 (+a)
3c→t (10→9), a→c (7→6)0 5 2 6 94 (+c)
4c→t (9→8)0 5 2 6 85 (+t)
5(none — the schedule stops)0 5 2 6 85

Each pass fixes exactly one more vertex along the chain, from the source outward, because within a pass the chain's edges are met in the wrong order: b→a is relaxed before s→b has given b its value, so a is only fixed in the pass after b, and so on — four passes for a four-edge path, plus a fifth to observe that nothing moves. That is the path-relaxation property being satisfied one edge per pass, and it is the worst case Bellman-Ford's V − 1 is sized for. The same eight edges relaxed with the chain's edges first and in order fix all five vertices in the first pass: s→b gives b = 2, then b→a gives a = 5, a→c gives c = 6, c→t gives t = 8, and the remaining four edges improve nothing. Measured by the routine under a counter: the reverse schedule performs 40 relaxations (five passes of eight) of which 10 improve an estimate, and the chain-first schedule 16 relaxations (two passes) of which 4 improve; the edge-list order as written happens to meet the chain in order too and finishes in one pass plus a confirming pass — 16 relaxations, 5 improvements (s→a sets a = 6 before b→a lowers it to 5); a seeded random order takes three passes, 24 relaxations and 8 improvements. All four end at the same d[], which the brute-force enumeration confirms — those are the numbers the tracing script produced, and the figure re-derives every one at load.

Relaxation, one edge at a time: the same eight edges in four schedules, until a full pass changes nothing amber = the edge being relaxed; green edge = it lowered d[v]; each vertex shows its estimate, green once it equals δ (from brute force); the readout counts relaxations and improvements and reports how many vertices are at δ after each pass

Vocabulary that the rest of the page uses without comment

An estimate d[v] is tight when d[v] = δ(s, v). An edge (u, v) is tense when d[v] > d[u] + w(u, v) — relaxing it would improve something — and a set of estimates is a fixed point of relaxation when no edge is tense; §08 and §10 use the fact that the estimates are all tight if and only if d[s] = 0 and no edge is tense (one direction is the triangle inequality applied to δ; the other is the telescoping argument above run along a shortest path). A vertex is settled or finalised when the algorithm promises never to lower it again — Dijkstra's S — and tentative otherwise. An algorithm that settles one vertex per step and never revisits it is label-setting — Dijkstra and its relatives A* and Dial's buckets, which settle in key order and need w ≥ 0 for the promise to hold (§05), and the DAG pass, which settles in topological order and needs no condition on the weights (§09); an algorithm that allows any estimate to fall again until no edge is tense is label-correcting — Bellman-Ford, its queue-driven variant SPFA, and Dijkstra with re-insertion of improved vertices (§05) — and its guarantee is a fixed point rather than a settled set, which is why it tolerates negative edges on cyclic graphs and pays for it in rounds. The shortest-paths tree is a rooted tree containing every reachable vertex in which the unique root-to-v path is a shortest path; when several shortest paths exist there are several such trees, the algorithms return one of them, and which one depends on the schedule and on tie-breaking — the figure's four schedules all produce the same π here only because this graph's shortest paths are unique.

03 Negative edges and negative cycles: what δ means then

A negative edge is an edge whose weight is below zero; a negative cycle is a cycle whose weights sum to less than zero; and the shortest-path weight δ(s, v) is well defined — ∞ if v is unreachable, otherwise a finite minimum attained by a simple path — exactly when no negative cycle is reachable from s on the way to v. Negative edges by themselves change nothing in the definition and only some things in the algorithms: Bellman-Ford (§07), the DAG pass (§09) and Johnson's reweighting (§14) handle them as written, and Dijkstra (§04) does not. A negative cycle changes the definition: walks around it get arbitrarily cheap, the minimum over walks does not exist, and the honest value of δ is −∞ for every vertex the cycle can reach. This section fixes what each case means, why restricting to simple paths does not rescue the negative-cycle case, why Dijkstra's hypothesis is the sign of the edges and not the absence of cycles, and why the obvious repair — shift every weight up until none is negative — is wrong, while a less obvious one is right.

the casesno negative cycle reachable from s: δ(s, v) = min over PATHS = min over WALKS, attained by a simple path with ≤ V − 1 edges — any walk contains a simple path of no greater weight: cut out each cycle, whose weight is ≥ 0 a negative cycle C reachable from s, and v reachable from C: inf over walks = −∞ (go round C once more: −|w(C)| cheaper each time) — define δ(s, v) = −∞; vertices NOT reachable from any such C keep a finite δ, which Bellman-Ford still computes correctly (§07) an undirected edge of negative weight = a directed 2-cycle u → v → u of weight 2·w(u, v) < 0: every undirected negative edge is a negative cycle the add-a-constant fallacy — three vertices s, a, tedges s→t (1), s→a (2), a→t (−2). δ(s, t) = 0 by s→a→t (the direct edge costs 1) add c = 2 to every edge: s→t (3), s→a (4), a→t (0). now s→a→t = 4 and s→t = 3: Dijkstra on the shifted graph returns s→t, original weight 1 ≠ 0 why: a path with k edges gains k·c, so the shift favours paths with FEWER edges — it changes which path is shortest whenever the true shortest path has more edges than a rival (the same happens for c = 3 or c = 10: the shifted answer is always s→t — measured by running the routine on the shifted instances) the reweighting that does work — potentials (Johnson, §14)choose any function h : V → ℝ and set w′(u, v) = w(u, v) + h(u) − h(v) for ANY s ⇝ v path p: w′(p) = w(p) + h(s) − h(v) — the correction depends on the endpoints only (the h terms telescope), so every s ⇝ v path shifts by the SAME amount hence p is shortest under w′ ⇔ p is shortest under w; and a cycle keeps its weight exactly (h(s) − h(s) = 0), so negative cycles are neither created nor destroyed if h satisfies h(v) ≤ h(u) + w(u, v) for every edge — for instance h = δ from a new source joined to every vertex by a 0-edge — then every w′ ≥ 0 and Dijkstra applies on the three-vertex graph: h = ⟨0, 0, −2⟩ gives w′ = s→t 3, s→a 2, a→t 0; Dijkstra returns d′ = ⟨0, 2, 2⟩ and d′(v) − h(s) + h(v) = ⟨0, 2, 0⟩ = δ

Why "shortest simple path" does not rescue the negative-cycle case

Faced with a negative cycle, the tempting redefinition is "the shortest path that visits no vertex twice". That is a well-posed problem — the minimum over finitely many simple paths exists — but it is not one any algorithm on this page solves, and there is strong evidence that no efficient algorithm solves it: the shortest simple path problem with arbitrary weights is NP-hard. The reduction is short. Given any directed graph, give every edge weight −1; a simple path from s to t of weight −(V − 1) uses V − 1 edges, hence visits every vertex — it is a Hamiltonian path — and conversely; so an algorithm for shortest simple paths would decide Hamiltonian path, which is NP-complete. Equivalently, shortest simple path with negated weights is longest simple path, and Dynamic Programming, §02 shows on four vertices exactly why the latter lacks optimal substructure: subpaths compete for vertices. The evidential status should be stated as it is: NP-hardness means a polynomial algorithm would give one for every problem in NP, which is widely believed impossible but not proved. What is proved is the equivalence, and it is enough to explain why the algorithms here compute δ over walks and report −∞ rather than searching for the cheapest cycle-free route. Every efficient shortest-path algorithm rests on optimal substructure, and optimal substructure for paths holds only when cutting a cycle out of a walk never makes it more expensive — which is precisely "no negative cycles".

Why Dijkstra's hypothesis is w ≥ 0, not "no negative cycle"

The two conditions are different in both directions of strength. "No negative cycle reachable" is what makes δ well defined and is all Bellman-Ford needs (§07). "Every edge non-negative" is stronger — it implies the first, since a cycle of non-negative edges is non-negative — and it is what Dijkstra needs, because Dijkstra's argument is not about the definition of δ but about its schedule: it finalises the closest tentative vertex on the grounds that no path leaving through a farther vertex can come back cheaper, and "cannot come back cheaper" is the statement that extending a path never lowers its weight, which is w ≥ 0 edge by edge. The graph of §05's figure has a single negative edge, no cycle at all (it is a DAG), a perfectly well-defined δ — and Dijkstra gets it wrong, because the negative edge lets a longer-looking route end up shorter after a vertex has already been finalised. So the hypotheses line up as: DAG (any weights, §09) and no-negative-cycle (any weights, Bellman-Ford) concern the definition; w ≥ 0 concerns Dijkstra's greedy step, and the Greedy Algorithms page's §20 names it as the greedy-choice lemma's hypothesis. A special case is worth knowing because it is a common exercise and a common misconception: if the only negative edges leave s, and no cycle through s is negative (so that δ is finite), Dijkstra is still correct — every simple path from s uses at most one such edge, as its first, and thereafter only grows, so the finalisation argument of §05 survives — but a negative edge anywhere else can break it, and there is no cheap patch for "just one negative edge" in general (§05 says what the patches cost).

Where negative weights come from, and what to do about each

Negative weights are not exotic. Log-probabilities: multiplying probabilities along a path becomes adding −log p ≥ 0, so the most probable path is a shortest path with non-negative weights and Dijkstra is fine; the Viterbi decoding of the DP page is this on a DAG. Gains and losses: an edge that pays out (a currency conversion at a favourable rate, a job that earns rather than costs) is negative, and a negative cycle is an arbitrage — a sequence of conversions that ends with more than it started with; Bellman-Ford's negative-cycle test (§07) is the arbitrage detector, run on weights −log(rate). Constraints: a system of inequalities xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ bₖ is a shortest-path problem whose edge weights are the bₖ, of either sign, and whose infeasibility is a negative cycle (§10). Reweighting: when a negative-weight instance has no negative cycle and must be solved many times or for all pairs, one Bellman-Ford computes potentials and thereafter every query is a Dijkstra (§14). The decision procedure of §28 begins with the sign of the weights for this reason, and the pitfalls of §29 include the constant-shift fallacy above because it is attempted every year: it is not that the shift makes the weights positive — it does — but that it adds a per-edge toll that penalises long paths, and nothing about shortest paths is invariant under a per-edge toll. Potentials add a per-endpoint toll instead, which every s ⇝ v path pays equally, and that is the entire difference between the fallacy and the theorem.

The summary the later sections assume. Weights all non-negative: every algorithm on the page applies; Dijkstra is the default. Some negative edges, no negative cycle reachable: δ is well defined, Bellman-Ford or Johnson, or one pass if the graph is a DAG; Dijkstra is wrong and no constant shift repairs it. A negative cycle reachable: δ = −∞ on everything the cycle reaches, Bellman-Ford reports the cycle (§07), and "shortest simple path" is a different problem with no known efficient algorithm. Undirected graphs with a negative edge are in the last case automatically.

04 Dijkstra's algorithm, stepped

Dijkstra's algorithm computes single-source shortest paths in a graph whose edge weights are all non-negative by maintaining a set S of finalised vertices and, repeatedly, moving into S the vertex outside it with the smallest estimate and relaxing that vertex's outgoing edges. The estimate of a vertex at the moment it enters S is its true distance — that is the invariant, and §05 proves it — so each vertex is finalised once, each edge is examined once — when its tail is finalised — and the work is V extract-mins plus E relaxations, each relaxation possibly lowering a key in whatever priority queue holds the tentative vertices. It is breadth-first search with the FIFO queue replaced by a min-priority queue keyed on d, and with "assign the distance on discovery" replaced by "relax, and allow the distance to be lowered again until finalisation".

the algorithmDIJKSTRA(G, w, s): INITIALISE(G, s) d[s] = 0, every other d = ∞, π = NIL S ← ∅; Q ← every vertex, keyed by d (or: Q ← {s}, and insert on first discovery — same schedule) while Q ≠ ∅: u ← EXTRACT-MIN(Q); S ← S ∪ {u} u is FINALISED: d[u] = δ(s, u) from now on (§05) for each edge (u, v) with v ∉ S: RELAX(u, v, w); if d[v] fell, DECREASE-KEY(Q, v, d[v]) return d, π the invariant, and the schedule it inducesINVARIANT at the start of every iteration: for every v ∈ S, d[v] = δ(s, v); for every v ∉ S, d[v] = the weight of the cheapest s ⇝ v path whose vertices other than v all lie in S SCHEDULE vertices enter S in non-decreasing order of δ(s, ·) — so the edges of every shortest path are relaxed in path order, once each (path-relaxation property, §02) TIE-BREAK two tentative vertices with equal keys may be extracted in either order; the answer is the same, the π-tree may differ. This page's routine breaks ties toward the smaller vertex id. COST V × EXTRACT-MIN + (number of successful relaxations) × DECREASE-KEY + E × O(1) — §06 gives the bounds for each queue with every vertex inserted at the start, every successful relaxation is a decrease-key; with insert-on-discovery, the first success per vertex is an insert instead

Worked in full: six vertices, ten edges

The figure's instance has source s and vertices 1 … 5, with edges s→1 (7), s→2 (3), s→3 (12), 2→1 (3), 2→3 (8), 1→3 (2), 1→4 (5), 3→4 (1), 3→5 (1), 4→5 (2) — chosen so that one vertex's estimate is lowered twice after its first discovery and so that a tie occurs at an extraction. Brute force over the 23 simple paths from s (24 counting the empty path) gives δ = ⟨0, 6, 3, 8, 9, 9⟩. The table shows d[] and π[] after each extraction, with the keys of the tentative vertices — the priority queue's contents — in the last column.

extracted (key)relaxations, in adjacency orderd[s 1 2 3 4 5]π[1 2 3 4 5]queue after (v : key)
(start)—0 ∞ ∞ ∞ ∞ ∞– – – – –s : 0, all others ∞
s (0)s→1: ∞→7 · s→2: ∞→3 · s→3: ∞→120 7 3 12 ∞ ∞s s s – –2 : 3, 1 : 7, 3 : 12
2 (3)2→1: 3 + 3 = 6 < 7 → 6 · 2→3: 3 + 8 = 11 < 12 → 110 6 3 11 ∞ ∞2 s 2 – –1 : 6, 3 : 11
1 (6)1→3: 6 + 2 = 8 < 11 → 8 · 1→4: ∞→110 6 3 8 11 ∞2 s 1 1 –3 : 8, 4 : 11
3 (8)3→4: 8 + 1 = 9 < 11 → 9 · 3→5: ∞→90 6 3 8 9 92 s 1 3 34 : 9, 5 : 9 — a tie
4 (9, tie → smaller id)4→5: 9 + 2 = 11 ≥ 9 → no change0 6 3 8 9 92 s 1 3 35 : 9
5 (9)(no out-edges)0 6 3 8 9 92 s 1 3 3∅

Vertex 3 is the one lowered twice: discovered at 12 by the direct edge, improved to 11 through 2, improved again to 8 through 1 — and only then extracted, so its finalised value is right even though its first two estimates were not. That is the design of the algorithm: an estimate may be wrong for as long as its vertex is tentative, and the guarantee is only that it is right at extraction. Vertex 1 is lowered once (7 → 6) and vertex 4 once (11 → 9). The tie at the fourth extraction is between 4 and 5, both at key 9; this page's routine takes the smaller id, so 4 goes first and its edge 4→5 is examined and fails to improve 5; taking 5 first instead would have produced the same distances and the same tree, since 4→5 improves nothing either way — in general the tree can differ under a tie, the distances cannot. The shortest-paths tree read off π is s→2, 2→1, 1→3, 3→4, 3→5: a chain s→2→1→3 with two leaves hanging off 3. Note what it is not: the direct edges s→1 and s→3 are in the graph and in no shortest path, and the edge 4→5, examined last, is the one relaxation that changes nothing.

What the counter reports

Run under a counter, the routine performs 6 extractions — one per vertex, V — and examines 10 edges, the sum of the out-degrees, which equals E here because every vertex is reachable and every edge is examined exactly once, from its tail's extraction. Of those ten relaxations 9 succeed: five are the first discovery of a vertex (∞ to a finite key) and four are genuine lowerings (7→6, 12→11, 11→8, 11→9); with every vertex sitting in the queue from the start, all nine are decrease-keys, and with insert-on-discovery they would be five inserts and four decrease-keys. The heap performed 19 sift steps in total — one per level examined while an entry sifted up after a decrease-key or down after an extraction — (the initial insertions included) against a heap of height ⌊log₂ 6⌋ = 2 — at most two moves per operation, three levels examined. The tie was detected once. All of these numbers are what the tracing script reported and what the figure's readout re-derives at load; the readout also confirms d[] against the brute-force enumeration for every vertex, and checks that the π-path to each vertex re-sums to its d.

Dijkstra, one extraction per step: S fills, the frontier's keys are shown, the relaxed edges light, and the shortest-paths tree remains blue vertices = S (finalised), amber = the vertex just extracted, keys are shown beside tentative vertices; green edge = a relaxation that lowered a key, dashed amber = one that did not, blue edges = the current π-tree; the table at the right is d[] / π[]

Reading the algorithm as BFS with a different frontier

Three differences from BFS, and only three. The queue is ordered by tentative distance rather than by arrival, so the vertex that leaves it next is the closest one known rather than the oldest; the discovery step is a relaxation, which may fire more than once per vertex (vertex 3 above fired three times) rather than an assignment that fires once; and a vertex is finished when it is extracted, not when it is discovered. With all weights equal, tentative distances are the BFS layer number times the weight, no relaxation ever lowers an estimate after discovery, and the three differences collapse: Dijkstra on unit weights is BFS, at the cost of a log V per queue operation it does not need — which is why Graph Representations & Traversal, §28 says to check for equal weights before reaching for a heap, and why §11 below recovers BFS's linear time for weights in {0, 1} and for small integers. Prim's algorithm (§19) is the same loop once more, keyed by the weight of the single edge into the tree instead of the total path length, and §20 shows on one graph that the two keys grow different trees.

05 Why Dijkstra is right, and exactly where it needs w ≥ 0

Dijkstra's algorithm is correct because the vertex it extracts always already has its true distance: at the moment u leaves the queue, d[u] = δ(s, u). The proof is by contradiction on the first extraction for which that fails, and it uses non-negative weights in exactly one inequality — the claim that a vertex on the way to u along a true shortest path cannot be farther from s than u is. Everything else in the proof is the relaxation properties of §02. This section gives the proof with that step marked, then breaks it: a three-vertex graph with one negative edge on which Dijkstra finalises a vertex too early, the four-vertex extension on which the error propagates whichever implementation variant is used, and a slider that finds the exact weight at which the answer first goes wrong.

theoremG directed, w(u, v) ≥ 0 for every edge, source s. When DIJKSTRA terminates, d[v] = δ(s, v) for every v ∈ V, and G_π is a shortest-paths tree. proof — the invariant "every vertex enters S with d = δ", by contradiction on the first failuresuppose u is the FIRST vertex extracted with d[u] ≠ δ(s, u). u ≠ s (s is extracted first with d[s] = 0 = δ(s, s), as no cycle is negative). so S ≠ ∅ just before u is extracted. u is reachable, else d[u] = ∞ = δ(s, u) (no-path property); so a shortest path p : s ⇝ u exists. walk along p from s: s ∈ S, u ∉ S, so p leaves S somewhere. let y be the FIRST vertex of p not in S, and x ∈ S its predecessor on p: p = s ⇝ x → y ⇝ u (x may be s; y may be u) (1) d[y] = δ(s, y) — x entered S with d[x] = δ(s, x) (u is the FIRST failure), and (x, y) was relaxed when x was extracted; s ⇝ x → y is a prefix of a shortest path, hence a shortest path; CONVERGENCE PROPERTY gives d[y] = δ(s, y) (2) δ(s, y) ≤ δ(s, u) — y precedes u on the shortest path p, and δ(s, u) = δ(s, y) + w(y ⇝ u part of p); the y ⇝ u part has weight ≥ 0 ◄── THIS is the only place non-negative weights are used (3) δ(s, u) ≤ d[u] — upper-bound property (4) d[u] ≤ d[y] — u was extracted rather than y, and both were in the queue: u has the minimum key chain them: d[y] = δ(s, y) ≤ δ(s, u) ≤ d[u] ≤ d[y], so every ≤ is an equality and d[u] = δ(s, u) — contradicting the choice of u. ∎ after termination every vertex has been extracted, so every d = δ; the PREDECESSOR-SUBGRAPH property makes G_π a shortest-paths tree. what a negative edge does to step (2)with w(y ⇝ u) < 0 possible, δ(s, u) can be LESS than δ(s, y): the path may leave S through a farther y and still arrive cheaper. then u is extracted with d[u] > δ(s, u), and — since u's edges are relaxed once, now — the too-high value propagates to everything reached through u.

The counterexample, worked

Three vertices s, a, b and edges s→a (2), s→b (1), a→b (−3). The true distances: δ(s, a) = 2 and δ(s, b) = min(1, 2 − 3) = −1, by the path s→a→b. Dijkstra: extract s and relax, giving d[a] = 2, d[b] = 1; extract b (key 1, the smaller) and finalise it — this is the wrong step, since d[b] = 1 ≠ δ(s, b) = −1; it is the first failure, y = a is the first vertex of the true shortest path outside S, and step (2) fails because δ(s, a) = 2 > δ(s, b) = −1: the shortest path to b leaves S through a vertex farther than b. Then extract a (key 2) and meet the edge a→b, which now points into S. Two implementations diverge here, and both are wrong. The variant this page's routine uses — skip edges into S, which is what a heap-based implementation must do since b is no longer in the queue — returns d[b] = 1. The relax-anyway variant, which relaxes every out-edge regardless of whether its head is finalised, updates d[b] to −1, which is the right number by accident: b has already been extracted, so nothing downstream of b is ever recomputed. Add a fourth vertex c with b→c (1) to see the accident fail: δ(s, c) = 0, and both variants report d[c] = 2, because b→c was relaxed while d[b] was still 1 and is never relaxed again. Measured on the four-vertex graph with the negative edge at −3: the skipping variant is wrong on b and c, the relax-anyway variant is wrong on c; neither is fixable without re-examining a finalised vertex.

The figure's slider moves the weight of a→b from +2 down to −5. For weights 2, 1, 0, −1 Dijkstra agrees with brute force at every vertex; the first weight at which it disagrees is −2, and at −1 it still agrees only because 2 + (−1) = 1 ties the direct edge rather than beating it — the same "ties are harmless, strict improvements after finalisation are not" boundary as in the proof, where step (2) needs ≤ and a weight of exactly zero on the y ⇝ u stretch preserves it. So the hypothesis of the theorem can be weakened only to "no path can get strictly shorter by continuing past a finalised vertex", which for general graphs is the same as w ≥ 0.

The counterexample: Dijkstra finalises b too early; slide the negative edge's weight to find where the answer first goes wrong stepped per extraction as in §04; a vertex whose finalised d differs from brute force is outlined red, with δ shown beside it; the readout sweeps the weight from +2 down to −5 and reports the first value at which Dijkstra and brute force disagree, for both implementation variants

Zero-weight edges, and edges into S

Zero weights are harmless. The proof needs the y ⇝ u stretch to weigh at least zero, and it does; the only visible effect is ties — several tentative vertices at the same key — and the theorem holds for every tie-break, since the argument never says which of two equal-key vertices is extracted. A zero-weight cycle is likewise fine: δ remains finite, and the algorithm never traverses the cycle because every vertex on it is extracted once. What is not harmless is a negative edge into S, in any implementation that never re-extracts. The finalised vertex is wrong forever in the skipping variant, wrong for its successors in the relax-anyway variant, and the damage is invisible to the algorithm: nothing in Dijkstra's state records that a finalised vertex was improved, so no error is raised. A defensive implementation can detect the situation cheaply — if a relaxation would lower the key of a vertex in S, a negative edge has been found — and either abort with a diagnosis or fall back to Bellman-Ford; that check costs one comparison per edge and is worth having in a library routine that cannot trust its input.

What can be salvaged with a few negative edges

Dijkstra cannot be patched for negative edges in general, but three special cases are genuinely solvable with it, and all three have the same shape: partition every shortest path into non-negative stretches and use Dijkstra on the stretches. Negative edges only out of s, and no negative cycle: unchanged Dijkstra is correct, because a simple path from s uses one such edge, its first, and the y ⇝ u stretch in step (2) never contains it (§03). One negative edge (p, q), no negative cycle: a shortest path uses it at most once, so δ(s, t) = min( δ₀(s, t), δ₀(s, p) + w(p, q) + δ₀(q, t) ) where δ₀ is computed in the graph without that edge — two Dijkstra runs, from s and from q; on the figure's graph with w(a→b) = −3: δ₀ from s without that edge is ⟨0, 2, 1, 2⟩, δ₀ from b is ⟨∞, ∞, 0, 1⟩, and min(δ₀(s, ·), δ₀(s, a) − 3 + δ₀(b, ·)) = min(⟨0, 2, 1, 2⟩, ⟨∞, ∞, −1, 0⟩) = ⟨0, 2, −1, 0⟩, matching brute force; the figure's readout re-derives this patch at every slider weight and flags its agreement. k negative edges, no negative cycle: alternate k + 1 times between a Dijkstra pass over the non-negative edges (started from the current estimates, all tentative vertices in the queue — the multi-source form, whose correctness argument is the theorem above with "d[s] = 0" replaced by "the initial estimates are upper bounds") and a single relaxation of each negative edge; after phase i every vertex whose shortest path uses at most i negative edges is correct, by the path-relaxation property, so k + 1 phases cost O(k · (V + E) log V) — cheaper than Bellman-Ford's O(VE) when k is small, and requiring that the negative edges be known. The general alternative — allow a finalised vertex to be re-inserted when it improves, turning Dijkstra into a label-correcting algorithm — is correct when no negative cycle exists but loses the bound: on adversarial instances the number of re-extractions is known to grow exponentially in V, so it is not a safe default. When the negative edges are many or unknown, the answer is Bellman-Ford (§07), a DAG pass if the graph is acyclic (§09), or Johnson's potentials if many queries follow (§14).

06 Dijkstra's cost: array, binary heap, Fibonacci heap, lazy deletion — measured

Dijkstra's running time is V extract-mins plus at most E decrease-keys plus Θ(V + E) bookkeeping, and every published bound for it is that formula with one priority queue's costs substituted in: Θ(V²) for an unsorted array, O((V + E) log V) for a binary heap with decrease-key, O(E log E) plus Θ(V) initialisation — equal to O((V + E) log E), and the same thing as the index-map bound since log E ≤ 2 log V — for a binary heap with lazy deletion, and O(E + V log V) amortised for a Fibonacci heap. There is no single "Dijkstra bound"; there is a bound per queue, and the right queue depends on the density E/V. This section derives each, says which wins where and why, and then measures the three that are implemented on this page across V at two densities — which turns up a fact the bounds hide: the number of decrease-keys that actually happen is far smaller than E.

the cost formula and the four queuesT = V · EXTRACT-MIN + D · DECREASE-KEY + Θ(V + E) D = number of successful relaxations ≤ E (each edge relaxed once, from its tail) unsorted ARRAY of keys extract = a scan of the V entries, Θ(V); decrease = one write, Θ(1) T = Θ(V² + E) = Θ(V²) worst case BINARY HEAP + index map extract = O(log V); decrease = sift-up, O(log V) T = O((V + E) log V) worst case BINARY HEAP, LAZY deletion no index map; "decrease" = push a fresh (key, v) pair, O(log E); pops ≤ V + D, stale ones discarded T = O((V + E) log E) = O((V + E) log V) worst case, since E ≤ V², log E ≤ 2 log V FIBONACCI HEAP extract = O(log V) AMORTISED; decrease = O(1) AMORTISED T = O(E + V log V) amortised — the best known for general weights which wins where — on the worst-case accounting, D = Earray beats binary heap ⇔ V² < (V + E) log V ⇔ E > V² / log V − V — DENSE graphs, E = Θ(V²): V² against V² log V, the array wins by a log factor binary heap beats array ⇔ E < V² / log V — SPARSE graphs, E = Θ(V): V log V against V², the heap wins by V / log V Fibonacci vs binary: E + V log V against (V + E) log V — a gap of min(E/V, log V), opening once E ≫ V and saturating at log V for E ≥ V log V lazy vs index-map heap: same asymptotics; the lazy heap holds up to V + D ≤ V + E entries instead of V, and pays log of that

Why the array is right for dense graphs

The array implementation keeps d[] as the queue: extract-min is a linear scan for the smallest tentative entry, V steps, done V times — Θ(V²) — and a decrease-key is a single assignment into d[v], so relaxing all E edges costs Θ(E) and disappears into the V². A heap makes extraction cheap, log V, but makes every decrease-key cost log V too, and when there are Θ(V²) edges that is Θ(V² log V) of decrease-key work — more than the array's whole run. The array is the correct structure whenever E is within a log V factor of V²; it is also the simplest to write, has no pointers, and scans a contiguous block of memory, and is often assumed to be faster in practice on dense graphs; the measurement below shows that assumption depends on how many relaxations actually succeed. The d-ary heap of Heaps & Priority Queues, §17 interpolates: with d = max(2, ⌈E/V⌉) its bound is O(E · log V / log(E/V)), which is the binary heap's O(E log V) for sparse graphs and falls to O(E) — the array's order on a dense graph — once E = V^{1+ε} for a constant ε.

The binary heap with decrease-key, and the one with lazy deletion

A heap that supports decrease-key must be able to find a vertex's entry — an index map pos[v] updated on every swap, which Heaps & Priority Queues, §16 builds — and every successful relaxation becomes pos lookup, key overwrite, sift-up. The queue holds at most V entries, so every operation is O(log V), and the total is O((V + E) log V): V extractions and up to E decrease-keys. The lazy variant drops the index map entirely, which is the form most library-based implementations take because the library's heap has no decrease-key. Instead of lowering an entry's key it pushes a new pair (d[v], v) and leaves the old pair where it is; when a pair is popped, the algorithm checks whether it is stale — whether its key is larger than the current d[v], or v has already been finalised — and if so discards it and pops again. The check is what makes the duplicates harmless: a stale pair can never be the one that finalises v, because the fresher pair with the smaller key was pushed later and will be popped first (same key or smaller), and after v is finalised every remaining pair for it is discarded on sight. The cost is that the heap can hold up to V + D pairs — one per successful relaxation — so each operation is O(log(V + E)) = O(log V), and the pops number V + D rather than V: the bound is O((V + E) log E), the same order as with decrease-key, with a larger heap and no index map. On the six-vertex instance of §04 the lazy routine pushed 9 pairs after the initial one, popped 10, discarded 4 as stale, and held at most 5 pairs at once; the index-map heap did 6 extractions and 9 decrease-keys on a heap that never exceeded 6 entries.

The Fibonacci heap: the bound that is best and the structure that rarely wins

A Fibonacci heap makes decrease-key O(1) amortised by cutting the decreased node out of its tree and adding it to the root list rather than sifting it up, and pays for that during extract-min, which consolidates the root list in O(log V) amortised; Heaps & Priority Queues, §21 gives the potential-function argument, and Algorithm Analysis, §18 the meaning of "amortised": the bound is on the total over the whole run, and an individual extract-min may take Θ(V). Substituted into the formula it gives O(E + V log V), which is asymptotically the best known for Dijkstra with arbitrary real non-negative weights, and which is a genuine improvement over the binary heap's O(E log V) only when E is well above V. In practice it rarely wins the race it wins on paper, for reasons that page's §23 spells out and that the measurement below makes concrete: its constants are large (four pointers, a mark and a degree per node, and a consolidation step on every extraction), its nodes are scattered in memory where a binary heap is one array, and — decisively — the operation it makes cheap is the one that seldom happens. The bound charges E decrease-keys; on the dense random graphs below the measured number is a few percent of E (708 of 16,384 at V = 256), and even on the sparse ones it is well under half (301 of 768).

Measured across V, at two densities

The figure runs the three implemented queues — array scan, index-map binary heap, lazy binary heap — on random directed graphs with weights in 1 … 20 at V = 8, 16, …, 256, in two families: sparse, E ≈ 3V, and dense, E ≈ V²/4. Each runs under its own counter, and the three d[] arrays are checked against each other and against Bellman-Ford at every size. What is plotted is what each queue is charged for: the array's scans (V² exactly, since the routine scans all V entries at each of V extractions), the heap's extractions plus decrease-keys, and the lazy heap's pushes plus pops; the closed forms V², (V + E) log₂ V and E log₂ E are drawn as reference lines. At V = 256 in the sparse family (E = 768) the array performed 65,536 scans, the heap 557 queue operations (256 extractions and 301 decrease-keys), the lazy heap 607; in the dense family (E = 16,384) the array again 65,536, the heap 964 (256 extractions and 708 decrease-keys — 4.3% of E), the lazy heap 1,405, against reference values of 133,120 for (V + E) log₂ V and 229,376 for E log₂ E. The bounds are honoured — every measured count is below its reference line — but by a wide margin on the heap side, because the bound assumes every relaxation succeeds and on random weights almost none do after the first few: a vertex's estimate is set on discovery and improved only when a later-extracted, farther vertex happens to offer a cheaper route, which for random weights is rare.

That is also why the "crossover density" the readout reports comes in three versions, all measured at V = 128 by sweeping E/V² from 0.02 to 1. On the bound's own accounting — V² + E against (V + E)·⌈log₂ V⌉ + E, every relaxation charged a decrease-key — the array wins from the first sampled density above the algebraic threshold, 0.15 (E = 2,458), upward — the threshold itself is E > V²/log₂ V − V ≈ 2,213, density ≈ 0.135, and 0.12 is the last sampled point below it. Charging the heap's measured operations at their worst case ⌈log₂ 128⌉ = 7 levels each, the array never catches up: at full density (E = 16,256) the heap's 494 operations cost at most 3,458 levels plus 8,128 relaxations, against the array's 16,384 scans plus the same relaxations. And counting the sift steps the heap actually took, the heap did 9,760 elementary steps to the array's 24,512 at full density — the array is at a 2.5× disadvantage on the very graphs the worst case says it should win. Only 366 of the 16,256 edges produced a decrease-key. None of this contradicts the bounds, which are worst-case statements over all inputs; it says that the input family on which the array's advantage materialises is one where relaxations succeed constantly, and random weights are not it. Adversarial weightings exist (make every later-discovered route cheaper), and dense graphs with structured weights — a complete graph with Euclidean distances, say — sit between the two. The recommendation stands as the bounds give it, with this caveat attached: a binary heap for anything sparse or of unknown density; the array for dense graphs when it is measured to win on the actual inputs, not assumed to.

Three Dijkstras across V: what each queue is charged for, measured under counters, against the closed-form bounds log-log axes; solid = measured (array scans, heap extractions + decrease-keys, lazy pushes + pops), dashed = the bounds V², (V + E) log₂ V, E log₂ E; the readout gives the counts at the largest V, the agreement checks, and the crossover density at V = 128 under three accountings

07 Bellman-Ford, stepped: V − 1 rounds, early exit, the negative cycle found and reported

The Bellman-Ford algorithm computes single-source shortest paths with edge weights of any sign by relaxing every edge of the graph, in any fixed order, V − 1 times over — and then relaxing every edge once more: if that extra round still lowers an estimate, a negative cycle is reachable from the source, and the algorithm reports it instead of distances. It needs no priority queue, no restriction on the weights and no structure in the graph, and it costs O(VE) for that generality: on a sparse graph a factor of V/log V more than Dijkstra. It is the algorithm of last resort for single-source shortest paths, the negative-cycle detector every other method relies on, the engine of difference-constraint solving (§10) and of Johnson's reweighting (§14), and — as §08 shows — a dynamic program in disguise.

the algorithmBELLMAN-FORD(G, w, s): INITIALISE(G, s) for i = 1 … V − 1: ← the rounds changed ← false for each edge (u, v) ∈ E, in a fixed order: RELAX(u, v, w); if d[v] fell, changed ← true if not changed: return d, π ← EARLY EXIT: a round that changes nothing means no edge is tense; nothing will ever change again for each edge (u, v) ∈ E: ← the V-th round, the TEST if d[v] > d[u] + w(u, v): report NEGATIVE CYCLE (and, optionally, find it: below) return d, π the invariant, and the costINVARIANT after round i, d[v] = δ(s, v) for every v whose shortest path from s has at most i edges (proof: §08, the path-relaxation property) since a shortest path is simple it has ≤ V − 1 edges — so after round V − 1 every reachable v is correct, if no negative cycle is reachable COST each round examines every edge once: Θ(E) per round; ≤ V − 1 rounds + the test round ⇒ O(V · E) time, Θ(V) space beyond the graph the round count is ≤ (the largest number of edges on any shortest path) + 1 with early exit — often far below V − 1, and it depends on the EDGE ORDER with the edges of every shortest path met in path order, one round suffices (+ one to confirm); met in reverse order, as many rounds as the longest shortest path has edges — all V − 1 when that path uses every vertex, as here reporting the cycle, not just its existencelet x be any vertex whose estimate fell in the test round. x lies on, or is reachable from, a negative cycle — but need not be ON it. walk the predecessor pointers back V times: y ← x; repeat V times: y ← π[y]. after V steps y is on a cycle of G_π (V + 1 vertices visited, one repeats) then follow π from y until y is reached again: that is the cycle, and its weight is negative (§08 proves it) the vertices with δ = −∞: every vertex whose estimate fell in the test round, and everything reachable from them (§08: exactly the vertices a reachable negative cycle can reach)

Worked in full: five vertices, eight edges, one negative edge

Vertices s, 1, 2, 3, 4; edges, in the order the routine relaxes them, 4→1 (3), 3→4 (−2), 2→4 (6), 2→3 (4), 1→3 (6), 1→2 (−3), s→2 (5), s→1 (4). There is one negative edge, 1→2, and one cycle, 1→2→3→4→1, of weight −3 + 4 − 2 + 3 = 2 > 0, so δ is well defined; brute force over the 14 simple paths from s gives δ = ⟨0, 4, 1, 5, 3⟩, with the shortest path to every vertex on the chain s→1→2→3→4. The edge order is the worst one for this chain — its edges appear in reverse — so each round fixes one more vertex and the algorithm needs all V − 1 = 4 rounds:

roundrelaxations that improved something (in edge order)d[s 1 2 3 4] afterchanged
(start)—0 ∞ ∞ ∞ ∞—
1s→2: ∞→5 · s→1: ∞→4 (the six earlier edges have ∞ tails)0 4 5 ∞ ∞2
22→4: 5 + 6 → 11 · 2→3: 5 + 4 → 9 · 1→3: 4 + 6 = 10 ≥ 9, no · 1→2: 4 − 3 → 10 4 1 9 113
34→1: 11 + 3 = 14, no · 3→4: 9 − 2 → 7 · 2→4: 1 + 6 = 7, no · 2→3: 1 + 4 → 50 4 1 5 72
43→4: 5 − 2 → 3 (nothing else moves)0 4 1 5 31
5 (test)nothing changes — no negative cycle; stop0 4 1 5 30

Read the rounds against the invariant: after round 1 the vertices whose shortest path has one edge (1, via s→1) are correct — and so is s; after round 2, vertex 2 (s→1→2, two edges) is correct; after round 3, vertex 3; after round 4, vertex 4. Each round also carries estimates that are not yet final — d[2] = 5 after round 1, d[3] = 9 and d[4] = 11 after round 2 — and those are the upper bounds the properties of §02 promise: never below δ, lowered later. The early exit fires in round 5, which found no tense edge; had the graph had many more vertices the algorithm would still have stopped there, because "no edge is tense" is a fixed point. Measured: 40 relaxations, five rounds of eight, of which 8 improved an estimate; rounds until stable 4. The same graph with its edge list reversed — s→1 first, 4→1 last — meets the chain in order, is correct after round 1, and exits after round 2: 16 relaxations, 7 improvements. Both counts are what the tracing script produced and what the figure re-derives; the edge order changes the work by a factor of 2.5 on this instance and by up to V/2 in general (V·E relaxations for the worst order against 2·E for the best, the confirming round included), which is why §08 discusses ordering heuristics — and why none of them changes the worst case.

Worked: the same graph with a negative cycle

Change the weight of 4→1 from 3 to −6. The cycle 1→2→3→4→1 now weighs −3 + 4 − 2 − 6 = −7, it is reachable from s, and it reaches every vertex but s; so δ(s, s) = 0 and δ = −∞ for 1, 2, 3, 4. The first three rounds are identical to the table above (the edge 4→1 has tail ∞ in round 1 and 11, 7 thereafter, and 11 − 6 = 5 ≥ 4 does not improve 1 in round 3). In round 4, 4→1 relaxes 7 − 6 = 1 < 4 and the cycle begins to turn: d = ⟨0, 1, −2, 5, 3⟩. The test round lowers three estimates more, to ⟨0, −3, −6, 2, 3⟩, and the algorithm reports a negative cycle. To name it, take the first vertex whose estimate fell in the test round — vertex 1 — and walk π back five times: 1 → 4 → 3 → 2 → 1 → 4, landing on 4; then follow π from 4 until it returns: 4, 3, 2, 1, which in edge direction is the cycle 1→2→3→4→1 of weight −7. The −∞ set is {1, 2, 3, 4}: the vertices improved in the test round and everything reachable from them. Brute force confirms both: the lightest of the 3 simple cycles reachable from s weighs −7, and the set of vertices reachable from a negative cycle is exactly {1, 2, 3, 4}. The finite values the algorithm holds for those vertices when it stops mean nothing — they are the estimates after five rounds of a process that would go to −∞ if allowed to continue — and an implementation must not return them as distances.

Bellman-Ford one round per step: the edge list at the right in relaxation order, d[] under each vertex, the early exit — or the negative cycle, reported and lit green vertex = its estimate fell this round, blue edges = the current π-pointers, red = the reported negative cycle; the edge list marks the edges that improved something this round

What the algorithm returns, and what to do with a negative cycle

Without a negative cycle the return value is the same d, π pair as Dijkstra's, and the predecessor-subgraph property makes π a shortest-paths tree; unreachable vertices keep d = ∞ and π = NIL by the no-path property. With a negative cycle three outputs are possible and an implementation should choose deliberately: a boolean ("there is a negative cycle reachable from s"), the cycle itself as a vertex list, or the partition of V into finite-δ vertices (still correct: a vertex no negative cycle can reach has its shortest path relaxed in order during the first V − 1 rounds regardless of what happens elsewhere), −∞ vertices, and unreachable ones. The third is what §10's constraint solver and §14's reweighting need. A negative cycle in the whole graph, not just the part reachable from s, is found by the usual trick of a virtual source joined to every vertex by a zero-weight edge, which makes everything reachable and adds nothing to any cycle. And in the arbitrage reading of §03 — weights −log(rate) — the reported cycle is the sequence of trades, its weight is −log of the round-trip gain, and the vertices it reaches are the currencies from which that gain is accessible.

08 Why Bellman-Ford is right: the path-relaxation property, and the DP view

Bellman-Ford is correct because a shortest path has at most V − 1 edges and each round relaxes every edge, so after round i the first i edges of every shortest path have been relaxed in path order — and the path-relaxation property of §02 then guarantees the estimate at the end of any shortest path with at most i edges is exact. The negative-cycle test is correct in both directions by a two-line argument each way, and the reported cycle is genuinely negative by the argument that already showed the predecessor subgraph is acyclic. Underneath, the algorithm is the dynamic program "shortest walk with at most k edges" evaluated for k = 1 … V − 1, which explains the round count, the space, and why the in-place one-array version is never worse than the recurrence it implements.

theorem and proof — no negative cycle reachable from sCLAIM after round i, d[v] = δ(s, v) for every v that has a shortest path with ≤ i edges. PROOF let p = ⟨v₀ = s, v₁, …, vₖ = v⟩ be a shortest path with k ≤ i edges (it exists: with no negative cycle reachable, some shortest path is simple, so k ≤ V − 1). round j relaxes every edge, in particular (vⱼ₋₁, vⱼ); so (v₀, v₁) is relaxed in round 1, (v₁, v₂) in round 2, …, (vₖ₋₁, vₖ) in round k ≤ i — the edges of p in path order, with other relaxations in between. PATH-RELAXATION PROPERTY: d[vₖ] = δ(s, vₖ) after round k, and it stays (upper-bound). ∎ COROLLARY after round V − 1 every reachable vertex is exact; unreachable vertices keep d = ∞ (no-path property); G_π is a shortest-paths tree (predecessor-subgraph property). and the early exit is sound: a round in which no estimate falls means no edge is tense, and a further round would test the same edges against the same values. the test round, both directions(no negative cycle reachable ⇒ the test finds no tense edge) after round V − 1, d[v] = δ(s, v) for all v. for any edge (u, v): d[v] = δ(s, v) ≤ δ(s, u) + w(u, v) = d[u] + w(u, v) (triangle inequality) — not tense. ∎ (a negative cycle c = ⟨v₀, …, vₖ = v₀⟩ reachable ⇒ the test finds a tense edge) suppose not: d[vᵢ] ≤ d[vᵢ₋₁] + w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) for i = 1 … k. every vᵢ is reachable, so every d[vᵢ] is finite after V − 1 rounds. sum the k inequalities: ∑ᵢ d[vᵢ] ≤ ∑ᵢ d[vᵢ₋₁] + w(c). the two sums run over the same vertices (vₖ = v₀), so they cancel: 0 ≤ w(c) — contradicting w(c) < 0. ∎ why the reported cycle is negativelet x be a vertex whose estimate fell in the test round. for every vertex v with finite d and π[v] ≠ NIL: d[v] ≥ d[π[v]] + w(π[v], v) (equality when π[v] was set; d[π[v]] can only have fallen since). if the π-chain from x ran back to s without repeating (π[s] = NIL, d[s] = 0): chaining the inequalities gives d[x] ≥ w(a simple s ⇝ x path) ≥ min over walks with ≤ V − 1 edges but after round V − 1, d[x] ≤ that minimum (the DP view below), and it FELL in round V: d[x] < that minimum. contradiction. so the chain repeats a vertex within V steps: walking π back V times from x lands on a cycle of G_π, and a cycle of G_π is a negative cycle — the summation argument of §02 (predecessor-subgraph property) shows that closing a π-cycle forces w(cycle) < 0. ∎

The dynamic-programming view: "at most k edges"

Dynamic Programming, §21 arrives at Bellman-Ford from the other side: shortest paths are a DP on a DAG, a graph with cycles has no evaluation order, and the cure is to add a parameter that strictly decreases — the number of edges allowed. Define dₖ(v) as the weight of the shortest s → v walk using at most k edges. Then d₀(s) = 0, d₀(v) = ∞ otherwise, and dₖ(v) = min( dₖ₋₁(v), min over edges (u, v) of dₖ₋₁(u) + w(u, v) ): a shortest walk of at most k edges either has fewer than k edges, or ends with some edge (u, v) preceded by a shortest walk of at most k − 1 edges to u — cut and paste on the last edge. The state space is V × V (vertex, edge budget), each state reads its in-edges, so the cost is (V − 1) × E: Bellman-Ford's bound is the DP's "states times choices". With no negative cycle reachable, a shortest walk is a simple path, d_{V−1}(v) = δ(s, v), and the recurrence is the algorithm, with round k computing row k from row k − 1. Two things the DP view makes obvious and the graph view does not. The rows are independent of edge order — dₖ is defined by a minimum over all walks, not by a schedule — so the two-array implementation always takes exactly V − 1 rounds and does not benefit from a good ordering; measured on the §07 instance, the rows are ⟨0 ∞ ∞ ∞ ∞⟩, ⟨0 4 5 ∞ ∞⟩, ⟨0 4 1 9 11⟩, ⟨0 4 1 5 7⟩, ⟨0 4 1 5 3⟩ for both the list order and its reverse, 32 relaxations either way. And the negative-cycle test is the statement that d_V ≠ d_{V−1}: a walk of V edges beats every walk of fewer, which is only possible if it goes round a cycle and the cycle pays.

Why the in-place version is at least as good as the two-array version

The algorithm of §07 keeps a single array and relaxes edges using values already updated in the current round; the recurrence reads only the previous row. The one-array version is not the recurrence, but it dominates it. Claim: after round k of the in-place algorithm, d[v] ≤ dₖ(v) for every v. Proof: induction on k; the base is initialisation. In round k, when the edge (u, v) is relaxed the in-place value of u is at most its value at the end of round k − 1, which is ≤ dₖ₋₁(u) by hypothesis, so the relaxation leaves d[v] ≤ dₖ₋₁(u) + w(u, v); and d[v] was already ≤ dₖ₋₁(v) at the round's start and never rises. Taking the minimum over in-edges, d[v] ≤ dₖ(v). Meanwhile the upper-bound property keeps d[v] ≥ δ(s, v) throughout, since the in-place algorithm is a sequence of relaxations. So the in-place estimates are squeezed between δ and the recurrence's rows: they reach δ no later than the two-array version, sometimes much earlier — on the §07 instance with the reversed edge order the in-place version is exact after one round while the recurrence still needs four — and they can be checked for stability to exit early, which the recurrence cannot. Space is Θ(V) either way (the recurrence keeps two rows), but the in-place form is the one to implement. This is the same observation as the DP page's rolling-array discussion, with one difference of substance: rolling a DP array is a space optimisation that must not change the answer, whereas relaxing in place changes the intermediate values and is licensed only because relaxation can never overshoot.

Ordering heuristics, and the queue-based variant

Because the round count depends on how the edge order meets the shortest paths, two heuristics are common and neither changes the worst case. The first fixes a vertex order, splits the edges into those that go forward in it and those that go backward, and relaxes all forward edges in vertex order then all backward edges in reverse vertex order; a shortest path alternates between forward and backward runs and each round handles one run of each kind, so ⌈V/2⌉ rounds suffice — half the work, same order of growth. The second is the queue-based variant, often called SPFA: keep a FIFO queue of vertices whose estimate has changed since their out-edges were last relaxed, initially just s; pop a vertex, relax its out-edges, and push any vertex whose estimate fell if it is not already queued. It relaxes only edges that might be tense, which on many graphs is a small fraction of E per "round", and it terminates when the queue empties. Its worst case is the same O(VE) — each vertex can be dequeued up to V − 1 times, once per edge count of its shortest path, and adversarial instances (certain grids with weights chosen so that estimates fall by one repeatedly) drive it there — and it detects a negative cycle when some vertex is dequeued a V-th time or, equivalently, when a π-chain grows to V edges. It is a heuristic with a good typical case and no better guarantee, and it is not measured here for that reason: an instance family on which it looks fast says nothing about the family on which it is slow. When the weights are non-negative, Dijkstra's ordering is the heuristic that does carry a guarantee — each edge relaxed once — and that is the whole difference between the two algorithms: one commits to an order and needs w ≥ 0 to justify it, the other commits to nothing and pays V − 1 rounds for the freedom.

What to carry forward. Bellman-Ford's proof is the path-relaxation property plus "a shortest path has ≤ V − 1 edges"; Dijkstra's is the same property plus a reason the first relaxation of each edge is already in path order. The negative-cycle test is exact in both directions, and the reported cycle is negative. The DP view gives the bound as states × choices and shows that rounds, not edge order, are what the recurrence counts; the in-place algorithm is a dominated-by-δ, dominating-the-recurrence sequence of relaxations, which is why it may exit early and why it is the version to write. The DAG algorithm of §09 is what remains when the graph has a topological order and the "at most k edges" parameter is no longer needed.

09 Shortest and longest paths in a DAG: one pass, critical paths

On a directed acyclic graph, single-source shortest paths are computed by relaxing every edge exactly once, taking the vertices in a topological order — Θ(V + E) time, no priority queue, and no restriction on the sign of the weights. The reason is the path-relaxation property of §02: relaxing the edges of a shortest path in the order they appear on the path makes the endpoint's estimate exact, whatever other relaxations happen in between. A topological order relaxes the edges of every path in path order at once, so one sweep suffices. The same sweep with max in place of min computes longest paths — which is intractable in general graphs and trivial here — and that is the critical-path computation of project scheduling.

the algorithm and its costDAG-SHORTEST-PATHS(G, w, s): order ← a topological order of G (Graph Representations & Traversal, §20 — Θ(V + E)) d[v] ← ∞ for every v; d[s] ← 0; π[v] ← NIL for u in order: (every u is taken AFTER every vertex with an edge into u) for each edge (u, v): RELAX(u, v, w) (if d[u] + w(u, v) < d[v]: d[v] ← d[u] + w(u, v); π[v] ← u) cost: Θ(V + E) for the sort + Θ(V) to initialise + exactly E relaxations = Θ(V + E) space Θ(V) beyond the graph the invariant the order buyswhen u is taken from the order, d[u] = δ(s, u) already — every edge into u was relaxed from a vertex whose own estimate was already exact LONGEST paths: the same sweep with d[v] ← −∞, and ">" in place of "<" — or negate every weight and run the shortest version, which is legal here because a DAG has no cycle to become negative

Why one pass is enough: the path-relaxation property applied to every path at once

Take any vertex v reachable from s and a shortest path p = ⟨s = v₀, v₁, …, vₖ = v⟩. Every edge (vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) of p is a directed edge, so vᵢ₋₁ precedes vᵢ in any topological order, and the sweep processes v₀ before v₁ before … before vₖ₋₁. Since the sweep relaxes all out-edges of a vertex when it processes that vertex, the edges of p are relaxed in the order (v₀, v₁), (v₁, v₂), …, (vₖ₋₁, vₖ) — possibly with many other relaxations in between, which is exactly the situation the path-relaxation property covers: after that sequence, d[v] = δ(s, v), and by the upper-bound property nothing later can lower it further or raise it at all. The argument used nothing about the weights' signs — only that p is a shortest path and that shortest paths are well defined, which in a DAG they always are, since a negative-weight cycle is the only thing that can make them undefined (§03) and a DAG has no cycle of any weight. Vertices not reachable from s keep d = ∞ because no relaxation from a finite estimate ever reaches them. The predecessor pointers form a shortest-path tree by the predecessor-subgraph property. The cost accounting is an aggregate argument: the inner loop runs once per out-edge of each vertex, and summing out-degrees over all vertices gives E, so exactly E relaxations happen regardless of the order chosen among the (possibly many) valid topological orders.

Longest paths, and why negation is legal here and not in general

Longest simple paths in a general directed graph are NP-hard (a longest simple path visiting every vertex is a Hamiltonian path). The reason the DAG case collapses to one pass is the same reason shortest paths needed no sign restriction: the cut-and-paste step "replace the prefix of the path by a better prefix" produces a walk that might repeat a vertex in a cyclic graph, and in a DAG cannot. So the argument above runs verbatim with max for min, −∞ for ∞, and "longest" for "shortest". The second route to the same answer is to negate every weight and run the shortest-path sweep unchanged: the longest path under w is the shortest under −w, and the value flips sign. The reason this is not a general recipe — "to get longest paths, negate and run Dijkstra" is a classic wrong answer — is that negation turns every positive cycle into a negative cycle, and a negative cycle makes shortest paths undefined and breaks Dijkstra's invariant (§05) besides. In a DAG there is no cycle to flip, so −w is as harmless as w. The routine behind figure 06 computes the longest values both ways on the task graph below and checks they agree.

Critical-path scheduling: seven tasks, worked in full

A project is a set of tasks with durations and precedence constraints, "u must finish before v starts".
