The earliest time the whole project can finish is the length of the longest chain of dependent tasks, the critical path; every task on it has no room to slip, and any other task can be delayed by its slack without delaying the project. Take seven tasks — A (3), B (2), C (4), D (5), E (2), F (3), G (1) — with C after A; D after A and B; E after C and D; F after D; G after E and F. The graph the figure builds is activity-on-node turned into an edge-weighted DAG: an edge u → v of weight dur(u) for each precedence, a source S with zero-weight edges to the tasks that have no prerequisites (A, B), and a sink T reached from each task without successors (G) by an edge of its own duration — 9 vertices and 11 edges. Four quantities per task fall out of two sweeps:

earliest start · latest start · slackES(v) = longest path from S to v = max over prerequisites u of ES(u) + dur(u) (0 if none) — the forward sweep in topological order EF(v) = ES(v) + dur(v); project length L = max over v of EF(v) = longest S → T path = 12 LF(v) = L − (longest path from v to T, excluding dur(v)) = min over successors x of LS(x) (L if none) — the backward sweep, i.e. the forward sweep on the reversed DAG LS(v) = LF(v) − dur(v); slack(v) = LS(v) − ES(v); critical ⇔ slack(v) = 0 two sweeps ⇒ 2·E = 22 relaxations measured on the 11-edge graph

Reading the forward sweep: A and B start at 0; D cannot start until both A (done at 3) and B (done at 2) finish, so ES(D) = max(3, 2) = 3; E waits for C (7) and D (8), so 8; G waits for E (10) and F (11), so 11, and the project finishes at 11 + 1 = 12. The critical path is A → D → F → G, 3 + 5 + 3 + 1 = 12, and it is the only chain of the five S → T chains (ACEG = 10, ADEG = 11, ADFG = 12, BDEG = 10, BDFG = 11) with the full length. Backwards: G must finish by 12, so LS(G) = 11; E must finish by 11, LS(E) = 9, one unit later than its earliest start of 8, so E has slack 1; C must finish by LS(E) = 9, LS(C) = 5, slack 2; B must finish by LS(D) = 3, slack 1. Every number in the table is computed by the figure's routine at load and cross-checked by enumerating all 20 paths that leave S (21 counting the empty path at S). The "shortest" toggle of the figure runs the identical sweep with min: it returns 10, the length of the shortest S → T chain (ACEG or BDEG), which is not a scheduling quantity — it is there to show that the code path is the same and only the comparison changes.

The dynamic-programming view, and where this sits among the algorithms

The sweep is a dynamic program whose state is a vertex and whose evaluation order is the topological order — Dynamic Programming, §21 develops that view, shows that every table-filling DP is a shortest- or longest-path computation on the DAG of its subproblems, and derives Bellman-Ford and Floyd-Warshall as the same recursion with an extra parameter that restores acyclicity when the graph has cycles. Relative to the rest of this page: Dijkstra (§04) needs w ≥ 0 and pays O((V + E) log V) for its heap; Bellman-Ford (§07) allows negative edges and pays O(VE); the DAG sweep allows negative edges, pays Θ(V + E), and computes longest paths for free — but only on a DAG. On a graph that is not known to be acyclic, the topological sort's failure (a back edge, or fewer than V vertices output) is the test, and it costs the same Θ(V + E). Two edge cases worth naming: a vertex with no in-edges other than the source is exact after the source's out-edges are relaxed; and a DAG with several valid topological orders gives the same d[] under all of them, though the predecessor tree may differ where ties exist, as it does for the two shortest chains above.

One pass over the task DAG in topological order — shortest or longest from S, the critical path lit vertices are taken left to right in topological order; the vertex being processed is amber and each vertex shows its current d[]; in longest mode the critical path A → D → F → G is drawn in green once the sweep ends; the readout measures the relaxations, enumerates every path from S to cross-check, and re-derives the longest values by negating the weights

10 Systems of difference constraints as a shortest-path problem

A system of difference constraints is a set of m inequalities of the form xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ bₖ over n unknowns, and it is feasible if and only if its constraint graph — one vertex per unknown, one edge vᵢ → vⱼ of weight bₖ per constraint, plus a source v₀ with a zero-weight edge to everything — has no negative-weight cycle; when it is feasible, xᵢ = δ(v₀, vᵢ) is a solution. This is the first place on the page where a shortest-path algorithm solves something that is not, on its face, a path problem, and it is the reason Bellman-Ford's negative-cycle report (§07) matters as an answer rather than as an error: the cycle is a certificate that the constraints contradict each other. Time windows in scheduling, clock-skew and timing analysis in circuits, and the feasibility half of a linear program whose every row has one +1 and one −1 are all instances.

the constraint graph, and the theoremunknowns x₁ … xₙ; constraint k: xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ bₖ ⟶ edge vᵢ → vⱼ of weight bₖ ("xⱼ is at most bₖ more than xᵢ") plus v₀ → vᵢ of weight 0 for every i (so every vertex — and every cycle — is reachable from v₀) V = n + 1, E = n + m THEOREM. (a) if the graph has no negative cycle, then x = ( δ(v₀, v₁), …, δ(v₀, vₙ) ) satisfies every constraint; (b) if the graph has a negative cycle, the system has no solution at all. COROLLARY. Bellman-Ford from v₀ decides feasibility and produces a solution: O(V·E) = O((n + 1)(n + m)) = O(n² + nm) (n·m once the zero edges are replaced by initialising every d[vᵢ] to 0 and starting the rounds — the same first round) NOT UNIQUE. if x is a solution so is x + c for any constant c: (xⱼ + c) − (xᵢ + c) = xⱼ − xᵢ. The δ solution has max xᵢ = 0.

Proof, both directions

(a) The distances satisfy every constraint. Take any constraint xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ bₖ, i.e. any edge vᵢ → vⱼ of weight bₖ. The triangle inequality for shortest-path weights (§02) says δ(v₀, vⱼ) ≤ δ(v₀, vᵢ) + w(vᵢ, vⱼ) — a shortest path to vⱼ is no longer than "a shortest path to vᵢ, then the edge". Substituting xᵢ = δ(v₀, vᵢ) and xⱼ = δ(v₀, vⱼ) gives xⱼ ≤ xᵢ + bₖ, which is the constraint. The v₀ edges are what make every δ finite (every vertex is one zero-weight edge from the source), so the substitution is always defined; without v₀, an unknown unreachable from the chosen source would get xᵢ = ∞. (b) A negative cycle is a contradiction. Let c = ⟨v₁, v₂, …, vₖ = v₁⟩ be a cycle of negative total weight; v₀ is not on it, since v₀ has no incoming edges, so every edge of the cycle is a constraint: x₂ − x₁ ≤ w(v₁, v₂), x₃ − x₂ ≤ w(v₂, v₃), …, x₁ − xₖ₋₁ ≤ w(vₖ₋₁, v₁). Suppose a solution existed and add these k − 1 inequalities. On the left every unknown appears once with + and once with −, so the sum telescopes to 0; on the right the sum is w(c) < 0. That is 0 ≤ w(c) < 0, a contradiction, so no solution exists. Note the asymmetry the proof exposes: a negative cycle is a local certificate of infeasibility, a handful of constraints that already contradict each other, and Bellman-Ford's predecessor walk (§07) produces it explicitly.

Worked: five unknowns, eight constraints

The system, with its edge, is:

kconstraintedgekconstraintedge
1x₁ − x₅ ≤ 5v₅ → v₁ (5)5x₃ − x₂ ≤ −1v₂ → v₃ (−1)
2x₅ − x₄ ≤ 2v₄ → v₅ (2)6x₅ − x₂ ≤ 3v₂ → v₅ (3)
3x₄ − x₃ ≤ −2v₃ → v₄ (−2)7x₁ − x₃ ≤ 4v₃ → v₁ (4)
4x₃ − x₅ ≤ 1v₅ → v₃ (1)8x₂ − x₁ ≤ −2v₁ → v₂ (−2)

The graph has V = 6 vertices and E = 13 edges (five from v₀, eight constraints). Bellman-Ford from v₀, relaxing the edges in the order listed — the five v₀ edges first, then constraints 1 to 8 — produces the rounds below; the order matters for how many rounds are needed, never for the answer.

after roundd[v₀]d[v₁]d[v₂]d[v₃]d[v₄]d[v₅]changed?
100−2−1−20yes
200−2−3−30yes
300−2−3−5−1yes
400−2−3−5−3yes
500−2−3−5−3no — stop

Five rounds of 13 relaxations, 65 in all, and the fifth round is the one that changes nothing and ends the run early (the worst case would be V − 1 = 5 productive rounds and a sixth to test for a cycle). The solution is x = (0, −2, −3, −5, −3). Checking every constraint: x₁ − x₅ = 3 ≤ 5; x₅ − x₄ = 2 ≤ 2; x₄ − x₃ = −2 ≤ −2; x₃ − x₅ = 0 ≤ 1; x₃ − x₂ = −1 ≤ −1; x₅ − x₂ = −1 ≤ 3; x₁ − x₃ = 3 ≤ 4; x₂ − x₁ = −2 ≤ −2 — all satisfied, four of them with equality, which is characteristic: the shortest-path solution pushes every unknown as far down as its constraints allow, so the constraints along the shortest-path tree are tight. Why does x₅ take four rounds to settle? Its final value comes from the chain v₀ → v₁ → v₂ → v₃ → v₄ → v₅ (0 − 2 − 1 − 2 + 2 = −3), and the listed edge order relaxes v₄ → v₅ (constraint 2) before v₃ → v₄ (3) before v₂ → v₃ (5) before v₁ → v₂ (8), so each round carries the improvement one edge further along the chain — v₂ in round 1, v₃ in round 2, v₄ in round 3, v₅ in round 4, as the table shows — the mechanism §08 proves in general. Shifting the whole solution by +4 to (4, 2, 1, −1, 1) satisfies every constraint too, as the lemma says; the figure checks that as well.

Infeasible: flip one constraint

Replace constraint 4 by x₃ − x₅ ≤ −3 (edge v₅ → v₃ of weight −3). Now the cycle v₅ → v₃ → v₄ → v₅ has weight −3 − 2 + 2 = −3 < 0, and the three constraints on it — x₃ − x₅ ≤ −3, x₄ − x₃ ≤ −2, x₅ − x₄ ≤ 2 — add to 0 ≤ −3: no assignment can satisfy all three, whatever the other five say. Bellman-Ford shows it dynamically: the rounds never stop changing (d[v₄] runs −2, −5, −5, −8, −8, −11, … — it drops by 3 every second round as the cycle turns; d[v₃] runs −3, −3, −6, −6, −9, −9, …), the sixth round — the V-th — still improves an estimate, and the predecessor walk from the improved vertex lands on exactly that cycle. 6 rounds × 13 = 78 relaxations, measured in the figure's "flipped" mode.

Where the systems come from

Scheduling with windows. Let xᵢ be the start time of event i. "j starts at least 2 hours after i" is xᵢ − xⱼ ≤ −2; "at most 5 hours after" is xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ 5; "j and i start within an hour of each other" is the pair xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ 1, xᵢ − xⱼ ≤ 1. Any mixture of minimum gaps, maximum gaps and simultaneity constraints is a difference system, and the shortest-path solution is the schedule in which every event happens as late as the constraints allow relative to the latest one (all xᵢ ≤ 0); reversing every edge (keeping its weight), solving from v₀ again, and negating the result gives the as-early-as-possible schedule (all xᵢ ≥ 0, componentwise minimal) — the substitution x = −x′ turns xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ b into x′ᵢ − x′ⱼ ≤ b, which reverses the edge and keeps its weight. Checked on two constraints, x₂ − x₁ ≤ 5 and x₁ − x₂ ≤ −2 ("event 2 at least 2 after event 1, at most 5 after"): the graph has v₁ → v₂ (5) and v₂ → v₁ (−2), so δ = (−2, 0) is the latest schedule; reversing gives v₂ → v₁ (5) and v₁ → v₂ (−2), δ′ = (0, −2), and negating gives the earliest schedule (0, 2). Negating the weights as well would give (11, 9), which violates x₁ − x₂ ≤ −2. Critical-path scheduling (§09) is the special case with only minimum gaps — no maximum gaps means no cycles, hence a DAG and one pass. Timing analysis. Signal arrival times along a circuit with minimum and maximum delays per stage, clock-skew bounds between registers, and retiming constraints are difference systems on thousands of unknowns; a negative cycle is a timing violation, and the cycle Bellman-Ford returns is the set of paths that cannot all meet their bounds. Linear programming. A difference system is a linear program with no objective, each row of the matrix holding one +1 and one −1. General LP needs the simplex method or an interior-point method; this special structure is solved by a graph algorithm in O(nm), and adding an objective "maximise Σ xᵢ" is still solvable by the same machinery (the δ solution is componentwise maximal among solutions with max xᵢ ≤ 0). Two things it does not extend to: constraints with a coefficient other than ±1, and constraints on three or more unknowns — either change destroys the telescoping that made the cycle sum vanish.

The constraint graph with Bellman-Ford from v₀ stepped round by round; the solution x[] and every constraint checked; flip one constraint to see the negative cycle each step is one round of relaxing all 13 edges; a vertex whose estimate changed in that round is amber; in the flipped system the V-th round still changes something and the cycle it certifies is drawn in red; the readout measures relaxations and checks every constraint against the computed x, then re-checks the shifted solution and the brute-force distances

11 0-1 BFS and Dial's buckets: when the weights are small integers

When every edge weight is 0 or 1, single-source shortest paths take O(V + E) with a deque in place of Dijkstra's heap: a relaxation across a 0-edge pushes the vertex to the front, across a 1-edge to the back, and the deque then pops vertices in non-decreasing distance order exactly as a heap would. When the weights are integers in 0 … C, Dial's algorithm keeps C + 1 buckets indexed by distance and sweeps them in order, in O(E + V·C). Both are Dijkstra (§04) with the priority queue replaced by a structure that exploits what the keys are: the correctness proof of §05 only needs the vertices to be settled in non-decreasing order of distance, and a heap is merely the general-purpose way to obtain that order. When the keys take few distinct values, a cheaper structure gives the same order.

0-1 BFSd[s] ← 0, others ∞; deque ← ⟨(s, 0)⟩ while deque not empty: (u, ℓ) ← pop-front; if ℓ > d[u] or u already expanded: skip (stale) — each vertex is expanded once for each edge (u, v): if d[u] + w < d[v]: d[v] ← d[u] + w; w = 0 ⇒ push-front (v, d[v]) w = 1 ⇒ push-back (v, d[v]) INVARIANT the labels in the deque, front to back, are non-decreasing and take at most TWO distinct values, D and D + 1 ⇒ pops come out in non-decreasing label order, so this is Dijkstra's settling order, and the §05 proof applies unchanged COST every vertex expanded once, every edge relaxed once, each push/pop O(1) ⇒ O(V + E); at most one entry enters the deque per successful relaxation, plus the source Dial's algorithm (bucket queue), integer weights 0 … Cbuckets B[0 … C] as a circular array, B[d mod (C + 1)] holds vertices whose label is d; a pointer cur sweeps 0, 1, 2, … pop from B[cur mod (C+1)] until empty, then cur ← cur + 1; a relaxation to label d[v] ≤ cur + C goes into B[d[v] mod (C+1)] WHY C + 1 SUFFICE every label in the buckets lies in [cur, cur + C] (it was cur′ + w with cur′ ≤ cur, w ≤ C, and nothing below cur is left) COST O(E) inserts and pops + the pointer advances at most max distance ≤ (V − 1)·C times ⇒ O(E + V·C) (radix heaps refine the buckets to widths 1, 1, 2, 4, 8, …, giving O(E + V·log C) — a pointer, not built here)

Why pushing to the front is right

The heap in Dijkstra exists to answer one question: which unsettled vertex has the smallest tentative distance? With weights in {0, 1}, the answer has a shape. Suppose the vertex just popped has label D. A relaxation across a 0-edge gives its neighbour label D — the same as the current minimum, so it belongs ahead of everything at D + 1 and is tied with everything at D; pushing it to the front is safe because ties may be settled in any order. A relaxation across a 1-edge gives label D + 1, which is at least as large as every label already present (they are all D or D + 1), so it belongs at the back. Induction on the operations proves the invariant stated above: initially the deque holds one label; a pop-front removes the minimum and leaves a non-decreasing sequence of at most two values; a push-front of D when the minimum is D keeps both properties; a push-back of D + 1 when the maximum is at most D + 1 keeps both. Once the minimum has advanced to D + 1, the new pushes are D + 1 and D + 2, and the same two-value picture holds one step along. Because a vertex's tentative distance strictly decreases each time it is pushed, a vertex has at most one entry with its final label, and only that entry is expanded — the entries with larger labels are skipped as stale when they surface. So each vertex is expanded once and each of its edges relaxed once, and with O(1) deque operations the total is O(V + E): the bound of unweighted BFS, of which this is a strict generalisation (with all weights 1 the front is never used and the deque is a queue). The figure's readout verifies the invariant on the instance it draws — the largest number of distinct labels the deque ever held is printed — and the readout also repeats the check on 200 random 4 × 6 grids with 0/1 weights each time the page loads: two distinct labels at most, non-decreasing, every vertex expanded exactly once, distances equal to Dijkstra's, in all 200 (the same check on 500 grids was run while this page was written, with the same result).

Worked: six vertices, a back entry that goes stale

Directed edges 0→1 (1), 0→2 (0), 2→3 (1), 2→4 (0), 4→3 (0), 1→5 (1), 3→5 (1), 4→5 (1), source 0. Each row shows the deque after the named vertex has been expanded, entries written vertex(label):

Vertex 3 is the point of the example: it is first reached across the 1-edge 2→3 and pushed to the back with label 1, then reached across the 0-edge 4→3 and pushed to the front with label 0. The front entry is expanded; the back entry surfaces later, its label 1 exceeds d[3] = 0, and it is skipped. 7 pops (6 expansions and 1 stale), 3 front pushes, 3 back pushes, 8 relaxations = E, never more than two distinct labels in the deque, and distances ⟨0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1⟩ agreeing with Dijkstra — all re-measured in the figure when this instance is selected.

Dial's buckets, worked

Directed edges 0→1 (2), 0→2 (3), 1→2 (1), 1→3 (3), 2→3 (1), 2→4 (2), 3→5 (2), 4→5 (3), C = 3, so four buckets B[0 … 3] and a label d lives in B[d mod 4]. Popping 0 at cur = 0 inserts 1 (label 2 → B[2]) and 2 (label 3 → B[3]); the pointer advances through the empty B[1] to B[2] and pops 1 at cur = 2; 1→2 offers 2 + 1 = 3, a tie with the label 2 already holds, so nothing changes (the relaxation is strict); 1→3 inserts 3 at label 5 → B[1]; cur = 3 pops 2, improving 3 to label 4 → B[0] and 4 to 5 → B[1]; cur = 4 pops 3 from B[0] and inserts 5 at label 6 → B[2]; cur = 5 pops the stale entry of 3 (its label 5 exceeds d[3] = 4) and then 4; cur = 6 pops 5. Distances ⟨0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6⟩; 7 inserts, 7 pops (one stale), 8 relaxations, and the pointer made 13 bucket inspections — one per pop plus one per advance of the pointer, i.e. pops + (label of the last pop) = 7 + 6, here equal to pops + max distance because the last pop was a live one; when the last entry popped is stale its label can exceed the largest finalised distance by up to C, which is the inequality the figure's readout checks alongside the exact count. The wrap-around is visible: label 4 sits in B[0] and label 5 in B[1] while cur is still 3, and nothing goes wrong because every live label is within C of the pointer.

When these beat a binary heap, measured

The figure's default instance is a 5 × 8 grid, 40 vertices and 67 undirected edges, each edge weighted 0 or 1 by a seeded coin (35 of the 67 are zero). 0-1 BFS from the corner: 49 pops, of which 9 stale, 26 front pushes and 22 back pushes, 134 relaxations = 2E (each undirected edge is two arcs, each relaxed once from its own endpoint), the deque never holding more than two distinct labels, all distances at most 3. Dijkstra with a binary heap and lazy deletion on the same instance (§06) does 47 pushes and 47 pops, 7 of them stale, and each of those heap operations costs O(log n) where the deque's cost O(1). Both give the same distances. With weights 1 … 4 (C = 4) on the same grid, Dial's algorithm does 45 inserts, 45 pops (5 stale) and 65 bucket inspections against Dijkstra's 45 heap pushes and 45 pops; the last pop's label is the largest distance, 20, so the pointer advanced 20 times and 65 = 45 + 20. On a 40-vertex grid nothing is slow, and the point is the bound, not the stopwatch: O(E + V·C) beats O((V + E) log V) when C is smaller than log V — road networks with weights in metres do not qualify (C is in the thousands), but hop counts, small penalties, unit costs with a few zero-cost shortcuts, and the "minimum number of edits/flips/moves" problems that arise as implicit graphs (Graph Representations & Traversal, §09) do. 0-1 BFS is the more common of the two in practice, because 0/1 weights arise whenever a problem asks "the fewest bad steps" and the good steps are free. The radix heap, mentioned above, closes the gap for larger C: O(E + V log C), which beats the binary heap whenever C < V and is the structure behind the fastest practical integer-weight Dijkstra variants.

0-1 BFS with the deque drawn, or Dial's buckets on small integer weights — stepped, one expansion per step the grid's vertices show their current d[]; the vertex being expanded is amber and settled vertices blue; below the grid the deque (or the C + 1 buckets) is drawn as vertex(label) entries after the expansion; the readout measures every deque/bucket operation and checks the distances against a binary-heap Dijkstra run under its own counter

12 All pairs: V runs of Dijkstra, and repeated matrix squaring

The all-pairs shortest-paths problem asks for δ(i, j) for every ordered pair of vertices — a V × V distance matrix D, and usually a predecessor matrix Π from which any one shortest path can be read off. The obvious solution is V runs of a single-source algorithm: V Dijkstras when the weights are non-negative, O(V·(V + E) log V) with a binary heap, or V Bellman-Fords when they are not, O(V²E) — O(V⁴) on a dense graph. The all-pairs algorithms of this section and the next two are the ways to do better when V²E is too much: a matrix formulation whose repeated squaring gives Θ(V³ log V) for any weights without negative cycles (this section), Floyd-Warshall's Θ(V³) (§13), and Johnson's reweighting, which restores the V-Dijkstra bound in the presence of negative edges and wins on sparse graphs (§14).

the matrix formulation: paths of at most m edgesW = the weight matrix: wᵢᵢ = 0, wᵢⱼ = w(i, j) if the edge exists, ∞ otherwise L⁽ᵐ⁾ᵢⱼ = the least weight of any path from i to j using AT MOST m edges; L⁽⁰⁾ = I_min (0 on the diagonal, ∞ off it); L⁽¹⁾ = W L⁽ᵐ⁾ᵢⱼ = min( L⁽ᵐ⁻¹⁾ᵢⱼ, min over k of L⁽ᵐ⁻¹⁾ᵢₖ + wₖⱼ ) = min over k of ( L⁽ᵐ⁻¹⁾ᵢₖ + wₖⱼ ) (the first term is the k = j case, since wⱼⱼ = 0) — a shortest path of ≤ m edges is a shortest path of ≤ m − 1 edges to some k, then the edge (k, j): cut and paste on the last edge that is a MATRIX PRODUCT with (min, +) in place of (+, ×): L⁽ᵐ⁾ = L⁽ᵐ⁻¹⁾ ⊗ W, (A ⊗ B)ᵢⱼ = min over k of (Aᵢₖ + Bₖⱼ) no negative cycles ⇒ every shortest path is simple, has ≤ V − 1 edges ⇒ D = L⁽ⱽ⁻¹⁾ = L⁽ⱽ⁾ = L⁽ⱽ⁺¹⁾ = … costone ⊗ product: V³ (min, +) steps = Θ(V³) SLOW: L⁽¹⁾, L⁽²⁾ = L⁽¹⁾ ⊗ W, …, L⁽ⱽ⁻¹⁾ — V − 2 products = Θ(V⁴) (it is Bellman-Ford from every source at once) FAST: ⊗ is associative, so L⁽²ᵐ⁾ = L⁽ᵐ⁾ ⊗ L⁽ᵐ⁾: W, W², W⁴, W⁸, … until the exponent reaches V − 1 — ⌈log₂(V − 1)⌉ products = Θ(V³ log V) overshooting is harmless: L⁽ᵐ⁾ = L⁽ⱽ⁻¹⁾ for every m ≥ V − 1; space Θ(V²) with two matrices

Why (min, +) is a semiring, and why the fast matrix multiplication algorithms do not transfer

The product (A ⊗ B)ᵢⱼ = min_k (Aᵢₖ + Bₖⱼ) is a matrix product over the structure (ℝ ∪ {∞}, min, +): "addition" is min, with identity ∞; "multiplication" is +, with identity 0; both are associative and commutative, + distributes over min (a + min(b, c) = min(a + b, a + c)), and ∞ annihilates (∞ + a = ∞). That is exactly the list of axioms a semiring satisfies, and it is enough for matrix multiplication to be associative — which is the fact repeated squaring needs, since W⁴ = (W ⊗ W) ⊗ (W ⊗ W) only equals W ⊗ W ⊗ W ⊗ W if the bracketing does not matter. Associativity is also what makes the squaring meaningful: (L⁽ᵃ⁾ ⊗ L⁽ᵇ⁾)ᵢⱼ is the least weight of a path of at most a + b edges that splits into an at-most-a-edge prefix and an at-most-b-edge suffix at some vertex k, and every path of at most a + b edges splits that way. What the structure lacks is subtraction: min has no inverse (there is no x with min(3, x) = ∞). Strassen's algorithm and its successors (Recursion, Divide & Conquer, §19) multiply n × n matrices in fewer than n³ multiplications by computing clever sums and differences of submatrices and cancelling terms; without subtraction those cancellations are unavailable, and the Θ(V³) per product stands. The (min, +) product is in fact known to be equivalent in difficulty to all-pairs shortest paths itself, so a truly subcubic all-pairs algorithm on general real weights would need a new idea rather than a transplanted one; the best known bounds shave only sub-polynomial factors off V³. For the boolean semiring (or, and) — reachability rather than distance — the situation is different: boolean matrix product can be embedded in integer product and done in Strassen time, which is why transitive closure (§13) has a subcubic algorithm while all-pairs distances do not.

Worked: four vertices, two squarings

Vertices 0 … 3, five directed edges 0→1 (5), 0→3 (9), 1→2 (−2), 2→3 (4), 3→0 (−1); two negative edges, no negative cycle (the one cycle through them, 0→1→2→3→0, weighs 5 − 2 + 4 − 1 = 6; the other, 0→3→0, weighs 8). V − 1 = 3, so ⌈log₂ 3⌉ = 2 squarings: L⁽¹⁾ = W, L⁽²⁾ = W ⊗ W, L⁽⁴⁾ = L⁽²⁾ ⊗ L⁽²⁾, and L⁽⁴⁾ = L⁽³⁾ = D because 4 ≥ 3.

L⁽¹⁾ = W0123L⁽²⁾ (≤ 2 edges)0123L⁽⁴⁾ = D0123

Reading one entry of each product. L⁽²⁾₀₂ = min_k (L⁽¹⁾₀ₖ + L⁽¹⁾ₖ₂) = min(0 + ∞, 5 + (−2), ∞ + 0, 9 + ∞) = 3: the two-edge path 0→1→2. L⁽²⁾₀₃ stays 9: with at most two edges the only routes are the direct edge (9) and 0→?→3 via a vertex adjacent to both, and 0→2 does not exist yet, so 0→1→2→3 is out of reach. In the second squaring L⁽⁴⁾₀₃ = min_k (L⁽²⁾₀ₖ + L⁽²⁾ₖ₃) = min(0 + 9, 5 + 2, 3 + 4, 9 + 0) = 7, the three-edge path 0→1→2→3 found as "two edges to 1, then two edges from 1" or equally "two edges to 2, then one from 2" — several splits find the same path, which is why the product is a min and not a sum. L⁽⁴⁾₂₁ = 8 is the four-vertex tour 2→3→0→1, 4 − 1 + 5, the longest shortest path in the graph at three edges. The figure runs the routine under a counter: 2 products, 128 (min, +) steps = 2 × 4³, and checks D against Floyd-Warshall (64 triples) and against brute force over all 13 non-empty simple paths in the graph; it also runs the slow version, which needs the same 2 products here (V − 2 = 2) but V − 2 in general against ⌈log₂(V − 1)⌉, and confirms one more squaring leaves D unchanged.

What the three all-pairs strategies cost, and when each wins

methodweightstimespacewins when
V × Dijkstra, binary heapw ≥ 0O(V·(V + E) log V) = O(VE log V) for connected graphsΘ(V²) outputsparse, non-negative
V × Dijkstra, Fibonacci heapw ≥ 0O(V² log V + VE) amortisedΘ(V²)sparse, large V, if the constants are paid for
V × Bellman-Fordany, no negative cycleO(V²E), O(V⁴) denseΘ(V²)rarely — Johnson replaces it
repeated squaringany, no negative cycleΘ(V³ log V)Θ(V²)conceptually; in practice Floyd-Warshall is a log factor better
Floyd-Warshall (§13)any, no negative cycleΘ(V³)Θ(V²), in placedense graphs, E ≈ V²; tiny constant, cache-friendly
Johnson (§14)any, no negative cycleO(V² log V + VE) with a Fibonacci heap; O(VE log V) binaryΘ(V²)sparse graphs with negative edges

The reason to know the matrix view when Floyd-Warshall is strictly faster is that it generalises: replace (min, +) by (max, min) and the same squaring computes widest (bottleneck) paths; by (or, and) and it computes reachability; by (+, ×) over the counting semiring and it counts walks of exactly length m (not "at most": the counting semiring has no zero diagonal to absorb shorter walks); by (min, +) with the entries as vectors and it becomes the tropical algebra behind sequence alignment and Viterbi decoding (Dynamic Programming, §26). Every "path problem" whose combining operations form a semiring has this algorithm and — when a Floyd-Warshall-style closure applies — the next section's as well.

All pairs by repeated (min, +) squaring: W, W², W⁴ — the matrices at each squaring, stepped each step shows the next matrix L⁽ᵐ⁾ with the entries that improved since the previous one in amber and the corresponding at-most-m-edge paths now representable; the readout counts the (min, +) steps and the products, and checks the final matrix against Floyd-Warshall and against brute force over all simple paths

13 Floyd-Warshall, stepped, with path reconstruction and transitive closure

Floyd-Warshall computes all-pairs shortest paths in Θ(V³) time and Θ(V²) space by a dynamic program over a different parameter from §12's edge count: d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) is the least weight of a path from i to j whose intermediate vertices all lie in {1, …, k}, and d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) = min( d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j), d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) + d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j) ) — either the best path avoids vertex k, or it passes through k exactly once. Three nested loops with k outermost, a one-line body, no data structure, negative edges allowed; a negative cycle shows up as a negative diagonal entry; a "next-hop" matrix updated alongside recovers any path; and replacing (min, +) by (or, and) gives the transitive closure.

recurrence · invariant · algorithmvertices numbered 1 … V; an INTERMEDIATE vertex of a path ⟨v₁, v₂, …, vₗ⟩ is any of v₂ … vₗ₋₁ (not the endpoints) d⁽⁰⁾(i, j) = wᵢⱼ (no intermediates at all: the edge, or 0 on the diagonal, or ∞) d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) = min( d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j), d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) + d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j) ) for k ≥ 1 answer D = D⁽ⱽ⁾ — every vertex is allowed as an intermediate, so d⁽ⱽ⁾(i, j) = δ(i, j) FLOYD-WARSHALL(W): D ← W; for k = 1 … V: for i = 1 … V: for j = 1 … V: if D[i][k] + D[k][j] < D[i][j]: D[i][j] ← D[i][k] + D[k][j]; next[i][j] ← next[i][k] cost V³ triples (k, i, j), Θ(1) each = Θ(V³); one V × V matrix, updated in place = Θ(V²) next[i][j] = the vertex after i on the current best i → j path (initially j if the edge (i, j) exists, NIL otherwise); path i → j: follow next from i until j negative cycle ⇔ some D[i][i] < 0 at the end; transitive closure: t⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) = t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j) ∨ ( t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) ∧ t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j) )

Proof of the recurrence: the "intermediates ⊆ {1..k}" invariant

Fix k ≥ 1 and a pair (i, j), and let p be a shortest path from i to j whose intermediate vertices all lie in {1, …, k}; assume there are no negative cycles, so p can be taken simple. Two cases. k is not an intermediate vertex of p. Then all of p's intermediates lie in {1, …, k − 1}, so p is a candidate for d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j), and conversely every candidate for d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j) is a candidate for d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j); hence d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) = d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j). k is an intermediate vertex of p. Because p is simple, k appears once, and p splits as i ⇝ k ⇝ j into p₁ and p₂. Neither piece has k as an intermediate (it is their endpoint), so both have intermediates in {1, …, k − 1}; and both are shortest among such paths by cut and paste — a shorter i ⇝ k path with intermediates in {1..k − 1} spliced onto p₂ would give a shorter candidate for p. So w(p) = d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) + d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j). The two cases are exhaustive and each value is attained by a real path, so the minimum of the two is exactly d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j). Induction on k from the base d⁽⁰⁾ = W finishes it. Where the no-negative-cycle assumption was used: to make p simple, so that k occurs at most once on it and the splice argument does not produce a walk that repeats k. With a negative cycle present the recurrence still runs and still produces finite numbers; they are no longer shortest simple-path weights, but the diagonal test below detects the situation.

Why dropping the superscript is safe — the in-place argument

The recurrence as written reads d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾ and writes d⁽ᵏ⁾, suggesting two matrices, or V + 1 of them (Θ(V³) space) if every stage is kept. A single matrix updated in place is correct, and the reason is that the entries a stage-k update reads — row k and column k — are the entries it never changes. Consider d⁽ᵏ⁾(i, k): the recurrence gives min( d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k), d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) + d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, k) ) = d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k), because d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, k) = 0 when there is no negative cycle (it is 0 initially and no path from k to itself is shorter). Symmetrically d⁽ᵏ⁾(k, j) = d⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j). So during stage k, whether a cell (i, k) or (k, j) has "already been updated this stage" or not makes no difference — its value is the same either way — and every other cell (i, j) reads only those two, so it reads stage-(k − 1) values as the recurrence demands. Hence one matrix suffices: Θ(V²) space, and the loop order of i and j inside k is irrelevant (the order of k is not: it is the induction). The same argument covers the next matrix, since next[i][k] and next[k][j] are likewise untouched in stage k.

Worked in full: five vertices, all six matrices

Vertices 0 … 4 (the page numbers from 0; the recurrence's k then runs 0 … 4 and D⁽ᵏ⁺¹⁾ below is the matrix after vertex k has been allowed), eight directed edges: 0→1 (6), 0→3 (2), 1→2 (−3), 2→4 (2), 3→1 (−1), 3→4 (7), 4→0 (3), 2→3 (5). Two negative edges; the five simple cycles weigh 8, 18, 3, 12 and 1, so no negative cycle. Each cell that changed in a stage is bold.
