Reading the stages. Allowing vertex 0 as an intermediate (D⁽¹⁾) helps only paths through 0, and only vertex 4 has an edge into 0: 4→0→1 = 9, 4→0→3 = 5. Allowing 1 (D⁽²⁾) opens the cheap edge 1→2 (−3) to everyone who can reach 1: 0→1→2 = 3, 3→1→2 = −4, 4→0→1→2 = 6 — note the last one is built from D⁽¹⁾(4, 1) = 9, a path that itself uses intermediate 0, which is what the invariant permits. Allowing 2 (D⁽³⁾) opens 2→4 and 2→3: 0→…→2→4 = 5, 1→2→3 = 2, 1→2→4 = −1, 3→1→2→4 = −2. Allowing 3 (D⁽⁴⁾) is the big stage, six cells, because 3→1 (−1) is the entrance to the negative edge: 0→3→1 = 1 beats the direct 6, 0→3→1→2 = −2, 0→3→1→2→4 = 0, 2→3→1 = 4, 4→0→3→1 = 4, 4→0→3→1→2 = 1. Allowing 4 (D⁽⁵⁾) finally connects 1, 2 and 3 back to vertex 0 through 4→0: 1→2→4→0 = 2, 2→4→0 = 5, 3→1→2→4→0 = 1. 18 cells changed over the five stages out of 125 triples examined = V³; the diagonal stayed 0, so no negative cycle. The figure re-derives every matrix, and checks D against five Bellman-Ford runs (one per source, 120 relaxations in total with early exit) and against brute force over all 42 non-empty simple paths.

Reconstruction with the next matrix, and the negative-cycle test

Whenever D[i][j] improves to D[i][k] + D[k][j], the new best i → j path starts the way the best i → k path starts, so next[i][j] ← next[i][k]. Following next from i until j lists the path in forward order — 0 → 4: next[0][4] = 3, next[3][4] = 1, next[1][4] = 2, next[2][4] = 4, giving 0 → 3 → 1 → 2 → 4, whose edge weights re-sum to 2 − 1 − 3 + 2 = 0 = D[0][4]. The predecessor form π[i][j] ("the vertex before j on the best i → j path", updated as π[i][j] ← π[k][j]) is equivalent and lists the path backwards; both cost Θ(V²) extra space and O(1) per update. A negative simple cycle through i gives D[i][i] < 0: the first time the cycle's other vertices are all allowed as intermediates, the path i ⇝ i around it beats the initial 0 (the recurrence's induction is over simple paths with distinct intermediates, and a simple cycle through i is one). Adding the edge 4→1 (−2) to the example creates the cycle 1→2→4→1 of weight −3, and the routine's final diagonal reads 0, −3, −3, −2, −6: every vertex on the cycle (1, 2, 4) is negative, as the recurrence guarantees; 3, which is not on the cycle but reaches it (3→1) and is reached by it (2→3), happens to go negative too (D[3][4] + D[4][3] = −2 + 0 at k = 4), while 0, which likewise reaches the cycle (0→1) and is reached by it (4→0), stays at 0 (D[0][4] + D[4][0] = 0 + 3). Off-cycle diagonal entries carry no guarantee either way, so the test is "some D[i][i] < 0", read over the whole diagonal, and the off-diagonal entries are then meaningless. Under the < test the algorithm never crashes on such input; it must simply be told to check the diagonal, and the reconstruction must not be trusted (the next pointers can loop).

Transitive closure: the boolean version

Ask only "is there a path from i to j?" and every weight can be 1, so D[i][j] < ∞ is the answer — or, more economically, keep one bit per cell: t⁽⁰⁾(i, j) = 1 if i = j or (i, j) is an edge, and t⁽ᵏ⁾(i, j) = t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, j) ∨ (t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(i, k) ∧ t⁽ᵏ⁻¹⁾(k, j)). Same three loops, Θ(V³) bit operations, and rows fit in machine words so the inner loop runs 64 columns at a time. This is the reachability matrix the graph page uses as its independent check on strongly connected components (Graph Representations & Traversal, §22: u and v are in the same component iff t(u, v) ∧ t(v, u)); on the five-vertex example the closure is all ones — the graph is strongly connected, as the row of finite distances in D already shows. When only the closure is wanted on a sparse graph, V traversals cost O(V(V + E)) and beat the matrix; the matrix wins when the graph is dense or when the closure is needed as a bit-matrix for further algebra. Its (∨, ∧) structure is the boolean semiring of §12, and unlike (min, +) it does embed in ordinary integer multiplication, which is why transitive closure has fast-matrix-multiplication algorithms and all-pairs distances do not.

Floyd-Warshall stepped by k: row k and column k highlighted, the cells that improve flashed, one path reconstructed and re-summed each step allows one more vertex as an intermediate; the row and column it reads are tinted, and the cells the stage improved are amber; the readout counts the (k, i, j) triples and checks the matrix against V runs of Bellman-Ford and against brute force over all simple paths, then follows the next matrix for one pair and re-sums the path's edges

14 Johnson's reweighting for sparse graphs

Johnson's algorithm computes all-pairs shortest paths on a graph with negative edges (and no negative cycle) in O(V² log V + VE) — the cost of V Dijkstra runs — by first computing a potential h(v) for every vertex with one Bellman-Ford run from an added source, then reweighting every edge to ŵ(u, v) = w(u, v) + h(u) − h(v), which is non-negative and changes the weight of every u → v path by the same constant h(u) − h(v), so that Dijkstra on ŵ finds the same shortest paths and the original distances are recovered by subtracting the constant back. On a sparse graph — E near V rather than V² — this beats Floyd-Warshall's Θ(V³) by a factor approaching V / log V, and it is the reason "negative edges ⇒ can't use Dijkstra" is only half true: one Bellman-Ford run buys permission to use it V times.

the algorithm1. G′ = G plus a new vertex s* with an edge s* → v of weight 0 to every v (s* reaches everything; nothing reaches s*, so no new cycles) 2. BELLMAN-FORD(G′, w, s*): if it reports a negative cycle, stop — G has one too; else h(v) ← δ′(s*, v) for every v O(VE) 3. ŵ(u, v) ← w(u, v) + h(u) − h(v) for every edge O(E) 4. for every u: DIJKSTRA(G, ŵ, u) → δ̂(u, v); d(u, v) ← δ̂(u, v) − h(u) + h(v) V × O(V log V + E) total O(VE) + O(V² log V + VE) = O(V² log V + VE) with a Fibonacci heap; O(VE log V) with a binary heap — still ≪ V³ when E ≪ V²/log V the two lemmasNON-NEGATIVITY. h(v) = δ′(s*, v) satisfies the triangle inequality h(v) ≤ h(u) + w(u, v) for every edge (u, v) ⇒ ŵ(u, v) = w(u, v) + h(u) − h(v) ≥ 0 PRESERVATION. for any path p = ⟨v₀, v₁, …, vₖ⟩: ŵ(p) = Σ [ w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) + h(vᵢ₋₁) − h(vᵢ) ] = w(p) + h(v₀) − h(vₖ) — the h terms TELESCOPE the correction h(v₀) − h(vₖ) depends only on the endpoints, not on the path ⇒ the same path is shortest under w and under ŵ a cycle has v₀ = vₖ, so ŵ(c) = w(c): reweighting creates no negative cycle and hides none

Why this is exactly what Dijkstra needs, and no more

Dijkstra's correctness (§05) rests on one inequality: a path cannot get shorter by being extended, so the unsettled vertex with the smallest estimate is final. Any non-negative weighting gives that; the reweighting supplies one that additionally has the same shortest paths as w. The proof of the second lemma is the telescoping sum above and nothing else, and it shows more than is needed: every potential function h preserves shortest paths, whether or not it comes from Bellman-Ford. What Bellman-Ford's h adds is the first lemma, non-negativity, and there the triangle inequality is doing the work — δ′(s*, v) ≤ δ′(s*, u) + w(u, v) holds because "the shortest way to u, then the edge" is one particular way to reach v. The added source is what makes h finite everywhere: with s* adjacent to all vertices, no δ′ is ∞, and since every original vertex has a zero-weight edge from s*, every h(v) ≤ 0, with h(v) < 0 exactly for vertices reachable by a negative-weight path from somewhere. The recovery step is the preservation lemma read backwards: δ(u, v) = δ̂(u, v) − h(u) + h(v). And the negative-cycle report is inherited, since G′ has a negative cycle if and only if G does (the new vertex has no incoming edge, so it lies on no cycle).

Worked: the five-vertex graph of §13

Same graph — 0→1 (6), 0→3 (2), 1→2 (−3), 2→4 (2), 3→1 (−1), 3→4 (7), 4→0 (3), 2→3 (5). Bellman-Ford from s* on the 6-vertex, 13-arc G′ stops after 4 rounds (52 relaxations) with h = (0, −1, −4, 0, −2): h(1) = −1 via s* → 3 → 1, h(2) = −4 via s* → 3 → 1 → 2, h(4) = −2 via … → 2 → 4, and h(0) = h(3) = 0 because no negative-weight path reaches them. The reweighted edges:

edgewh(u)h(v)ŵ = w + h(u) − h(v)edgewh(u)h(v)ŵ
0→160−173→1−10−10
0→320023→470−29
1→2−3−1−404→03−201
2→42−4−202→35−401

All eight are non-negative, and three are exactly zero — the edges on shortest-path trees from s* always reweight to zero, because for them the triangle inequality is an equality. Now Dijkstra from vertex 0 on ŵ: settle 0 (0), then 3 (ŵ = 2), then 1 (2 + 0 = 2), then 2 (2 + 0 = 2), then 4 (2 + 0 = 2) — δ̂(0, ·) = (0, 2, 2, 2, 2), a run that never sees a negative number. Undo the reweighting with δ(0, v) = δ̂(0, v) − h(0) + h(v) = δ̂(0, v) + h(v): (0, 2 − 1, 2 − 4, 2 + 0, 2 − 2) = (0, 1, −2, 2, 0), which is row 0 of §13's D. The telescoping is visible on a single path: 0→3→1→2→4 has w = 2 − 1 − 3 + 2 = 0 and ŵ = 2 + 0 + 0 + 0 = 2, difference 2 = h(0) − h(4); the competing 0→1→2→4 has w = 5 and ŵ = 7, the same difference — so the comparison between them is unchanged, which is the whole point. Five Dijkstra runs in all: 29 heap pushes, 29 pops (4 stale), 40 relaxations = V·E, producing the full matrix, which the figure checks against Floyd-Warshall and against brute force over the 42 non-empty simple paths.

When it beats Floyd-Warshall, and when it does not

Compare O(V² log V + VE) with Θ(V³). With E = Θ(V) — road networks, meshes, most "real" sparse graphs — Johnson is O(V² log V) against V³, a factor of V / log V; at V = 10⁴ that is on the order of 10⁹ heap-driven operations (V² log₂ V ≈ 1.3 × 10⁹) against 10¹², the difference between seconds and hours. With a binary heap the bound is O(VE log V) = O(V² log V) on such graphs, the same story. At the other extreme, E = Θ(V²), Johnson is O(V³) with a Fibonacci heap and O(V³ log V) with a binary one, and Floyd-Warshall wins on the constant: its inner loop is an add, a compare and a store on a contiguous array, with no heap, no adjacency list and no pointer chasing, and it vectorises. The crossover is roughly where E log V ≈ V². Two further points. If the weights are already non-negative, h ≡ 0 and Johnson is just "run Dijkstra from every vertex" — step 2 costs one pass that changes nothing. And the potential idea outlives the algorithm: the same telescoping argument is what makes A*'s heuristic legitimate (§15), and potentials are the standard way to keep reduced costs non-negative in min-cost-flow and assignment algorithms, where every augmentation re-runs Dijkstra on reweighted residual edges.

Johnson stepped through its phases: h from Bellman-Ford, the reweighted edges, one Dijkstra run per source, the answer un-reweighted the graph shows w on each edge until the reweighting step, then ŵ (zero edges in teal); h(v) is written above each vertex once computed; each Dijkstra phase shows its settling order and d̂ then d below the vertices; the readout checks every ŵ ≥ 0 and the final matrix against Floyd-Warshall and brute force over all simple paths

15 A*: admissible and consistent heuristics, optimality, and what it saves

A* is Dijkstra's algorithm for a single target t, with the priority of a vertex changed from g(v) — its tentative distance from the source — to f(v) = g(v) + h(v), where h(v) is a cheap estimate of the distance still to go from v to t; it stops when t is popped. If h never overestimates (admissible: h(v) ≤ δ(v, t) for every v), the path returned is optimal. If moreover h obeys a triangle inequality along edges (consistent: h(u) ≤ w(u, v) + h(v)), then no vertex is ever expanded twice, and A* is literally Dijkstra on the reweighted graph ŵ(u, v) = w(u, v) + h(v) − h(u) — the same potential trick as Johnson's algorithm (§14) with the sign flipped, which is why it is correct. What the heuristic buys is fewer expansions: Dijkstra explores a ball around the source until it happens to swallow the target; A* explores an ellipse-like region stretched toward it, and a better estimate makes the region thinner.

the algorithm, and the two conditions on hg[s] ← 0, others ∞; open ← {s} keyed by f(v) = g[v] + h(v) loop: u ← pop the vertex of least f from open; if u = t: stop; mark u closed for each edge (u, v): if g[u] + w(u, v) < g[v]: g[v] ← g[u] + w(u, v); π[v] ← u; (re)insert v in open with key g[v] + h(v) — a closed v is REOPENED ADMISSIBLE h(v) ≤ δ(v, t) for every v (and h(t) = 0) ⇒ the path returned is a shortest path CONSISTENT h(u) ≤ w(u, v) + h(v) for every edge (u, v), h(t) = 0 ⇒ admissible (induction along any u → t path) and f never decreases along the expansion order ⇒ when v is popped, g[v] = δ(s, v): no vertex is reopened, each expanded at most once h ≡ 0: f = g, A* is Dijkstra (on a unit grid: BFS) h = δ(·, t) exactly: only the vertices on one shortest path are expanded the potential view — the same trick as §14ŵ(u, v) = w(u, v) + h(v) − h(u) ≥ 0 ⇔ h consistent (§14 had ŵ = w + h(u) − h(v): a Johnson potential is π(v) = −h(v)) along any s → v path the h terms telescope: ĝ(v) = g(v) + h(v) − h(s) = f(v) − h(s) so "pop the least f" is "pop the least ĝ": A* with a consistent h IS Dijkstra on ŵ, and Dijkstra's correctness proof (§05) applies verbatim since ŵ ≥ 0 shortest paths under ŵ are shortest paths under w (the correction h(v) − h(s) is the same for every s → v path), and δ(s, t) = δ̂(s, t) − h(t) + h(s) = δ̂(s, t) + h(s)

Optimality with an admissible heuristic, proved

Suppose A* (with reopening, as written above) pops t with g[t] > δ(s, t). Take a shortest path p = ⟨s = v₀, …, vₖ = t⟩ and let vᵢ be the first vertex on p that is not closed at that moment, or is closed with g[vᵢ] > δ(s, vᵢ); such a vertex exists because t itself qualifies. Its predecessor vᵢ₋₁ is closed with the exact value g[vᵢ₋₁] = δ(s, vᵢ₋₁) (by the choice of i; if i = 0 then vᵢ = s, which is open with the exact value g = 0 from the start, and the next sentence applies to it directly), and when it was expanded it relaxed the edge to vᵢ, so g[vᵢ] ≤ δ(s, vᵢ₋₁) + w(vᵢ₋₁, vᵢ) = δ(s, vᵢ), hence g[vᵢ] = δ(s, vᵢ), since g never drops below the true distance; and vᵢ is in the open set — it was inserted there at that relaxation, and by the choice of i it is not closed with an exact value, so it has not been popped since. Then f(vᵢ) = δ(s, vᵢ) + h(vᵢ) ≤ δ(s, vᵢ) + δ(vᵢ, t) = δ(s, t) < g[t] = f(t), using admissibility and the fact that p is shortest. So vᵢ has strictly smaller f than t and would have been popped first — contradiction. The argument needs reopening only to handle the case where an inadmissible-looking ordering closes a vertex with a stale g; consistency removes that case, and then the closed set can be final and the proof is simply Dijkstra's. Consistency implies admissibility by induction along any path from v to t: h(v) ≤ w(v, v′) + h(v′) ≤ … ≤ w(path) + h(t) = w(path), and taking the shortest path gives h(v) ≤ δ(v, t). The converse fails: an admissible but inconsistent h can force a vertex to be reopened, and without reopening the graph-search version can return a suboptimal path.

Heuristics on a grid, and what each one saves — measured

The figure's default instance is a 9 × 14 grid with 25 wall cells (101 free), 4-neighbour unit moves, start at (4, 1) and goal at (4, 12); the true shortest path, by BFS, has 21 steps. Four heuristics, with Δr, Δc the row and column offsets to the goal:

heuristich(v)admissible / consistentnodes expandedpath length
zero (= Dijkstra = BFS here)0yes / yes8621 ✓
Euclidean√(Δr² + Δc²)yes / yes6521 ✓
Manhattan|Δr| + |Δc|yes / yes5021 ✓
2 × Manhattan (weighted A*)2(|Δr| + |Δc|)no / no4925 ✗

Manhattan distance is the exact distance on an empty 4-connected grid, so it is admissible (walls only make the true distance longer) and consistent (one move changes |Δr| + |Δc| by at most 1, the move's cost). Euclidean distance is at most Manhattan, hence also admissible, and consistent by the triangle inequality of the plane — but it is a weaker bound on a grid, where diagonal shortcuts do not exist, and it expands more nodes: 65 against 50. The zero heuristic expands 86, most of the 101 free cells, because it grows the frontier in every direction until the goal happens to be reached. The ordering 86 ≥ 65 ≥ 50 is the general fact that a heuristic that dominates another (h₂ ≥ h₁ pointwise, both consistent) expands no more nodes, up to ties — every node A* expands with h₂ it would also expand with h₁, because f₂(v) < δ(s, t) implies f₁(v) < δ(s, t). Counts depend on the tie-break; the figure's routine breaks equal f toward larger g (deeper first, the usual choice, since it pushes toward the goal) and then first-inserted, and the figure re-measures everything at load and checks each path length against the BFS distance. Doubling Manhattan makes it inadmissible: the search is greedier still (49 expansions) and returns a 25-step path — it dives toward the goal, hits the wall column, and commits to a detour that a 21-step path avoids. Weighted A* with factor ε has the guarantee cost ≤ ε · δ(s, t), here 25 ≤ 42, and is used deliberately when a quick near-optimal answer is worth more than an optimal one; the figure's second instance, a grid with two long walls, shows that the inadmissible heuristic sometimes lands on the optimum anyway (43 expansions, 19 steps, optimal) — it is not reliably wrong, which is exactly what makes it dangerous when optimality is required.

Where the saving comes from, and its limits

A* with a consistent heuristic expands every reachable vertex with f(v) < δ(s, t), no vertex with f(v) > δ(s, t), and some of those with f(v) = δ(s, t), depending on ties. Under the potential view that set is "the vertices within reweighted distance δ̂(s, t) of s", the same ball Dijkstra would explore on ŵ — but the reweighting has shrunk the edges that point toward the goal (h drops by the edge's length, so ŵ ≈ 0) and stretched the edges that point away (ŵ ≈ 2w), so the ball is elongated toward t and squashed behind s. With the perfect heuristic h = δ(·, t) every edge on a shortest path has ŵ = 0, every other edge is positive, and the search walks straight to the goal. The limits: the heuristic must be cheap (it is evaluated once per relaxation, so anything costing more than O(1) or a table lookup eats the saving); it must be informed (on a maze whose shortest path winds away from the goal, Manhattan distance is admissible but nearly useless, and the expansion count approaches Dijkstra's); and memory is the open set, which for implicit graphs (Graph Representations & Traversal, §09 — puzzle states, robot configurations) is the binding constraint, motivating the memory-bounded variants. Bidirectional search is the other standard saving, independent of heuristics: run Dijkstra from s forward and from t backward on the reversed graph, stop when the two frontiers' minimum keys sum to at least the best meeting-point path found; each side explores roughly a ball of half the radius, which on a graph whose balls grow polynomially in the radius cuts the work by a large constant, and on graphs where they grow exponentially cuts it to the square root. Combining the two — bidirectional A* — is possible but delicate: the naïve stopping rule can miss the optimum, and the consistent-potential formulation is what makes the correct rule expressible.

A* on a grid with obstacles: expansions stepped, four heuristics, and the path checked against the true shortest walls are dark; closed (expanded) cells are blue, the open frontier amber-outlined, the cell being expanded amber; when the goal is popped the path is drawn in green; the readout measures the expansions for the chosen heuristic and for all four, and flags the inadmissible one when its path is longer than the BFS distance

16 Minimum spanning trees: the cut property and the cycle property

A minimum spanning tree (MST) of a connected, undirected, edge-weighted graph is a spanning tree — an acyclic subgraph that connects every vertex, which forces it to have exactly V − 1 edges — whose total edge weight is as small as any spanning tree's. Everything in this section and the four after it is for undirected graphs: on a directed graph the analogous object is the minimum spanning arborescence — a rooted out-tree reaching every vertex, each vertex except the root with exactly one incoming arc — and the cut and cycle properties below do not carry over to it as stated, so it needs a different algorithm (Chu-Liu/Edmonds: take each vertex's cheapest incoming arc, contract every cycle those arcs form, adjust the weights of the arcs entering the contracted vertex, and repeat), not Kruskal or Prim run on the arcs. An MST exists if and only if the graph is connected (a disconnected graph has a minimum spanning forest, one tree per component, and every algorithm below produces that instead), it is the cheapest way to make everything reachable from everything, and it is found by greedy algorithms — Kruskal's, Prim's and Borůvka's — whose correctness rests on a single lemma about cuts and its mirror image about cycles. This section states the problem, proves both lemmas, derives uniqueness under distinct weights, sets out the generic algorithm the three named ones instantiate, and previews the contrast the page keeps returning to: an MST is not a shortest-path tree, and the two answer different questions on the same graph. The shared instance is the six-vertex, nine-edge graph on which Greedy Algorithms, §20 first states the cut property; that page states it, this one proves it, checks it by brute force over all 55 spanning trees, and builds the algorithms.

definitionsG = (V, E) undirected, connected, w : E → ℝ. A SPANNING TREE T ⊆ E is acyclic and connects all of V; |T| = V − 1 (either of "acyclic" or "connected" plus the edge count gives the other). w(T) = ∑ over e ∈ T of w(e). A MINIMUM spanning tree minimises w(T) over all spanning trees. The SPANNING FOREST version (G disconnected) is the union of one MST per component. a CUT (S, V − S) is a partition of V, S ≠ ∅, S ≠ V. An edge CROSSES the cut if exactly one endpoint is in S. An edge set A RESPECTS the cut if no edge of A crosses it. a LIGHT edge across a cut is a crossing edge of minimum weight (there may be several). An edge is SAFE for A ⊆ some MST if A ∪ {e} is still contained in some MST. the CUT property — the greedy-choice lemma for spanning treesHYPOTHESIS A ⊆ T for some MST T; (S, V − S) a cut that A respects; e = (u, v) a light edge crossing it. CONCLUSION e is safe for A: A ∪ {e} ⊆ T′ for some MST T′. proof — the exchange argumentif e ∈ T there is nothing to do (T′ = T). Otherwise T ∪ {e} contains exactly one cycle: the unique tree path P from u to v in T, closed by e. P starts in S (at u) and ends outside S (at v), so it crosses the cut an odd number of times — at least once. Let e′ be any edge of P that crosses the cut. e′ ∉ A, because A respects the cut and e′ crosses it. Let T′ = T − {e′} ∪ {e}. T′ is a spanning tree: removing e′ from T splits it into two components, one containing u and one containing v (e′ lies on the u–v path); e re-joins them; |T′| = V − 1 and it is connected. w(T′) = w(T) − w(e′) + w(e) ≤ w(T), because e is light across the cut and e′ crosses the same cut, so w(e) ≤ w(e′). T is minimum, hence w(T′) = w(T) and T′ is also an MST; and A ∪ {e} ⊆ T′ since A ⊆ T, e′ ∉ A, e ∈ T′. ∎ COROLLARY at any stage of any algorithm whose A is contained in an MST, a light edge across ANY cut respected by A can be added without loss.

The cycle property — the same fact seen from the other side

The cut property says which edges must be in: the lightest way out of any set of vertices. Its dual says which edges must be out: the heaviest way around any cycle. Both are the same exchange argument with the roles of "cheap crossing edge" and "expensive cycle edge" swapped, and the two together are what every MST algorithm and every MST verification rests on.

the CYCLE propertyHYPOTHESIS C is a cycle in G and f is an edge of C with w(f) > w(e) for every other edge e of C (f is the STRICTLY heaviest edge on C). CONCLUSION f lies in no minimum spanning tree. proof: suppose f = (x, y) ∈ T for an MST T. Remove f: T splits into two components, x on one side and y on the other — a cut (S, V − S) that f crosses. C is a cycle through f, so C − {f} is an x–y path, and it must cross the cut somewhere — at an edge e ∈ C, e ≠ f, e ∉ T (T − {f} does not cross the cut). T′ = T − {f} ∪ {e} is a spanning tree (same argument as above) with w(T′) = w(T) − w(f) + w(e) < w(T), contradicting minimality. ∎ with TIES if f is merely a heaviest (not strictly) edge on C, the same exchange gives w(T′) = w(T): f may be in some MST, but SOME MST omits it. The strict version is what "distinct weights" buys. on the shared instance — every non-MST edge is the heaviest on the cycle it closes (the figure re-derives this)BD (5) closes A–B–D with AB (4), AD (2) BC (6) closes B–E–F–C with BE (3), EF (5), CF (1) CE (7) closes C–F–E with CF (1), EF (5) DE (8) closes D–A–B–E with AD (2), AB (4), BE (3) so the four edges outside the MST {AD, AB, BE, EF, CF} are exactly the four that the strict cycle property bars from every MST; the five that remain form a spanning tree, so that tree is the unique MST — a complete certificate of optimality without enumerating a single other tree. VERIFICATION in general: a spanning tree T is minimum ⇔ every non-tree edge e is at least as heavy as every edge on T's path between e's endpoints (⇒ by the exchange; ⇐ because Kruskal's run would then accept exactly T's edges under a suitable tie order).

Uniqueness: distinct weights are sufficient, ties may or may not break it

THEOREM if all edge weights are distinct, the minimum spanning tree is uniqueproof: let T₁ ≠ T₂ be two MSTs. Their symmetric difference is non-empty (both have V − 1 edges and they differ), so let e be the LIGHTEST edge in exactly one of them — say e ∈ T₁, e ∉ T₂ (weights distinct ⇒ "lightest" is unambiguous). remove e from T₁: a cut (S, V − S) that e crosses. T₂ connects the two sides, so some e′ ∈ T₂ crosses the cut, and e′ ∉ T₁ (T₁ − {e} has no crossing edge). e′ is in exactly one of the trees and is not e, so by the choice of e, w(e) < w(e′) (strict — distinct weights). T₂ − {e′} ∪ {e} is a spanning tree of weight w(T₂) − w(e′) + w(e) < w(T₂): T₂ was not minimum. Contradiction. ∎ CONVERSE FAILS the shared instance has BD = EF = 5 and still exactly one MST (55 trees enumerated, one of weight 15): the tie is between an edge that closes a cycle of lighter edges (BD) and one that does not (EF), so it never has to be broken. NON-UNIQUE EXAMPLE a triangle with all three edges of weight 1 has three spanning trees, all of weight 2, all minimum; a 4-cycle with equal weights has four. In general the number of MSTs can be exponential in V: a chain of k triangles joined at shared vertices, all weights equal, has 3ᵏ minimum spanning trees — each triangle independently drops one of its 3 edges (checked by brute force: 3, 9, 27, 81 trees for k = 1 … 4). WHAT TIES COST THE ALGORITHMS nothing for correctness — each algorithm returns SOME MST, with the tie broken by whatever order it examines edges — but two algorithms (or one algorithm from two roots) may return different edge SETS of equal weight (§19 checks this).

The generic algorithm, and why three different loops are the same algorithm

Every MST algorithm on this page is the following loop: keep an edge set A that is always a subset of some MST (initially empty — trivially contained in any MST), and repeatedly add a safe edge until A has V − 1 edges. The cut property supplies the safe edges: choose any cut that A respects, take a light edge across it. Since the invariant "A ⊆ some MST" holds at the start and is preserved by every step, it holds at the end, and an edge set of size V − 1 contained in a spanning tree is that spanning tree. The three named algorithms differ only in which cut they choose at each step, and therefore in the data structure that finds the light edge across it:

GENERIC-MST(G, w)A = ∅ while A is not a spanning tree: find an edge e that is SAFE for A ← the cut property: any cut A respects, any light edge across it A = A ∪ {e} return A INVARIANT before each iteration, A ⊆ some MST. TERMINATION |A| grows by one per step; after V − 1 steps A is a spanning tree (connected: else a cut it respects would still have a crossing edge). the three instantiationsKRUSKAL (§17) A is a forest. Cut = (the tree containing u, everything else) for the lightest edge (u, v) whose endpoints are in different trees — light across that cut by construction. Needs: sorted edges + union-find. PRIM (§19) A is one tree grown from a root r. Cut = (vertices of A, the rest). Light edge = the lightest edge leaving the tree — a priority queue keyed by "lightest edge into A" per outside vertex. BORŮVKA (§20) A is a forest; EVERY tree adds its lightest outgoing edge in the same round — each is light across (that tree, the rest), a cut A respects; needs distinct weights (or a consistent tie-break) so the added edges cannot close a cycle. all three add only safe edges ⇒ all three are correct by the invariant; they differ in cost profile, not in what they compute.

Worked: a cut on the shared instance, and the exchange made concrete

The instance: vertices A … F, edges AB 4, AD 2, BC 6, BD 5, BE 3, CE 7, CF 1, DE 8, EF 5. Take the cut S = {A, B, D}. The crossing edges are those with exactly one endpoint in S: BC (6), BE (3), DE (8) — AB, AD and BD have both endpoints inside and do not cross. The light edge is BE. The theorem promises that BE belongs to some MST, and since the MST is unique it belongs to the MST — the figure confirms this the honest way, by enumerating every 5-edge subset of the 9 edges (C(9, 5) = 126 of them), keeping the 55 that are acyclic, and finding one of minimum weight 15. The exchange is shown on the lightest spanning tree that avoids BE: T = {AB, AD, BC, CF, EF}, weight 18. Adding BE closes the cycle B–C–F–E–B; of its edges, only BC crosses the cut (CF and EF have both endpoints outside S), so e′ = BC, and T′ = T − {BC} ∪ {BE} has weight 18 − 6 + 3 = 15 — in this case the exchange lands on the MST itself. Change the cut to {A} and the light edge becomes AD (2); the tree avoiding it, {AB, BD, BE, CF, EF} (18), exchanges AB for AD and drops to 16 — a strict improvement that is not yet minimum, which is all the theorem claims. Every number in that paragraph is recomputed by the figure at load.

Minimum bottleneck spanning trees: every MST is one, and not conversely

definition and theoremthe BOTTLENECK of a spanning tree is its heaviest edge; a MINIMUM BOTTLENECK spanning tree (MBST) minimises that maximum. THEOREM every minimum spanning tree is a minimum bottleneck spanning tree. proof: let T be an MST with bottleneck edge f = (x, y), w(f) = b, and suppose some spanning tree T* has every edge lighter than b. Remove f from T: a cut (S, V − S). T* connects the two sides, so it has an edge e crossing the cut, with w(e) < b = w(f). Then T − {f} ∪ {e} is a spanning tree lighter than T — contradiction. ∎ CONVERSE FAILS on the shared instance the MST's bottleneck is 5 (edge EF), and {AD, BD, BE, CF, EF} also has bottleneck 5 but weight 16 — an MBST that is not an MST. (The figure's readout lists every tree sharing the minimum bottleneck.) USE "the path between u and v whose heaviest edge is lightest" (the MINIMAX path — widest path, safest route) is the MST path between u and v, for every pair at once: one MST answers all V² bottleneck queries.

MST is not a shortest-path tree

The two trees are easy to confuse because Prim's algorithm and Dijkstra's algorithm (§04) are the same loop with a different key, and both produce a spanning tree rooted at a start vertex. But they optimise different things: the MST minimises the sum of the tree's edges, the shortest-path tree minimises every root-to-vertex path length, and neither objective implies the other. On the shared instance, from root A, the shortest-path tree is {AB, AD, BE, BC, CF} with total weight 16, and the MST is {AB, AD, BE, EF, CF} with weight 15; the MST's path from A to C runs A–B–E–F–C for 13, while the true distance is 10. §20 draws both trees side by side and measures the largest such stretch. The general facts: a shortest-path tree's weight is at least the MST's (it is a spanning tree), an MST path can be nearly V − 1 times longer than the shortest path (a cycle of unit edges with one edge of weight 1 + ε: the MST drops that edge, so its path between that edge's endpoints has length V − 1 against a distance of 1 + ε), and with non-negative weights the one edge the two trees are guaranteed to share is the root's lightest incident edge when it is unique — it is light across the cut ({r}, V − {r}) and it is the shortest path to its other endpoint.

The cut property, proved by exhaustion — a cut, its crossing edges, the light edge, the exchange, and every spanning tree of the graph enumerated left: vertices in S filled, crossing edges dashed, the light edge amber; right: the lightest spanning tree that avoids the light edge, with the edge added (amber) and the crossing edge it displaces (red, dashed); the readout enumerates all 55 spanning trees, confirms the unique MST of weight 15, checks the cycle property on every non-MST edge and the bottleneck corollary

17 Kruskal's algorithm, stepped

Kruskal's algorithm builds a minimum spanning tree by sorting the edges by weight and walking them in that order, accepting an edge if and only if its endpoints are currently in different trees of the forest built so far — so that it never closes a cycle — and stopping when V − 1 edges have been accepted. The test "different trees?" is a pair of find operations on a union-find structure, and each acceptance is a union; with the two heuristics of §18 the whole walk costs O(E·α(V)), which is negligible beside the Θ(E log E) sort. Since E ≤ V², log E ≤ 2 log V, and the bound is O(E log V): the sort is the algorithm's cost. Correctness is the cut property applied once per accepted edge, and the section hand-works the shared instance edge by edge — order, verdict, reason, forest — with every count the figure reports measured by running the routine.

KRUSKAL(G, w)A = ∅ for each vertex v: makeSet(v) V make-sets sort E by weight, non-decreasing Θ(E log E) comparisons — the dominant term for each edge (u, v) in sorted order: E iterations, 2E finds in all if find(u) ≠ find(v): the endpoints are in different trees of the forest A A = A ∪ {(u, v)}; union(u, v) exactly V − 1 of these succeed on a connected graph return A (may stop early once |A| = V − 1; the figure runs the whole loop) INVARIANT A is a forest, and A ⊆ some MST (the second half is what the cut property gives) costsort Θ(E log E) = Θ(E log V) since log E ≤ log V² = 2 log V (and E ≥ V − 1 on a connected graph, so log E ≥ log(V − 1)) union-find V make-sets + 2E finds + (V − 1) unions = O((V + E)·α(V)) with union by rank + path compression (§18) — O(E log V) with rank alone, still dominated by the sort total O(E log V) time; Θ(V + E) space beyond the input (the sorted edge array, the parent array) already sorted or weights small integers (counting/radix sort, Sorting & Searching, §20–§21): the loop's O(E·α(V)) is the whole cost — near-linear correctness — one application of the cut property per accepted edgelet (u, v) be the edge about to be accepted, and C₁ the tree of A containing u. The cut is (C₁, V − C₁). A respects it: every edge of A lies inside one tree of the forest, and C₁ is a whole tree. (u, v) crosses it (v ∉ C₁, since find(u) ≠ find(v)) and is LIGHT across it: suppose a strictly lighter edge (x, y) crossed it, x ∈ C₁, y ∉ C₁. It was examined earlier. if it was ACCEPTED, x and y have been in one tree ever since (trees only merge) — so y ∈ C₁; if it was REJECTED, x and y were already connected then, hence still are — so y ∈ C₁. Either way y ∈ C₁: contradiction. so (u, v) is safe for A, A ∪ {(u, v)} ⊆ some MST, and by induction the final A — a spanning tree with V − 1 edges — IS an MST. ∎ REJECTED edges need no argument: a rejected edge closes a cycle whose other edges are all lighter-or-equal (they were accepted earlier), so it is a heaviest edge on that cycle — the cycle property says some MST omits it.

Worked in full: the shared instance

Edges sorted by weight, ties by input order (so BD precedes EF at weight 5): CF 1, AD 2, BE 3, AB 4, BD 5, EF 5, BC 6, CE 7, DE 8. The forest starts as six singletons. Each row below is one iteration; "components" lists the trees of the forest after the verdict, and the reason for a rejection names the path that already connects the endpoints.

#edge (w)find(u), find(v)verdict · reasonforest A aftercomponentsw(A)
1CF (1)C, F — differentaccept{CF}{A} {B} {CF} {D} {E}1
2AD (2)A, D — differentaccept{CF, AD}{AD} {B} {CF} {E}3
3BE (3)B, E — differentaccept{CF, AD, BE}{AD} {BE} {CF}6
4AB (4)A's tree, B's tree — differentaccept{CF, AD, BE, AB}{ABDE} {CF}10
5BD (5)same treereject · B–A–D already connects them; BD would close the cycle A–B–Dunchanged{ABDE} {CF}10
6EF (5)E's tree, F's tree — differentaccept — the fifth edge: a spanning tree{CF, AD, BE, AB, EF}{ABCDEF}15
7BC (6)same treereject · B–E–F–Cunchanged{ABCDEF}15
8CE (7)same treereject · C–F–Eunchanged{ABCDEF}15
9DE (8)same treereject · D–A–B–Eunchanged{ABCDEF}15

Five acceptances, four rejections, MST {CF, AD, BE, AB, EF} of weight 15. The figure runs this loop under a counter and reports what it measured: 18 finds (two per edge, 2E), 5 unions (V − 1), 18 comparisons in the merge sort of the nine edges (the sort's worst case on 9 keys is 9·⌈log₂ 9⌉ − 2^⌈log₂ 9⌉ + 1 = 21), and only 9 parent-pointer hops inside all eighteen finds with both heuristics on — 12 with neither, on a graph this small; the difference is invisible here and enormous in §18's thousand-element workload. The readout then enumerates all 55 spanning trees independently and confirms that 15 is the minimum, that it is unique, and that Kruskal's edge set is that tree. A loop that stops after the fifth acceptance would skip rows 7–9 and save six finds; the figure deliberately runs to the end so that the 2E count is visible.

Ties, stability and what "the" MST means

When several edges share a weight, the sorted order among them is whatever the sort produces, and Kruskal's output can depend on it: on a graph with several MSTs, different tie orders give different (equally minimum) trees. A stable sort (Sorting & Searching, §09) keeps tied edges in input order, which makes the output a deterministic function of the input list — the figure's merge sort breaks ties by edge index for exactly this reason, and it is why the worked table can say "BD precedes EF" rather than "one of them". On the shared instance the tie is harmless — BD is rejected whichever way it is ordered, because A–B–D is already connected by lighter edges — and the MST is unique despite the tie, as §16 proved by enumeration. When the sort is by a comparison that ignores ties, an implementation that wants reproducible output should add a secondary key.

Where the time goes, and when Kruskal is the right choice

The sort is Θ(E log E) and everything else is near-linear, so Kruskal's algorithm is the natural choice when the edges are already sorted, when they can be sorted in linear time (small integer weights: counting or radix sort), or when the graph is sparse (E close to V) so that E log E is small. On a dense graph (E = Θ(V²)) the sort costs Θ(V² log V), and Prim's algorithm with an array (§19) does Θ(V²); the crossover is the same density argument that decides adjacency lists against adjacency matrices (Graph Representations & Traversal, §08). Kruskal has two structural advantages the cost table does not show: it processes edges as a stream (no adjacency structure is needed — an edge list suffices), and it produces the minimum spanning forest of a disconnected graph with no special handling, since the loop simply runs out of edges with fewer than V − 1 accepted. A third: stopping the loop early — after k acceptances short of V − 1 — leaves a forest of V − k trees that is the single-linkage clustering into V − k clusters (§20). A fourth, on the analysis side: it is the matroid greedy algorithm of Greedy Algorithms, §18 run on the graphic matroid with increasing weights, which is a second, independent proof of its correctness.

Kruskal on the shared instance — the sorted edge list walked in order, each edge accepted (green) or rejected (red, struck), the forest growing vertices are coloured by the tree they belong to; the edge under examination is amber; the readout reports the measured finds, unions, sort comparisons and parent-pointer hops, and enumerates all 55 spanning trees to confirm the weight and uniqueness

18 Union-find built: union by rank, path compression, and the inverse-Ackermann bound

Union-find (the disjoint-set forest) maintains a partition of n elements into sets under two operations — find(x), which returns a canonical representative of the set containing x, and union(x, y), which merges the sets of x and y — by storing each set as a rooted tree in a single parent array: find climbs parent pointers to the root, and union links one root under the other. Naively, a chain of unions can make a tree of height n − 1 and a find cost Θ(n). Two heuristics fix this. Union by rank links the shorter tree under the taller and bounds every height by ⌊log₂ n⌋, with proof. Path compression makes every find re-point the nodes it visited straight at the root, so no path is climbed twice. Together they give the celebrated bound: any sequence of m operations on n elements costs O(m·α(n)), where α is the inverse of an Ackermann-type function and is at most 4 for every n that will ever be written down — not constant in theory, indistinguishable from constant in practice. This section builds the structure that Graph Representations & Traversal, §27 named and deferred here, gives the proofs it can give in full, states the ones it cannot with their exact hypotheses, and measures every claim.

the structure — three arrays of n entries, or twoparent[x] the parent of x in its tree; parent[x] = x ⇔ x is a ROOT = the representative of its set rank[x] an upper bound on the height of the subtree at x (exact height until path compression starts shortening paths; never decreases; only roots' ranks change) makeSet(x): parent[x] = x; rank[x] = 0 Θ(1) find(x): r = x; while parent[r] ≠ r: r = parent[r] ← climb; the cost of a find is the number of hops (path compression, second pass) while parent[x] ≠ r: next = parent[x]; parent[x] = r; x = next return r union(x, y): rx = find(x); ry = find(y); if rx = ry: return ← already one set (union by rank) if rank[rx] > rank[ry]: parent[ry] = rx else: parent[rx] = ry; if rank[rx] = rank[ry]: rank[ry] += 1 ← the only time a rank grows (naive linking) parent[rx] = ry — x's root under y's root, whatever their sizes UNION BY SIZE is the same idea with size[root] = number of nodes: link the smaller under the larger; the same O(log n) height bound with the same proof shape. what a find returns is a NAME for the set that is stable until the next union touching it: "same set?" ⇔ find(u) = find(v).

Why the naive version is Θ(n) per find, and why the fix is two lines

With parent[rx] = ry unconditionally, the sequence union(0, 1), union(1, 2), …, union(n − 2, n − 1) builds a chain: after union(0, 1) the root of 0 is 1; union(1, 2) links that root under 2; and so on, until 0 is at depth n − 1 and find(0) costs n − 1 hops — on the figure's twelve elements, 11. Repeat that find m times and the total is Θ(mn). Nothing in the structure is wrong; the linking direction is simply arbitrary, and an adversary picks the bad direction every time. The fix is to make the direction informed — link the smaller (by rank or by size) under the larger — and, independently, to make each find leave the tree flatter than it found it. The figure's "chain" preset shows the naive chain of height 11; switching on either heuristic alone collapses it to height 1, for different reasons (rank never lets it form; compression flattens it on the first find(0), which costs 11 hops once and 1 thereafter).

Union by rank alone: height ≤ ⌊log₂ n⌋, proved

LEMMA with union by rank (and no compression) a root of rank r has at least 2ʳ nodes in its treeproof by induction on the operations. Initially every root has rank 0 and 1 = 2⁰ node. a LINK of rx under ry with rank[rx] < rank[ry]: ry's rank is unchanged and its tree only grew — the claim holds. a LINK with rank[rx] = rank[ry] = r: ry's rank becomes r + 1 and its tree has ≥ 2ʳ + 2ʳ = 2ʳ⁺¹ nodes — the claim holds. non-roots never change rank, and (without compression) a root's rank IS its height: the height only changes at a tie link, where it becomes r + 1. ∎ COROLLARY every rank is ≤ ⌊log₂ n⌋ (a rank-r tree needs 2ʳ ≤ n nodes); every find costs ≤ ⌊log₂ n⌋ + 1 hops; m operations cost O(m log n) — worst case, not amortised. ALSO the number of nodes of rank EXACTLY r is at most n / 2ʳ, hence of rank > k at most n/2ᵏ⁺¹ + n/2ᵏ⁺² + … ≤ n / 2ᵏ. (charge each rank-r node the ≥ 2ʳ nodes in its tree at the moment it reached rank r; these charged sets are disjoint, because a node that once sat below a rank-r ancestor only ever acquires ancestors of higher rank — ranks strictly increase up every path, and compression re-points only upwards.) This counting fact survives path compression (ranks never change once a node is a non-root) and is what the log* and α analyses lean on. MEASURED the figure's random workload (n = 1,000) ends with height 4 under rank alone, well under ⌊log₂ 1000⌋ = 9; the pairs preset on n = 12 ends at height 3 ≤ ⌊log₂ 12⌋ = 3.

Path compression alone, and the bound that is stated rather than proved here

Path compression on its own — naive linking, but every find re-points the path at the root — has a worse worst case per operation (a chain can still be built and its first traversal costs Θ(n)) and a much better amortised one: any m ≥ n operations cost Θ(m·log1 + m/n n). The bound is tight — both an upper bound and an adversarial sequence achieving it are known — and for m = Θ(n) it is Θ(n log n), while for m ≥ n^{1+ε} it collapses to O(m). The proof is a potential-function argument in the same family as the one sketched below and is not reproduced here; the fact worth carrying is that compression alone is already near-linear whenever the finds outnumber the elements substantially, which is Kruskal's situation (2E finds on V elements). The figure measures it: on the random workload, compression alone averages 2.65 hops per operation against 104.47 with nothing, and 1.28 with both heuristics.

Both heuristics: the Ackermann-type function, its inverse, and the table

the fast-growing function Aₖ(j), for integers k ≥ 0 and j ≥ 1A₀(j) = j + 1 Aₖ(j) = Aₖ₋₁^{(j+1)}(j) for k ≥ 1 — the function Aₖ₋₁ applied j + 1 times, starting from j (functional iteration: f^{(0)}(j) = j, f^{(i)}(j) = f(f^{(i−1)}(j))) closed forms for the first levels: A₁(j) = 2j + 1 (j + 1 successive "+1" steps starting from j) A₂(j) = 2^{j+1}·(j + 1) − 1 (j + 1 successive "2x + 1" steps: each maps x ↦ 2x + 1, so after i steps x = 2ⁱ(j + 1) − 1) values at j = 1: A₀(1) = 2 A₁(1) = 3 A₂(1) = 7 A₃(1) = A₂(A₂(1)) = A₂(7) = 2⁸·8 − 1 = 2047 A₄(1) = A₃(A₃(1)) = A₃(2047) ≥ A₂(2047) = 2²⁰⁴⁸·2048 − 1 ≫ 10⁸⁰ (the figure's routine computes A₀ … A₃ exactly; A₄(1) overflows double precision — it exceeds 10⁶⁰⁰) the slow-growing inverseα(n) = min { k : Aₖ(1) ≥ n } — the lowest level whose value at 1 already reaches n the table — every n that fits in a computer, or in the universeα(n) = 0 for 0 ≤ n ≤ 2 α(n) = 1 for n = 3 α(n) = 2 for 4 ≤ n ≤ 7 α(n) = 3 for 8 ≤ n ≤ 2047 α(n) = 4 for 2048 ≤ n ≤ A₄(1) — and A₄(1) exceeds 10⁸⁰, the estimated number of atoms in the observable universe so α(n) ≤ 4 for every input that will ever exist, and α is unbounded only in the sense that mathematics does not stop at 10⁸⁰. THEOREM (union by rank + path compression)any sequence of m makeSet, union and find operations, n of them makeSet, on a forest that starts empty, costs O(m·α(n)) time in total. BOTH heuristics are required for this bound: rank alone is Θ(m log n) in the worst case, compression alone is Θ(m log_{1+m/n} n); neither alone is O(m·α(n)). it is AMORTISED: a single find may still cost Θ(log n) hops (rank keeps it under ⌊log₂ n⌋ + 1); it is the average over the sequence that is ≤ c·α(n). and it is TIGHT: sequences forcing Ω(m·α(m, n)) hops are known for this structure, and a matching lower bound holds for a broad class of pointer-based algorithms — the inverse-Ackermann term is intrinsic, not an artefact of the proof.

Why it is not constant, and why it is as good as constant — the shape of the proof

The proof is a potential-function argument (Algorithm Analysis, §19) and its idea can be understood without its bookkeeping. Ranks strictly increase along any path to a root, and a node's rank is fixed forever once it stops being a root, while its parent's rank only ever increases — every compression re-points a node at a strictly higher-ranked ancestor. So each non-root node x sits at some "level": the largest k such that rank[parent[x]] ≥ Ak(rank[x]). Because ranks are at most n − 1 and Aα(n)(1) ≥ n, every level is between 0 and α(n) − 1 — there are only α(n) possible levels. A node's level can only rise (its parent's rank only rises), and within a level a second counter — how many times Ak must be iterated on rank[x] to exceed the parent's rank — also only rises, up to rank[x] times before the level itself must tick over. Give each non-root node a potential of roughly (α(n) − level)·rank − iterations, and each root a potential of α(n)·rank.

Now take a find that follows a path of s hops. Charge the hops to the nodes on the path. A node whose next-higher-level ancestor is also on the path gets its potential reduced by the compression (after re-pointing at the root, its parent's rank jumped enough to advance its counter or its level), so that hop is paid by released potential — and nodes of this kind can be all but a few. The hops that are not paid by potential are the last node at each level on the path plus the root and its child: at most α(n) + 2 of them. So the amortised cost of a find is O(α(n)); a link costs O(α(n)) because at most one node's potential rises, by at most α(n); and a makeSet is O(1). Sum over the sequence: O(m·α(n)). The reason the bound is not O(1) is the count of distinct levels — the potential can be released only α(n) times "per unit of rank", and that is what the unpaid hops per find add up to. The reason it is as good as constant is the table above: the levels are so coarse that four of them cover every rank up to 10⁸⁰.

the simpler bound one CAN prove in a paragraph — O(m log* n), by the same idea with cruder levelslog* n = the number of times log₂ must be applied to n to bring it to ≤ 1: log* 2 = 1, log* 4 = 2, log* 16 = 3, log* 65536 = 4, log* 2⁶⁵⁵³⁶ = 5 (and log* 1000 = 4, log* 10⁸⁰ = 5) group the ranks into blocks {1}, {2}, {3, 4}, {5 … 16}, {17 … 65536}, {65537 … 2⁶⁵⁵³⁶}, … — block after {k+1 … 2ᵏ} is {2ᵏ+1 … 2^{2ᵏ}}; the number of blocks up to rank log₂ n is log* n. ACCOUNTING give each node 2ᵏ credits when it stops being a root with a rank in block {k+1 … 2ᵏ}. Nodes of rank > k number ≤ n/2ᵏ (the counting fact above), so a block costs ≤ n credits in total, and all blocks ≤ n·log* n. a FIND of s hops: nodes on the path whose parent is in a HIGHER block number at most log* n (blocks increase along the path) — charge those hops to the find: O(log* n). a node whose parent is in the SAME block pays one credit — and after compression its parent's rank strictly increased, which can happen at most 2ᵏ times before the parent leaves the block; so its credits suffice. TOTAL m finds cost O(m·log* n) + the n·log* n credits = O((m + n)·log* n). ∎ (unions are two finds plus O(1)) log* n ≤ 5 for every n < 2⁶⁵⁵³⁶; the α bound is the same argument with the blocks replaced by the levels of Aₖ, which are coarser still.

One-pass variants, and what the structure cannot do

Two-pass compression (climb to the root, then re-point) is the cleanest statement but does the walk twice. Path halving does one pass — at each node x on the way up, set parent[x] = parent[parent[x]] and step to that grandparent — so every other node on the path is re-pointed two levels up. Path splitting also does one pass, re-pointing every node at its grandparent and stepping to the old parent. Both keep the O(m·α(n)) bound with union by rank (the same proof goes through with a constant-factor change), both touch each path node once, and both are what production code tends to use; on the figure's random workload with rank, halving averages 1.16 hops per operation, splitting 1.29, full compression 1.28 — differences within the noise of one seed, all a small constant. What union-find cannot do is delete: a union cannot be undone, so "are u and v still connected after this edge is removed?" is outside its contract — the offline trick (process deletions backwards as insertions) or a fully dynamic connectivity structure is needed, as Graph Representations & Traversal, §27 says. It is also symmetric — nothing about direction, nothing about strong components — and it knows nothing about distances; it answers one question, "same set?", and answers it in effectively constant time.

Measured: the four combinations on the same sequences

The figure runs one preset sequence on twelve elements under whichever heuristics are switched on, draws the forest after every operation, and then re-runs the same sequence under all four combinations and tabulates total hops, hops per find, the worst single find and the final height. Beyond the toy, it measures two thousand-element workloads at load. On a random workload of 5,000 operations (about half unions of random pairs, half finds of random elements), naive linking averages about 104 hops per operation and ends with a tree of height around 197 — because linking a large tree under a singleton adds one to the depth of every node in it, and random pairs do that constantly — while rank alone averages 2.27, compression alone 2.65, and both together 1.28, a ratio of about 80× between nothing and both. On the adversarial chain (999 sequential unions on 1,000 elements, then 4,001 finds of the deepest element), naive linking averages 799 hops per operation; rank alone, compression alone and both all average 1.00 — compression alone pays 999 hops once and 1 thereafter, which is the amortisation in a single number. These are measurements, re-taken by the page at every load; the prose quotes them only because the routine produced them.

Union-find on twelve elements — the forest after every operation of a preset sequence, with union by rank and path compression as toggles, and the cost of every find measured roots are filled; the elements an operation touched are amber; with rank on, each root shows its rank; the readout tabulates hops, hops per find, worst find and final height under all four heuristic combinations on the same sequence, then on a random and an adversarial 1,000-element workload

19 Prim's algorithm, stepped — Dijkstra's skeleton with a different key

Prim's algorithm grows a minimum spanning tree from a single root, one vertex at a time: every vertex outside the tree carries a key, key[v] = the weight of the lightest edge joining v to the tree so far (∞ if none), and each step extracts the outside vertex of minimum key, adds it and its key edge to the tree, and lowers the keys of its neighbours that are still outside. The loop is Dijkstra's loop (§04) with one substitution: where Dijkstra relaxes with d[u] + w(u, v), Prim relaxes with w(u, v) alone. Everything else — the min-priority queue, extract-min then decrease-key, the settled set that only grows — is identical, and so is the cost: O(E log V) with a binary heap, Θ(V²) with a plain array, which wins on dense graphs. Correctness is the cut property applied to the cut (tree, everything else) at every extraction. The section states the substitution exactly, hand-works the shared instance from a chosen root with the full key/π table per extraction, and measures the extractions and decrease-keys.

PRIM(G, w, r)for each vertex v: key[v] = ∞; π[v] = NIL π[v] = the tree endpoint of v's lightest known edge into the tree key[r] = 0 Q = min-priority queue of all vertices keyed by key[·] V inserts (or one Θ(V) build-heap) while Q is not empty: u = extractMin(Q) V extractions; u joins the tree via the edge (π[u], u) — for u ≠ r for each edge (u, v): every edge is examined twice over the run: 2E scans if v ∈ Q and w(u, v) < key[v]: ← THE line that differs from Dijkstra π[v] = u; key[v] = w(u, v); decreaseKey(Q, v) at most E decrease-keys: an edge can lower a key only when its FIRST endpoint is extracted; by the second scan the other endpoint is in the tree return { (π[v], v) : v ≠ r } INVARIANT the vertices extracted so far form a tree A ⊆ some MST; for every v still in Q, key[v] = min { w(u, v) : u already extracted } (∞ if no such edge) and π[v] attains it. the substitution, stated preciselyDIJKSTRA relax(u, v): if d[u] + w(u, v) < d[v]: d[v] = d[u] + w(u, v); π[v] = u key = length of the best known PATH from the source PRIM relax(u, v): if w(u, v) < key[v]: key[v] = w(u, v); π[v] = u key = weight of the best known single EDGE from the tree same queue, same extract-min-then-relax-neighbours loop, same settled-set-only-grows structure, same π-tree output, same cost profile. different question: Dijkstra's tree minimises every root-to-vertex distance; Prim's minimises the sum of the tree's edges. §16 and §20 show the trees differ on the shared instance. one hypothesis fewer: Prim needs NO sign condition on weights (negative weights are fine — the cut property never adds distances); Dijkstra needs w ≥ 0 (§05). cost, by queuebinary heap V extract-mins × O(log V) + ≤ E decrease-keys × O(log V) + 2E scans × Θ(1) = O((V + E) log V) = O(E log V) on a connected graph (E ≥ V − 1) array (no heap) V min-searches × Θ(V) + 2E scans × Θ(1) = Θ(V² + E) = Θ(V²) — better than the heap when E = Θ(V²): V² < V² log V Fibonacci heap V × O(log V) extract-mins + ≤ E × O(1) amortised decrease-keys + 2E scans = O(E + V log V) — the same trade-off as for Dijkstra (§06), and the same caveat that the constants are large lazy deletion push a fresh (w, v) entry instead of decreasing: O(E log E) = O(E log V), simpler code, a heap of up to E entries space Θ(V) for key, π and the heap positions — plus the graph.

Correctness: the cut is always (tree, rest)

Let A be the set of vertices extracted so far, and suppose by induction that the edges (π[v], v) for v ∈ A − {r} form a tree contained in some MST — true at the start, when A = {r} and the edge set is empty. The cut (A, V − A) is respected by the tree (all its edges have both endpoints in A). The invariant on keys says that for each outside v, key[v] is the lightest edge from A to v; so the minimum key over all outside vertices is the lightest edge crossing the cut — a light edge — and the vertex u with that key, together with (π[u], u), is exactly what the cut property says is safe. Adding it preserves "tree ⊆ some MST". The key invariant itself is preserved by the relaxation loop: after u joins, the only crossing edges that are new are those from u, and each is compared against the outside endpoint's key. The proof needs the graph to be connected for the loop to produce a spanning tree; on a disconnected graph the queue eventually returns a vertex with key ∞, and the routine stops there with the MST of the root's component (a second run from an unvisited vertex gives the next component — the figure's routine breaks on ∞ for this reason). The proof does not need non-negative weights: nothing is ever summed.

Worked in full: the shared instance from root A

Same graph as §16–§17; root A. The decrease-keys performed during that extraction's relaxation loop are listed with their cause.

Extraction order A D B E F C; tree {AD, AB, BE, EF, CF}, weight 15 — the same edge set Kruskal produced in §17, in a different order. Notice the extraction order is not Dijkstra's: Dijkstra from A settles A (0), D (2), B (4), E (7), C (10), F (11), and takes BC rather than EF (§20 measures this). Measured by the figure: 6 extract-mins (= V), 7 decrease-keys — 5 of them the first finite key a vertex receives (the ∞ → w cases, which a queue built lazily would count as inserts) and 2 genuine improvements (E: 8 → 3, C: 6 → 1) — 18 adjacency scans (= 2E), and 8 sift swaps inside the heap. The array version from the same root scans 6 + 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 21 = V(V + 1)/2 candidates across its six min-searches and returns the same weight. Change the root: from C the order is C F E B A D with 9 decrease-keys (4 genuine), and the edge set is identical — as it must be, since the MST is unique; on a graph with tied MSTs the sets could differ between roots, and between Prim and Kruskal, with equal weight, which the readout checks and reports.

Prim or Kruskal?

The asymptotic bounds match at O(E log V) with a binary heap, so the choice is about the graph and the representation. Prim wants an adjacency structure (it walks u's neighbours) and pays per edge scan into the heap; Kruskal wants an edge list and pays once for the sort. On dense graphs Prim with an array is Θ(V²) and beats any sort-based method; on sparse graphs the two are close, and Kruskal wins outright when the edges arrive sorted or integer-weighted. Prim's tree is connected at every moment — useful when the algorithm is stopped early (a partial MST around a root) or when the graph is implicit and edges are generated on demand from a vertex; Kruskal's partial output is a forest — useful for clustering. In the vocabulary of Heaps & Priority Queues, §23, Prim is the second of the two graph algorithms whose inner loop is a decrease-key, and the discussion there of lazy versus indexed heaps applies unchanged. Borůvka (§20) is the third option, and the one that parallelises.

Prim on the shared instance from a chosen root — keys on the frontier, one extract-min per step, the tree growing, and the key/π table after every extraction in-tree vertices are filled; each frontier vertex shows its current key and its lightest known edge into the tree is dashed; the extracted vertex and its edge are amber; the readout reports the measured extractions, decrease-keys and scans, runs the array version, and compares the weight with Kruskal's and with brute force over all 55 trees

20 Borůvka, bottleneck and second-best trees, MST versus shortest-path tree, MST clustering

Borůvka's algorithm is the third instantiation of the generic MST loop: in each round every tree of the current forest selects its lightest outgoing edge, and all the selected edges are added at once — so the number of trees at least halves per round and the whole forest is one tree after at most ⌈log₂ V⌉ rounds, each costing O(E). It is the oldest of the three algorithms, the one whose rounds are naturally parallel, and the seed of the expected-linear-time randomised MST algorithm. Around it this section gathers the questions a minimum spanning tree answers once it exists: how the MST differs from the shortest-path tree on the very same graph (measured: the two trees from root A differ by one edge and two vertices' MST paths are longer — C 13 against 10, F 12 against 11 — and the trees differ by one edge each way), the second-best spanning tree and why a single edge swap finds it, the maximum spanning tree by negation, the bottleneck tree already proved in §16, single-linkage clustering as "cut the k − 1 heaviest MST edges", and the Steiner tree as the NP-hard cousin that looks like an MST and is not one.

BORŮVKA(G, w) — assumes distinct weights, or a consistent tie-breakA = ∅; each vertex is its own component while there is more than one component: ≤ ⌈log₂ V⌉ rounds for each component C: e(C) = the lightest edge with exactly one endpoint in C one pass over E, tracking the best edge per component: O(E) with union-find labels, or O(E) after relabelling A = A ∪ { e(C) : all components C }; merge the components each added edge joins return A INVARIANT A ⊆ some MST, and A is a forest. WHY THE ROUNDS HALVE every component chooses an edge, every chosen edge merges ≥ 2 components, and an edge chosen by two components merges those two: k components → at most ⌊k/2⌋ afterwards. COST ≤ ⌈log₂ V⌉ rounds × O(E) = O(E log V) — the same as Kruskal and heap-Prim, with no sort and no heap, and with a round's work trivially parallel over components/edges. why distinct weights (or a consistent tie-break) are NEEDED — the cycle that ties can closea triangle x, y, z with all three edges of weight 1: x may pick xy, y may pick yz, z may pick zx — three edges added at once, a cycle, and A is not a forest. with DISTINCT weights the chosen edges cannot form a cycle: on any cycle of chosen edges, the heaviest edge f = (p, q) was chosen by p's or q's component — but that component also touches a lighter edge of the cycle, which it would have preferred. CONSISTENT TIE-BREAK compare (w(e), index(e)) lexicographically — the figure's routine does — and every comparison behaves as if weights were distinct; then the argument above goes through verbatim. CORRECTNESS given that: with distinct weights the light edge across a cut is in EVERY MST (an MST omitting it would be made strictly lighter by the §16 exchange), and the MST is unique. e(C) is the unique light edge across (C, V − C), so every chosen edge is an edge of THE minimum spanning tree — and adding any number of them at once keeps A ⊆ that tree. under a consistent tie-break the MST for the lexicographic order (w, index) is an MST for w (it has the same weight as every tree that is minimum for w), so the argument transfers.
