Two rounds against the bound ⌈log₂ 6⌉ = 3: six components became three, then one. The tree is {AD, BE, CF, AB, EF}, weight 15, the same set as Kruskal's and Prim's (unique MST). The figure's routine measures 2 × 9 = 18 edge scans (each round scans every edge once) and 9 union calls (one per component per round, of which 5 actually link). The tie between BD and EF at weight 5 never matters here — no component has both as its lightest outgoing edge — but the routine breaks ties by edge index regardless, because on another instance it would.

The randomised linear-time algorithm, as a pointer

Two or three Borůvka rounds cut the vertex count by a factor of 4 or 8 while keeping the edge count — a graph with V/8 vertices is much easier, but the edges have not gone away. The expected-linear-time algorithm adds a sampling step: take a random half of the edges, recursively find their minimum spanning forest F, and discard every unsampled edge that is the heaviest on the cycle it closes with F (the cycle property, checked in linear time by a tree-path-maximum verification routine); a lemma shows only about 2V edges survive in expectation. Recurse on the survivors. The recurrence resolves to O(V + E) expected time. It is a beautiful construction and, on the machines and graphs one actually meets, slower than the three deterministic algorithms above; it belongs here as the answer to "is the sort in Kruskal or the heap in Prim really necessary?" — not in the comparison model deterministically (that is open), but in expectation with randomness, no.

MST versus shortest-path tree, measured

the same graph, root A — two trees, two objectivesMST {AB, AD, BE, EF, CF} total weight 15 minimises ∑ w(e) over the tree SHORTEST-PATH TREE {AB, AD, BE, BC, CF} total weight 16 minimises δ(A, v) for every v simultaneously — the tree is a by-product of Dijkstra's π pointers the symmetric difference is one edge each way: the MST has EF (5) where the SPT has BC (6). per vertex — path length from A along the MST / the true distance δ(A, v): B 4 / 4 D 2 / 2 E 7 / 7 F 12 / 11 C 13 / 10 ← the largest excess: A–B–E–F–C on the MST (13) against A–B–C (10); stretch 1.30 any spanning tree weighs ≥ the MST (the MST is minimum over all spanning trees, and the SPT is one), so w(SPT) ≥ w(MST) is a theorem, not a coincidence; 16 ≥ 15. from root C the two trees still differ: the largest EXCESS is at D — 15 along the MST against δ = 11, 4 units — while the largest stretch RATIO is at B, 9 against 6 (1.50) (the figure's root select shows every case).

The two objectives pull in different directions for a structural reason. The SPT is rooted: it optimises distances from one vertex and would be a different tree from another root, and it may pay for a heavy edge if that edge shortens a path. The MST is rootless: it is the same tree whoever asks, and it never takes a heavy edge if lighter ones connect the same vertices, however long the detour becomes. Applications choose accordingly: a network that must be cheap to build wants an MST; one that must be fast to traverse from a hub wants an SPT; one that wants both settles for a spanner — a subgraph whose paths are at most t times the shortest, with a few more edges than a tree. The overlay in the figure draws the two trees side by side from any root and reports the total weights and the vertex whose MST path is longest relative to its distance.

Second-best minimum spanning tree: one swap away

definition and the swap lemmaa SECOND-BEST MST is a spanning tree T₂ ≠ T (T an MST) of minimum weight among all spanning trees other than T. It need not be unique; with tied weights it may weigh the same as T. LEMMA some second-best MST differs from T in exactly one edge: T₂ = T − {f} ∪ {e} with e ∉ T, f on T's path between e's endpoints. proof: let T₂ be a second-best MST that differs from T in as few edges as possible, and suppose |T − T₂| ≥ 2. Pick e ∈ T − T₂; removing e from T defines a cut, and e is LIGHT across it (else T − {e} plus a lighter crossing edge would beat T). T₂ connects the two sides, so some e′ ∈ T₂ crosses the cut, and e′ ∉ T. T₂′ = T₂ − {e′} ∪ {e} is a spanning tree with w(T₂′) ≤ w(T₂), and T₂′ ≠ T (it still lacks another edge of T). T₂ is minimum among trees ≠ T, so w(T₂′) = w(T₂): T₂′ is also second-best, and differs from T in one edge fewer — contradicting the choice of T₂. So |T − T₂| = 1: T₂ = T − {f} ∪ {e}. for T₂ to be a tree, f must lie on T's path between e's endpoints; to make it lightest, f is the heaviest such edge. ∎ ALGORITHM for every non-tree edge e = (u, v): f(e) = the HEAVIEST edge on the tree path u ⇝ v; candidate weight w(T) − w(f(e)) + w(e). Take the minimum. COST the path maxima max[u, v] for all pairs by one traversal from each vertex: Θ(V²) — then Θ(E) for the candidates: O(V²) in all. Or with a tree-path-maximum structure (binary lifting / LCA with maxima): O(E log V). BRUTE FORCE on the instance (55 trees): weights sorted 15, 16, 16, 17, 17, 18, 18, 18, … second-best weight 16, attained by two trees — the swap method finds it. the four candidates on the shared instance (T = {AB, AD, BE, EF, CF}, w = 15)add BD (5): tree path B–A–D = {AB 4, AD 2}, heaviest AB (4) → 15 − 4 + 5 = 16 add BC (6): tree path B–E–F–C = {BE 3, EF 5, CF 1}, heaviest EF (5) → 15 − 5 + 6 = 16 add CE (7): tree path C–F–E = {CF 1, EF 5}, heaviest EF (5) → 15 − 5 + 7 = 17 add DE (8): tree path D–A–B–E = {AD 2, AB 4, BE 3}, heaviest AB (4) → 15 − 4 + 8 = 19 second-best weight 16, two ways: {AD, BD, BE, EF, CF} and {AB, AD, BE, BC, CF} — and the second of these IS the shortest-path tree from A of the previous block. Brute force over all 55 trees agrees (the figure checks it at load).

Maximum spanning tree, and the bottleneck tree again

A maximum spanning tree is an MST of the graph with every weight negated (or, equivalently, Kruskal with the edges sorted in decreasing order, Prim with a max-queue): every proof above goes through with the inequalities reversed, so no new algorithm is needed. It is what the maximum-weight forest of Greedy Algorithms, §18 computes on a connected graph, and it has its own applications — the maximum spanning tree of a similarity graph is the basis of tree-structured approximations of a joint distribution, and its path between two vertices is the widest-bottleneck (maximin) path. The minimum bottleneck tree of §16 needs nothing beyond the MST either: every MST is an MBST, so Kruskal's output answers "the path whose heaviest edge is lightest" for every pair at once. When only the bottleneck value is wanted — not the tree — it can be found in linear time by a median-of-weights binary search that repeatedly discards half the edges, a divide-and-conquer trick (Recursion, Divide & Conquer) that beats the E log V of building the tree.

MST for clustering: single linkage is Kruskal stopped early

the correspondencepoints → vertices; d(p, q) → edge weight. Run Kruskal. After V − k acceptances the forest has k trees; those are the k SINGLE-LINKAGE clusters. equivalently: build the whole MST and CUT its k − 1 heaviest edges. The clusters maximise, over all k-partitions, the minimum distance between any two points in different clusters — the SPACING of the clustering. proof: let d* = the weight of the (V − k + 1)-th edge Kruskal accepts — the lightest of the k − 1 cut edges. Every edge lighter than d* was examined before it and either accepted (so it lies inside a cluster) or rejected because its endpoints were already joined by accepted edges (so it lies inside a cluster too): no edge lighter than d* joins two different clusters, and the spacing of Kruskal's clustering is exactly d*. any other k-partition P must split some Kruskal cluster C (if every cluster sat inside one part of P, P would be the same partition); take p, q ∈ C on different sides of P. C's tree path from p to q uses only the first V − k accepted edges, all of weight ≤ d*, and crosses P's boundary somewhere: spacing(P) ≤ d*. ∎ on the shared instance: k = 2 cuts EF (5) → {A, B, D, E} and {C, F}; k = 3 cuts EF, AB → {A, D}, {B, E}, {C, F}; k = 4 cuts EF, AB, BE → {A, D}, {B}, {E}, {C, F} (computed by the figure's routine; these are the forests §17 said an early-stopped Kruskal leaves behind) the DENDROGRAM of single-linkage agglomerative clustering is Kruskal's merge sequence read as a tree; the "chaining" weakness of single linkage is the MST's willingness to take a long detour (the SPT contrast, again).

The clustering page in the machine-learning notes (Clustering, hierarchical clustering) presents single, complete and average linkage as choices of inter-cluster distance; the fact recorded here is that the single-linkage choice makes the whole agglomerative procedure a minimum spanning tree computation, O(n² log n) on the complete distance graph or O(n²) with Prim's array version, rather than the O(n³) of the naive agglomerative loop. Image segmentation by region merging, percolation thresholds, and the "connected components at threshold t" query for every t at once are the same computation under other names: sort the edges, union in order, and read the components off the union-find structure at whichever threshold is asked.

Steiner trees: the NP-hard cousin

Change the question slightly — connect only a given subset of terminal vertices as cheaply as possible, using any other vertices as optional intermediates — and the problem becomes the Steiner tree problem, which is NP-hard in general graphs and in the Euclidean plane (where optional points may be placed anywhere). The MST of the terminals alone, computed on the metric closure (shortest-path distances between terminals), is a 2-approximation: it weighs at most twice the optimal Steiner tree, because doubling the Steiner tree's edges gives an Eulerian walk through every terminal, and shortcutting that walk to visit each terminal once gives a spanning tree of the terminals no heavier than the doubled tree. The gap between "spanning tree of all vertices — polynomial, greedy, three algorithms" and "spanning tree of some vertices — NP-hard, approximable within a constant" is one of the sharpest in the field, and the reasons on the hard side are the subject of the NP-completeness notes in this series (see the series map).

Borůvka by round on the shared instance — every component's lightest outgoing edge lit and added at once — with an overlay comparing the MST against the shortest-path tree from any root Borůvka mode: vertices coloured by component, the round's chosen edges amber, earlier rounds' edges green, and each component's choice listed; MST-vs-SPT mode: the MST on the left with every vertex's tree-path length from the root, the shortest-path tree on the right with δ, edges in one tree but not the other in amber; the readout measures rounds against ⌈log₂ V⌉, checks the weight, computes the second-best MST by brute force and by one swap, and names the vertex with the largest stretch

21 Flow networks, residual graphs, augmenting paths and cuts

A flow network is a directed graph with a non-negative capacity c(u, v) on every edge, a source s and a sink t; a flow is an assignment f(u, v) to every edge that never exceeds the capacity and is conserved at every vertex other than s and t; its value |f| is the net amount leaving s; and the maximum-flow problem asks for a flow of largest value. Four objects built on that definition are the whole vocabulary of the next five sections. The residual network G_f records, for the current flow, how much more can be sent along each edge and how much already sent can be taken back. An augmenting path is an s → t path in G_f, and pushing its bottleneck amount along it produces a flow of larger value. A cut (S, T) is a partition of the vertices with s ∈ S and t ∈ T, its capacity is the total capacity of edges leaving S, and the net flow across it equals |f| for every cut — which gives the one inequality, |f| ≤ c(S, T), that every algorithm on these pages turns into a certificate.

the definitionsNETWORK G = (V, E) directed; c(u, v) ≥ 0 for (u, v) ∈ E and c(u, v) = 0 otherwise; s, t ∈ V; every vertex on some s ⇝ t path (so E ≥ V − 1) convention: no antiparallel pair — if both (u, v) and (v, u) are wanted, split one: u → v′ → v with the same capacity (see below) FLOW f : V × V → ℝ with 0 ≤ f(u, v) ≤ c(u, v) (capacity constraint) ∑ᵥ f(v, u) = ∑ᵥ f(u, v) for every u ∉ {s, t} (conservation: in = out) VALUE |f| = ∑ᵥ f(s, v) − ∑ᵥ f(v, s) net flow out of the source (the second sum is 0 unless an edge enters s) RESIDUAL c_f(u, v) = c(u, v) − f(u, v) if (u, v) ∈ E (room left on the edge) c_f(u, v) = f(v, u) if (v, u) ∈ E (flow on the reverse edge that can be cancelled) c_f(u, v) = 0 otherwise G_f = (V, E_f) with E_f = { (u, v) : c_f(u, v) > 0 }; |E_f| ≤ 2·|E| AUGMENTING a simple s ⇝ t path p in G_f; its residual capacity c_f(p) = min over (u, v) on p of c_f(u, v) > 0 augmenting f along p: f(u, v) += c_f(p) on forward arcs of p, f(v, u) −= c_f(p) on reverse arcs; |f| grows by c_f(p) CUT (S, T) with S ∪ T = V, S ∩ T = ∅, s ∈ S, t ∈ T capacity c(S, T) = ∑_{u ∈ S} ∑_{v ∈ T} c(u, v) — only edges S → T count net flow f(S, T) = ∑_{u ∈ S} ∑_{v ∈ T} f(u, v) − ∑_{u ∈ S} ∑_{v ∈ T} f(v, u) — edges T → S are subtracted the two facts every cut satisfiesf(S, T) = |f| for EVERY cut and every flow (proved below from conservation) |f| ≤ c(S, T) for every flow and every cut (weak duality: f(S, T) ≤ ∑ f(u, v) ≤ ∑ c(u, v)) hence max over flows |f| ≤ min over cuts c(S, T) — §22 shows the two are equal

What the definition allows and forbids, and the two fixes

The flow is defined on all pairs of vertices, with f(u, v) = 0 wherever there is no edge; this makes the conservation sums clean. The convention that a network has no antiparallel edges exists for the residual network: if both (u, v) and (v, u) were present, "the residual capacity of (u, v)" would be two numbers — the room on the forward edge and the cancellable flow on the reverse edge — and the definition above, which reads c_f(u, v) off whichever edge exists, would be ambiguous. The fix is to subdivide one of the pair: replace (v, u) by v → v′ → u through a new vertex v′, both edges with the old capacity. The subdivided network has the same maximum flow (a flow through v′ is exactly a flow on the old edge), one more vertex and one more edge. An implementation does not need to do this: the routine on this page stores each input edge as a pair of arcs, a forward arc with the capacity and a reverse arc with capacity zero, and the two carry opposite flows; each input edge then owns its own reverse arc, and an antiparallel input pair is simply two pairs. The definition's convention and the implementation's pairing are two ways to say the same thing — every unit of residual capacity belongs to exactly one edge.

Several sources and sinks are handled with a supersource and supersink: add a vertex s with an edge of capacity ∞ to each original source and a vertex t with an edge of capacity ∞ from each original sink. Any flow in the multi-source network is a flow in the augmented one of the same value, and conversely, so the two problems have the same optimum. (When each source can supply at most some amount, that amount is the capacity of its edge from s instead of ∞; that is the supply-and-demand form, which §26 uses for circulations.)

The residual network: room left, and flow that can be taken back

Two things can be done to the flow on an edge (u, v) carrying f(u, v) of c(u, v): add up to c(u, v) − f(u, v) more, or remove up to f(u, v) of what is there. The residual network records both as arcs with positive capacity: a forward arc u → v of capacity c − f if any room is left, and a reverse arc v → u of capacity f if any flow is present. Pushing on a reverse arc is cancellation: sending 3 units back along v → u means the edge now carries f − 3, which is why c_f(v, u) = f(u, v) and not c(v, u). Cancellation is not a convenience but the mechanism that lets a greedy sequence of augmentations reach the optimum: an early augmentation may route flow through an edge a better solution needs empty, and the only way to undo that choice without starting over is to push against it. §22's pathological instance, where the worst path rule cancels the same unit 200 times, and §24's push-relabel, which sends excess back towards the source along reverse arcs, both live on this one definition.

Formally, if f is a flow in G and f′ a flow in G_f, their augmentation (f ↑ f′)(u, v) = f(u, v) + f′(u, v) − f′(v, u) on edges of E (and 0 elsewhere) is a flow in G with |f ↑ f′| = |f| + |f′|. The capacity constraint holds because f′(u, v) ≤ c_f(u, v) = c(u, v) − f(u, v) bounds the sum above and f′(v, u) ≤ c_f(v, u) = f(u, v) bounds it below zero; conservation holds because both f and f′ are conserved and the definition is linear; the value adds because the terms at s add. An augmenting path p defines the simplest such f′ — c_f(p) on the arcs of p, zero elsewhere — so augmenting along p increases |f| by exactly c_f(p) > 0. That is the step every augmenting-path algorithm repeats, and the residual network is where it looks for the path.

Cuts, and why the net flow across every cut is |f|

Take any cut (S, T). Start from the value |f| = f(s, V) − f(V, s), where f(X, Y) abbreviates ∑_{x ∈ X} ∑_{y ∈ Y} f(x, y). For each u ∈ S − {s}, conservation says f(u, V) − f(V, u) = 0; add those zero terms to the value to get |f| = f(S, V) − f(V, S). Now split V = S ∪ T in both terms: f(S, V) = f(S, S) + f(S, T) and f(V, S) = f(S, S) + f(T, S). The f(S, S) terms cancel, leaving |f| = f(S, T) − f(T, S), which is the definition of the net flow across the cut. Nothing about the cut was used except that s ∈ S and t ∉ S (so t's non-conservation never entered), so the identity holds for every cut. Weak duality follows in one line: |f| = f(S, T) − f(T, S) ≤ f(S, T) ≤ c(S, T), the first inequality because flows are non-negative and the second by the capacity constraint on each S → T edge. Every cut is therefore an upper bound on every flow, and a flow that equals some cut's capacity is maximum, with that cut as its certificate. Whether such a pair always exists is the content of §22.

Hand-worked: the six-vertex network, a flow of value 19, its residual graph, and one cut

The instance used by every flow figure on this page (SI.gFlow) has vertices s, v₁, v₂, v₃, v₄, t and nine edges: s→v₁ (16), s→v₂ (13), v₂→v₁ (4), v₁→v₃ (12), v₃→v₂ (9), v₂→v₄ (14), v₄→v₃ (7), v₃→t (20), v₄→t (4). Consider the flow that sends 11 along s→v₁, 8 along s→v₂, 1 along v₂→v₁, 12 along v₁→v₃, 4 along v₃→v₂, 11 along v₂→v₄, 7 along v₄→v₃, 15 along v₃→t and 4 along v₄→t. Its value is 11 + 8 = 19. Conservation, checked at each internal vertex:

vertexflow inflow outresidual arcs it gains (c_f)
v₁11 + 1 = 1212v₁→s 11 (cancel), v₁→v₂ 1 (cancel); v₁→v₃ is saturated: no forward arc
v₂8 + 4 = 121 + 11 = 12v₂→s 8, v₂→v₁ 3 (room), v₂→v₃ 4 (cancel), v₂→v₄ 3 (room)
v₃12 + 7 = 194 + 15 = 19v₃→v₁ 12, v₃→v₂ 5 (room), v₃→t 5 (room), v₃→v₄ 7 (cancel)
v₄117 + 4 = 11v₄→v₂ 11 (cancel), v₄→v₃ and v₄→t saturated: no forward arcs

The residual network has 15 arcs (the figure counts them): six forward arcs with room left — s→v₁ 5, s→v₂ 5, v₂→v₁ 3, v₃→v₂ 5, v₂→v₄ 3, v₃→t 5; the three saturated edges v₁→v₃, v₄→v₃, v₄→t have residual capacity 0 and no forward arc — and nine reverse arcs, one per edge carrying flow: v₁→s 11, v₂→s 8, v₁→v₂ 1, v₃→v₁ 12, v₂→v₃ 4, v₄→v₂ 11, v₃→v₄ 7, t→v₃ 15, t→v₄ 4. That is 6 + 9 = 15 arcs of the possible 2E = 18. There is an augmenting path in it: s → v₂ → v₃ → t with arcs of residual capacity 5, 4, 5, so c_f(p) = 4. Note the middle arc: it is the reverse of the edge v₃→v₂, so augmenting cancels the 4 units that edge was carrying — after the augmentation s→v₂ carries 12, v₃→v₂ carries 0, v₃→t carries 19, and the value is 19 + 4 = 23. In the new residual network no s ⇝ t path exists; the vertices reachable from s are {s, v₁, v₂, v₄}.

Now the cut S = {s, v₁, v₂}, T = {v₃, v₄, t}. The edges leaving S are v₁→v₃ (12) and v₂→v₄ (14), so c(S, T) = 26; the flow on them is 12 + 11 = 23, and the one edge entering S from T, v₃→v₂, carries 4, so the net flow is 23 − 4 = 19 = |f|, as the identity requires, and 19 ≤ 26. The cut S = {s, v₁, v₂, v₄} is tighter: leaving edges v₁→v₃ (12), v₄→v₃ (7), v₄→t (4) give c(S, T) = 23, the net flow is again 12 + 7 + 4 − 4 = 19, and the same cut evaluated on the augmented flow of value 23 gives net flow 12 + 7 + 4 − 0 = 23 = c(S, T): the flow of value 23 and this cut certify each other. Brute force over the 2⁴ = 16 cuts (every subset of {v₁, v₂, v₃, v₄} joined to s) gives capacities 29, 25, 34, 26, 58, 42, 54, 34, 40, 36, 31, 23, 62, 46, 44, 24 in subset order — the minimum, 23, is attained only at {s, v₁, v₂, v₄}, which is exactly the set reachable from s in the final residual network. The figure re-derives all of this at load: it checks conservation and the capacity constraint for whichever flow is selected, evaluates the selected cut, enumerates the 16 cuts, and runs Edmonds-Karp (§23) to confirm the maximum.

Vocabulary that will recur

An edge with f(u, v) = c(u, v) is saturated; its forward residual arc is absent. A flow is maximum if no flow has larger value; maximum flows are not unique (the flow of value 23 obtained above and the one Edmonds-Karp finds in §23 differ on three edges — their difference is a circulation — a sum of cycle flows, in the language of the flow-decomposition theorem §26 states), but the value is. A cut is minimum if no cut has smaller capacity; it need not be unique either, though on this instance it is. The bottleneck of a path is its residual capacity. A flow with |f| = c(S, T) for some cut (S, T) is both maximum and a proof of its own maximality, and every algorithm on this page ends by exhibiting that pair. Linear-programming language says the same thing: maximum flow is a linear program, minimum cut is (the integral solution of) its dual, and the identity f(S, T) = |f| together with weak duality is the LP weak-duality inequality written by hand; the connection is stated here and not pursued (the Lagrangian view of constrained optimisation is Optimization).

The six-vertex network with a flow (left), its residual graph (right), and a chosen cut — conservation, |f|, f(S,T) and c(S,T) checked edge labels are f/c on the left and c_f on the right (blue = room left, violet = cancellable flow); vertices on the S side of the chosen cut have an amber ring, T side a teal ring; edges crossing S → T are rose; the readout enumerates all 16 s-t cuts by brute force

22 Ford-Fulkerson and the max-flow min-cut theorem

The Ford-Fulkerson method starts from the zero flow and repeats one step — find an augmenting path in the residual network, push its bottleneck along it — until no augmenting path exists; the max-flow min-cut theorem says that at that moment the flow is maximum, and that its value equals the capacity of the cut whose source side is the set of vertices still reachable from s in the residual network. "Method" rather than "algorithm" because the path is not specified: any rule for choosing it gives a correct maximum flow when the method terminates, and with integer capacities it terminates in at most |f*| augmentations of O(E) each, for O(E·|f*|) in total. That bound is tight and bad: a four-vertex network with capacities M and a perverse path rule takes 2M augmentations to move 2M units, one at a time, and with irrational capacities the method can fail to terminate at all. Choosing the path well is the subject of §23 and §24; this section proves that any path works.

the methodFORD-FULKERSON(G, s, t): f(u, v) = 0 for every edge while there is an augmenting path p in G_f: — any s ⇝ t path with every arc of positive residual capacity c_f(p) = min over arcs of p of c_f(u, v) for each arc (u, v) of p: if (u, v) ∈ E: f(u, v) += c_f(p) else: f(v, u) −= c_f(p) — forward: add; reverse: cancel return f — and the cut S = { v : s ⇝ v in G_f }, which certifies it the max-flow min-cut theorem — for a flow f in G, the following are equivalent(1) f is a maximum flow (2) the residual network G_f has no augmenting path (3) |f| = c(S, T) for some cut (S, T) and therefore max over flows |f| = min over cuts c(S, T) integrality, and the cost with integer capacitiesif every c(u, v) is an integer, the method keeps every f(u, v) an integer, ends with an integer maximum flow, and each augmentation adds ≥ 1 to |f| so augmentations ≤ |f*|, each a search of G_f costing O(V + E_f) = O(E) (E ≥ V − 1, E_f ≤ 2E) ⇒ O(E · |f*|) total this is a function of the capacities, not of V and E alone — pseudo-polynomial; on the pathological instance below it is attained capacity scaling — a Ford-Fulkerson rule that is weakly polynomialΔ ← 2^⌊log₂ U⌋ with U = max c(u, v) (integer capacities); while Δ ≥ 1: { augment along any s ⇝ t path of G_f whose arcs all have c_f ≥ Δ, until none exists; Δ ← Δ/2 } when the Δ-phase ends, the vertices reachable by arcs with c_f ≥ Δ form a cut every crossing residual arc of which has c_f < Δ ⇒ the remaining flow is < E·Δ the next phase (Δ/2) adds ≥ Δ/2 per augmentation ⇒ < 2E augmentations per phase; ⌊log₂ U⌋ + 1 phases, O(E) per search ⇒ O(E² log U) WEAKLY polynomial: polynomial in V, E and the bit length of the capacities, log U — not in V and E alone (that is STRONGLY polynomial: Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²), §23)

Proof of the theorem

(1) ⇒ (2). If f is maximum but G_f has an augmenting path p, then augmenting along p (§21) gives a flow of value |f| + c_f(p) > |f|, contradicting maximality. (2) ⇒ (3). Suppose G_f has no s ⇝ t path. Let S be the set of vertices reachable from s in G_f and T = V − S; s ∈ S by definition and t ∈ T by assumption, so (S, T) is a cut. Take any u ∈ S, v ∈ T. If (u, v) ∈ E, then f(u, v) = c(u, v): otherwise c_f(u, v) = c − f > 0 would make v reachable from u, hence from s, putting v in S. If (v, u) ∈ E, then f(v, u) = 0: otherwise c_f(u, v) = f(v, u) > 0 would again make v reachable. (If neither edge exists both flows are 0 anyway.) So every edge leaving S is saturated and every edge entering S is empty, and the net flow across the cut is f(S, T) = ∑ c(u, v) − 0 = c(S, T). By §21's identity, |f| = f(S, T) = c(S, T). (3) ⇒ (1). By weak duality, |f′| ≤ c(S, T) for every flow f′; if |f| = c(S, T) then no flow exceeds f. The three implications close the cycle. The equality of the maximum flow value and the minimum cut capacity follows: a maximum flow exists (the method finds one for integer capacities; for real capacities compactness supplies one), it satisfies (3) for some cut, and that cut cannot exceed the minimum cut while the minimum cut cannot be below any flow.

The step to notice is (2) ⇒ (3): it is constructive. The set S it names is exactly what a breadth-first or depth-first search from s in the final residual network returns, so the method ends with the minimum cut in hand for one extra O(E) traversal — on the six-vertex network, S = {s, v₁, v₂, v₄}, the unique minimum cut of §21. Every application in §25–§26 that asks for a cut rather than a flow (a vertex cover, a project set, a pixel labelling) reads it off this set.

Integrality, termination, and what irrational capacities break

The integrality theorem: if all capacities are integers, the maximum flow value is an integer and there is a maximum flow in which every f(u, v) is an integer. The proof is the method itself — the zero flow is integral, the residual capacities of an integral flow are integers, so every bottleneck c_f(p) is a positive integer, and every augmentation keeps the flow integral and raises its value by at least 1. Hence the method terminates after at most |f*| augmentations, and the flow it stops with is integral and, by the theorem, maximum. Rational capacities reduce to this case by scaling by the common denominator. §25 depends on integrality entirely: with unit capacities an integral flow is a set of edges, which is what makes "maximum flow = maximum matching" a statement about matchings and not about fractional ones.

With irrational capacities the guarantee is gone. A network with six vertices and irrational capacities is known on which a particular sequence of augmenting-path choices never terminates: the bottlenecks form a geometric series, the flow values converge, and the limit is strictly less than the maximum flow. Its status on this page is evidential only — the construction is a specific, checkable instance in the literature, not something the figures reproduce, because they compute with integers throughout (the pathology they can show is the integer one below). The practical reading is that "Ford-Fulkerson terminates" is a theorem about integer (or rational) capacities, and "Ford-Fulkerson is O(E·|f*|)" is a theorem about integer capacities; neither is a statement about the method in general. Edmonds-Karp (§23) removes the dependence on both the capacities and the path choice.

A second cure keeps the capacities in the bound but tames them. Capacity scaling is the Ford-Fulkerson method with the rule "only augment along paths whose every arc has residual capacity at least Δ", starting with Δ the largest power of two not above the largest capacity U and halving Δ each time no such path remains. When a Δ-phase ends, the set of vertices reachable from s through arcs of residual capacity ≥ Δ is a cut whose crossing residual arcs each carry less than Δ, so at most E·Δ more flow can ever be added; every augmentation of the following phase adds at least Δ/2, so that phase has fewer than 2E augmentations, and there are ⌊log₂ U⌋ + 1 phases: O(E² log U) in total, against the plain method's O(E·|f*|). The bound is weakly polynomial — it depends on the capacities through their bit length log U — where Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) is strongly polynomial, with no capacity in it at all; the two names mark exactly that distinction, and §28 lists scaling as the rule to reach for when capacities are integers with a wide range and E is small relative to V. On the four-vertex pathology below, scaling with U = 100 starts at Δ = 64, saturates the two capacity-100 paths in that first phase, and finds nothing to do in the six phases that follow — the capacity-1 middle edge is never used: 2 augmentations instead of the 200 the "worst" rule performs (checked by running the rule on the page's residual structure; on the six-vertex network it takes 3 augmentations, one at Δ = 8 and two at Δ = 4).

Hand-worked: the six-vertex network under three path rules

The figure implements three rules for the path. Depth-first explores the residual arcs in adjacency order and takes the first s ⇝ t path found. BFS takes a shortest path, which is Edmonds-Karp. Worst prefers any path through the edge of smallest capacity (on the six-vertex network that is v₂→v₁, the first of the two capacity-4 edges in input order; on the four-vertex instance it is the middle edge) and falls back to depth-first only when no such path exists — a rule chosen to be bad. On the six-vertex network, measured at load:

ruleaugmenting paths (bottleneck)augmentationsarc scans|f|
depth-firsts v₁ v₃ v₂ v₄ t (4), s v₁ v₃ t (8), s v₂ v₃ t (4), s v₂ v₄ v₃ t (7)46723
worsts v₂ v₁ v₃ t (4), s v₁ v₂ v₄ t (4), s v₂ v₁ v₃ t (4), s v₁ v₂ v₄ v₃ t (4), s v₂ v₁ v₃ t (4), s v₁ v₂ v₄ v₃ t (3)618323
BFS (Edmonds-Karp)s v₁ v₃ t (12), s v₂ v₄ t (4), s v₂ v₄ v₃ t (7)36623

The depth-first run's first path, s → v₁ → v₃ → v₂ → v₄ → t, goes the long way round through v₃→v₂ and is capped at 4 by v₄→t; its third path s → v₂ → v₃ → t then uses the reverse arc v₂→v₃ to cancel those 4 units, which is cancellation doing exactly what §21 said it is for. The worst rule alternates between two paths through v₂→v₁ and its reverse, pushing 4 units forward and then 4 back through the same edge, and needs six augmentations; all three reach 23, and all three end with the same reachable set {s, v₁, v₂, v₄}, because the minimum cut here is unique. The arc-scan counts are the searches' work; each is O(E_f) per augmentation.

The pathological instance: 2M augmentations on four vertices

Vertices s, u, v, t; edges s→u, s→v, u→t, v→t of capacity M and one middle edge u→v of capacity 1. The maximum flow is 2M: M along s→u→t and M along s→v→t, never touching the middle edge; a cut of capacity 2M is S = {s}. Now choose paths perversely. Path 1: s → u → v → t, bottleneck 1 (the middle edge). The residual network gains the reverse arc v → u of capacity 1. Path 2: s → v → u → t, bottleneck 1 again — it uses that reverse arc and cancels the unit on the middle edge, restoring c_f(u, v) = 1. Path 3 is path 1 again, and so on: every augmentation moves exactly one unit and flips the middle edge, so 2M augmentations are needed, each an O(E) search, for Θ(E·M) total on a graph with V = 4 and E = 5. With M = 10⁶ that is two million augmentations of a five-edge graph; the figure uses M = 100 and the "worst" rule and measures 200 augmentations and 2 402 arc scans, against 4 augmentations for depth-first (its first path also takes the middle edge for one unit, its second pushes 99 along s→u→t, its third cancels the middle unit along s→v→u→t, and its fourth pushes 99 along s→v→t) and 2 for BFS (the two length-2 paths are shortest, and after them the middle edge is useless). The lesson is precise: the bound O(E·|f*|) is not pessimism about an unlucky constant; it is the actual running time of a legitimate implementation of the method on a legitimate input, and the cure is a rule for the path.

Ford-Fulkerson stepped: the augmenting path (amber), the flow after it (left), the residual graph (right), and the minimum cut lit at the end choose the instance and the path rule; each step is one augmentation; the final step draws the source side of the cut {v : s ⇝ v in G_f} with amber rings and the crossing edges in rose; the readout measures all three rules on the current instance and enumerates the s-t cuts by brute force

23 Edmonds-Karp: the O(VE²) bound, proved and measured

Edmonds-Karp is the Ford-Fulkerson method with one rule added: the augmenting path is a shortest s ⇝ t path in the residual network, counted in edges, found by breadth-first search. The rule costs nothing — a BFS is what the search was going to be anyway — and it changes the bound from a function of the capacities to a function of the graph: at most VE augmentations regardless of the capacities, each an O(E) search, so O(VE²) in total, with no assumption of integrality and no dependence on the path being lucky. The proof has two steps that are worth knowing on their own: shortest-path distances from s in the residual network never decrease as the flow grows, and each residual arc can be the bottleneck of an augmentation only V/2 times because its endpoint's distance grows by two between bottleneck events. Dinic's algorithm keeps the same idea and augments along all shortest paths at once, which improves the bound to O(V²E) and, on unit-capacity networks, to far less.

the algorithm, and the quantities the proof tracksEDMONDS-KARP: Ford-Fulkerson with p = a shortest s ⇝ t path in G_f (BFS from s over arcs with c_f > 0) δ_f(s, v) = the number of arcs on a shortest s ⇝ v path in G_f (∞ if unreachable) an arc (u, v) on the augmenting path p is CRITICAL if c_f(u, v) = c_f(p) — the bottleneck; it vanishes from G_f after the augmentation every augmentation has ≥ 1 critical arc the lemma — distances are monotonefor every v ∈ V − {s, t}: δ_f(s, v) is non-decreasing over the augmentations (proof below) the boundeach arc (u, v) is critical at most V/2 times: δ(s, u) ≥ 0 at its first critical event, grows by ≥ 2 between events, and stays ≤ V − 2 while (u, v) lies on an s ⇝ t path at most 2E arcs can ever be residual ⇒ ≤ 2E · V/2 = V·E critical events ⇒ ≤ V·E augmentations each augmentation: one BFS, O(V + E_f) = O(E) ⇒ O(V·E²) time, O(V + E) space on the six-vertex network (measured in the figure): 3 augmentations ≤ V·E = 54; each critical arc critical once ≤ V/2 = 3; 22 vertex dequeues over 4 BFS runs (the last finds no path) Dinic — all shortest paths per phasephase: BFS gives δ_f; the LEVEL GRAPH keeps arcs (u, v) with δ_f(s, v) = δ_f(s, u) + 1; a BLOCKING FLOW saturates some arc on every s ⇝ t path of the level graph after a phase δ_f(s, t) increases by ≥ 1 ⇒ ≤ V − 1 phases; blocking flow by DFS with current-arc pointers: O(V·E) per phase ⇒ O(V²·E) unit capacities: blocking flow in O(E) per phase; on unit networks (every internal vertex has in-degree 1 or out-degree 1 — the matching networks of §25) ≤ 2√V phases ⇒ O(E·√V); general unit-capacity networks: O(E · min(√E, V^{2/3})) on the six-vertex network: 2 phases ≤ V − 1 = 5, 3 path augmentations within them (measured in the figure)

Why the distances never decrease

Suppose an augmentation takes the flow from f to f′ and, for some vertex, δ_{f′}(s, v) < δ_f(s, v). Among all such vertices choose v with the smallest δ_{f′}(s, v). Let p′ be a shortest s ⇝ v path in G_{f′} and (u, v) its last arc, so δ_{f′}(s, u) = δ_{f′}(s, v) − 1; by the choice of v, the distance to u did not decrease: δ_{f′}(s, u) ≥ δ_f(s, u). Two cases. If (u, v) was already a residual arc in G_f, then δ_f(s, v) ≤ δ_f(s, u) + 1 ≤ δ_{f′}(s, u) + 1 = δ_{f′}(s, v), contradicting the assumed decrease. If it was not — (u, v) ∉ E_f but (u, v) ∈ E_{f′} — then the augmentation created it, which means it pushed flow from v to u: the augmenting path contained the arc (v, u). That path was a shortest path in G_f, so along it δ_f(s, u) = δ_f(s, v) + 1, giving δ_f(s, v) = δ_f(s, u) − 1 ≤ δ_{f′}(s, u) − 1 = δ_{f′}(s, v) − 2, again contradicting the decrease. The shortest-path rule is used exactly once, in the second case: an arbitrary augmenting path could push flow along (v, u) while v was further from s than u, creating a residual arc (u, v) that shortens the way to v, and then distances could fall; the shortest-path rule guarantees that the arcs an augmentation creates always point backwards, from a vertex to one closer to s, and a backward arc can shorten nothing.

Why each arc is critical at most V/2 times, and the O(VE) count

Let (u, v) be critical on the augmenting path p under flow f. Since p is shortest, δ_f(s, v) = δ_f(s, u) + 1. After the augmentation the arc is gone from the residual network, and it can reappear only when some later augmentation pushes flow from v to u — that is, when (v, u) lies on a later shortest augmenting path under a flow f′, at which moment δ_{f′}(s, u) = δ_{f′}(s, v) + 1. By the lemma δ_{f′}(s, v) ≥ δ_f(s, v), so δ_{f′}(s, u) ≥ δ_f(s, v) + 1 = δ_f(s, u) + 2. Between two critical events of the same arc, the distance to its tail rises by at least 2. That distance is 0 at least (it is 0 when u = s) and, while the arc still lies on an s ⇝ t path, at most V − 2 (the path continues through v to t); a quantity that starts at ≥ 0, rises by ≥ 2 each time and stays ≤ V − 2 can take at most V/2 values, so (u, v) is critical at most V/2 times. The arcs that can ever be residual are the 2E forward and reverse arcs of the edges, so the total number of critical events is at most 2E · V/2 = VE, and since every augmentation makes at least one arc critical, there are at most VE augmentations. Each is a BFS over at most 2E arcs — O(V + E) = O(E) because every vertex lies on an s ⇝ t path — for O(VE²) in all. Nothing in the argument mentions the capacities: they may be irrational, and the bound stands.

Hand-worked: the six-vertex network

augmentationBFS layers δ_f(s, ·) for s, v₁, v₂, v₃, v₄, tshortest path (bottleneck)critical arc|f|
10, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3s → v₁ → v₃ → t (12)v₁→v₃ (12 of 12)12
20, 1, 1, 3, 2, 3s → v₂ → v₄ → t (4)v₄→t (4 of 4)16
30, 1, 1, 3, 2, 4s → v₂ → v₄ → v₃ → t (7)v₄→v₃ (7 of 7)23
40, 1, 1, ∞, 2, ∞none — t unreachable—23

Read the layers down the columns and the lemma is visible: δ(s, v₃) goes 2, 3, 3, ∞ and δ(s, t) goes 3, 3, 4, ∞; nothing decreases. Each augmentation's critical arc — the one whose residual capacity equals the bottleneck — is different, and each is critical once, well inside the bound of V/2 = 3. The path lengths 3, 3, 4 are non-decreasing too, which is the observation Dinic's algorithm is built on: all the length-3 paths can be exhausted before any length-4 path is needed, so one BFS per length would do. Here Dinic needs two phases (lengths 3 and 4) and finds the same three paths inside them; the figure runs it under its own counter. The final BFS, which finds no path, is the one that returns the cut: the vertices it reaches, {s, v₁, v₂, v₄}, are the minimum cut of §21–§22.

The bound's slack, measured

VE augmentations is a worst case, and on small instances the algorithm is nowhere near it: 3 against 54 here. The figure's random mode generates seeded networks with 8 vertices and 16 edges (capacities 1–9, every vertex on an s ⇝ t path) and reports the same counts; over 300 such seeds the checking script found at most 8 augmentations against the bound VE = 128, and no arc critical more than once. Families that force the bound exist — layered constructions on which Edmonds-Karp genuinely performs Θ(VE) augmentations — but they are not random-looking, and in practice the augmentation count tends to grow far more slowly than VE. Two remarks follow. First, the theoretical improvements — Dinic's O(V²E), push-relabel's O(V²E) generic bound and O(V³) or O(V²√E) with an order (§24), and the more recent almost-linear bounds — matter when the graphs are large and structured (grids of pixels in §26, dense bipartite graphs in §25), not on toy networks. Second, the cross-check that matters is not the count but the value: the figure confirms Edmonds-Karp's |f| against Dinic's, and both against the brute-force minimum cut over all 2^{V−2} cuts, with a flag that would say DISAGREE if they ever differed.

Dinic in a little more detail

A phase runs one BFS from s to get δ_f, keeps only the arcs that advance one level (the level graph), and then finds a blocking flow in it — a flow in the level graph such that every s ⇝ t path of the level graph has a saturated arc. That is not a maximum flow of the level graph, only a maximal one, and it is found by repeated DFS from s that advances along level-graph arcs with residual capacity, retreats (and deletes the arc from further consideration) when stuck, and augments along each s ⇝ t path found; a current-arc pointer per vertex, which never moves backwards within a phase, makes each phase O(VE): each augmentation is O(V) and there are O(E) of them because each saturates a level-graph arc, and the pointer advances total O(E). After a blocking flow every shortest path is destroyed, so the next BFS finds δ(s, t) larger by at least one; δ(s, t) ≤ V − 1 gives at most V − 1 phases and O(V²E) overall. On unit-capacity networks the blocking flow is O(E) per phase (every augmentation saturates every arc on its path, so the DFS never revisits an arc), and the number of phases collapses: after k phases every remaining augmenting path has length > k, and in a unit network (in-degree 1 or out-degree 1 at every internal vertex) the remaining flow is at most V/k, so the phases number at most about 2√V — the O(E√V) that §25 states for Hopcroft-Karp, which is Dinic specialised to a bipartite matching network. The figure runs Dinic under a counter for the phase count only; its per-phase mechanics are described here and hand-worked below, not stepped.

Dinic's two phases on the six-vertex network, hand-worked

Starting from the zero flow, each phase is a BFS for the level numbers, the level graph it leaves, and a blocking flow found by depth-first search in adjacency order (the figure's routine records 2 phases and 3 augmentations, which is what the table re-derives; the residual capacities in the third column are those at the start of the phase):

phaseδ_f(s, t)level graph (arcs advancing one level, residual capacity)blocking flow|f| after
13s→v₁ (16), s→v₂ (13), v₁→v₃ (12), v₂→v₄ (14), v₃→t (20), v₄→t (4) — v₂→v₁, v₃→v₂ and v₄→v₃ do not advance a level and are left outs→v₁→v₃→t pushes 12 (saturating v₁→v₃), then s→v₂→v₄→t pushes 4 (saturating v₄→t): 12 + 4 = 1616
24s→v₁ (4), s→v₂ (9), v₂→v₄ (10), v₄→v₃ (7), v₃→t (8) — s→v₁ is a dead end (v₁'s only residual out-arc, v₁→v₃, is saturated)s→v₂→v₄→v₃→t pushes 7 (saturating v₄→v₃); the DFS from v₁ retreats: 723
3∞BFS reaches v₁, v₂, v₄ and stops: t unreachable—23 = the minimum cut

Two things to read off the table. First, δ_f(s, t) went 3, 4, ∞ — strictly up each phase, as the argument above requires, and the bound V − 1 = 5 phases is met with room to spare. Second, a blocking flow is not in general the maximum flow of its level graph, only a maximal one: in phase 1 here the two happen to coincide (the level graph is two vertex-disjoint paths, whose maximum flow is min(16, 12, 20) + min(13, 14, 4) = 12 + 4 = 16, exactly what the blocking flow pushed), but a level graph of the form s→a, s→b, a→c, b→c, c→t, a→d, d→t with unit capacities shows the gap — a DFS that takes s→a→c→t first has blocked every remaining path (s→b→c→t at c→t, s→a→d→t at s→a) with a blocking flow of 1, while the level graph's maximum flow is 2 (s→b→c→t and s→a→d→t); run through the page's routine that network takes two phases (the second along the reverse arc c→a) and two augmentations to reach 2. Blocking is all a phase needs, because it guarantees the next δ_f(s, t) is larger; asking for the level graph's maximum flow instead would cost a full flow computation per phase for no gain in the phase count.

Edmonds-Karp stepped: the shortest augmenting path with the BFS layers drawn, the critical arc marked, and the counts against the O(VE) bound left: the network with f/c labels, the augmenting path in amber and its critical (bottleneck) arc in rose; right: the same vertices placed in columns by their BFS distance δ_f(s, ·) in the residual graph before the augmentation, unreachable vertices greyed; the readout compares the measured augmentations, per-arc critical counts and BFS work with the bounds, and checks |f| against Dinic and the brute-force minimum cut

24 Push-relabel: preflows, heights, and the O(V²E) generic bound

Push-relabel algorithms compute a maximum flow without ever looking for a path: they work with a preflow, in which a vertex may temporarily hold more flow than it sends on (its excess), and move excess one edge at a time "downhill" according to a height label, raising a vertex's height when it has excess and nowhere lower to send it. The source starts at height V with every outgoing edge saturated, the sink stays at height 0, and the two local operations — PUSH along a residual edge from a vertex to one exactly one unit lower, RELABEL a stuck vertex to one more than its lowest residual neighbour — are repeated in any order until no vertex other than s and t has excess. At that point the preflow is a flow, and it is maximum because the height labels certify that no augmenting path can exist. The analysis counts the three kinds of operation: fewer than 2V² relabels, fewer than 2VE saturating pushes, and O(V²E) non-saturating pushes by a potential argument, giving O(V²E) for any order; specific orders bring it to O(V³) and O(V²√E), and with two heuristics the method is the practical winner on dense and structured networks.

preflow, excess, heightPREFLOW f with 0 ≤ f(u, v) ≤ c(u, v) and in(u) ≥ out(u) for every u ≠ s — conservation relaxed to "in ≥ out" EXCESS e(u) = in(u) − out(u) ≥ 0 for u ≠ s; u is OVERFLOWING (active) if u ∉ {s, t} and e(u) > 0 HEIGHT h : V → ℕ with h(s) = V, h(t) = 0, and h(u) ≤ h(v) + 1 for every residual arc (u, v) ∈ E_f an arc (u, v) ∈ E_f with h(u) = h(v) + 1 is ADMISSIBLE — the only arcs flow is pushed along the two operations and their preconditionsPUSH(u, v): requires e(u) > 0, c_f(u, v) > 0, h(u) = h(v) + 1 Δ = min(e(u), c_f(u, v)); f(u, v) += Δ (or cancel on the reverse edge); e(u) −= Δ; e(v) += Δ SATURATING if Δ = c_f(u, v) (the arc vanishes), NON-SATURATING otherwise (then e(u) becomes 0) RELABEL(u): requires e(u) > 0 and h(u) ≤ h(v) for every residual arc (u, v) — no admissible arc out of u h(u) = 1 + min { h(v) : (u, v) ∈ E_f } (the set is non-empty: an overflowing u has a residual arc back to whoever sent it flow) GENERIC: h(s) = V, all other h = 0; saturate every edge out of s; while some vertex is overflowing: PUSH or RELABEL it (any applicable operation) why it is rightboth operations preserve the height property (below); h(s) = V and a simple s ⇝ t path has ≤ V − 1 arcs, so h(s) ≤ h(t) + (V − 1) = V − 1 < V is impossible ⇒ no s ⇝ t path in G_f, ever at termination no vertex is overflowing ⇒ f is a flow; no augmenting path ⇒ f is maximum (§22, (2) ⇒ (1)) the counts (generic order)h(u) ≤ 2V − 1 always (an overflowing u has a residual path to s of ≤ V − 1 arcs: h(u) ≤ h(s) + V − 1) relabels ≤ (2V − 1)(V − 2) < 2V² each raises a height, heights only rise, only V − 2 vertices are relabelled saturating pushes < 2·V·E between two saturating pushes on the same pair {u, v} one of their heights rises by ≥ 2 non-saturating < 4·V²·(V + E) = O(V²E) potential Φ = Σ h(u) over overflowing u: a non-saturating push drops Φ by ≥ 1; relabels add < 2V², saturating pushes add < 2VE·(2V − 1) total O(V²E) operations, O(1) each amortised with a current-arc pointer per vertex (relabel scans cost O(VE) in all) five-vertex instance, measured in the figure: 5 saturating + 3 non-saturating pushes, 5 relabels, |f| = 6; bounds 27, 60, 1 100 six-vertex network: 5 + 10 pushes, 11 relabels, |f| = 23; bounds 44, 108, 2 160

Why the operations keep h a height function, and why that forbids augmenting paths

RELABEL(u) changes only h(u), and raises it: its precondition says every residual arc out of u had h(u) ≤ h(v), so the new value 1 + min h(v) is larger than the old. For arcs out of u the new height is at most h(v) + 1 by construction (it equals h(v) + 1 for the lowest neighbour, which becomes admissible). For arcs into u, (w, u) with h(w) ≤ h(u) + 1 before, raising h(u) only loosens the constraint. PUSH(u, v) changes no height but may change the residual arcs: it may delete (u, v) (deleting an arc removes a constraint) and may create (v, u), for which the constraint h(v) ≤ h(u) + 1 holds because h(v) = h(u) − 1. So the height property is an invariant. Now suppose G_f had an s ⇝ t path s = v₀, v₁, …, v_k = t; it can be taken simple, so k ≤ V − 1. Applying the property along it, h(s) ≤ h(v₁) + 1 ≤ h(v₂) + 2 ≤ … ≤ h(t) + k = k ≤ V − 1, but h(s) = V. So there is never an augmenting path, at any point of the run — not only at the end. The algorithm is, in this sense, Ford-Fulkerson run backwards: instead of maintaining a flow and working towards "no augmenting path", it maintains "no augmenting path" and works towards a flow.

Termination needs one more fact: an overflowing vertex can always be pushed or relabelled. If some residual arc out of u is admissible, push along it. Otherwise every residual arc out of u has h(u) ≤ h(v) (strict h(u) < h(v) + 1 with integers), which is exactly RELABEL's precondition, and the set of residual arcs out of u is non-empty because u is overflowing — flow arrived at u along some edge, and the reverse of that edge is a residual arc. So the loop never stalls, and the counts below show it ends.

The bounds, with the potential argument spelled out

Heights are bounded. If u is overflowing, there is a simple path from u to s in G_f: let U be the set of vertices reachable from u in G_f; if s ∉ U, then no flow enters U from outside (an edge (w, x) with x ∈ U, w ∉ U carrying flow would give a residual arc (x, w), putting w in U), so the total excess in U is −(flow leaving U) ≤ 0; but excess is non-negative off s and positive at u, a contradiction. Along that path of at most V − 1 arcs, h(u) ≤ h(s) + (V − 1) = 2V − 1. Since RELABEL is only applied to overflowing vertices, no height ever exceeds 2V − 1. Relabels. Each raises a height by at least 1 from 0 to at most 2V − 1, and only the V − 2 internal vertices are ever relabelled: at most (2V − 1)(V − 2) < 2V² relabels. Saturating pushes. Fix a pair {u, v} joined by an edge in either direction. A saturating push u → v needs h(u) = h(v) + 1; before the next push u → v the arc must be recreated by a push v → u, which needs h(v) = h(u) + 1, so h(v) has risen by at least 2 in between; symmetrically for the other direction. Heights rise at most 2V − 1 in total, so each pair sees fewer than 2V saturating pushes, and there are at most E pairs: fewer than 2VE. Non-saturating pushes. Let Φ be the sum of h(u) over overflowing u; Φ = 0 at the start and end and never negative. A non-saturating push u → v empties u (removing h(u) from the sum) and may make v overflowing (adding h(v) = h(u) − 1): Φ falls by at least 1. A relabel raises Φ by the height increase, less than 2V² over the whole run; a saturating push may add a newly overflowing v with h(v) ≤ 2V − 1, less than 2VE(2V − 1) over the run. The total decrease cannot exceed the total increase, so the non-saturating pushes number fewer than 2V² + 2VE(2V − 1) < 4V²(V + E), which is O(V²E) because E ≥ V − 1. Every bound holds for any order of applicable operations, which is what "generic" means; the figure measures all three counts and prints them beside these bounds, and the slack is large on small instances.

Hand-worked: five vertices, thirteen operations

Vertices s, a, b, c, t; edges s→a (5), s→b (3), a→b (2), a→c (2), b→t (4), c→t (3). Maximum flow 6, the cut S = {s, a, b} having capacity c(a, c) + c(b, t) = 2 + 4 = 6. The figure's routine discharges active vertices in first-in first-out order, pushing along admissible arcs in adjacency order and relabelling when none is admissible; its trace, reproduced at load:

#operationheights h(s, a, b, c, t)excess e(a, b, c, t)why
0initialise5, 0, 0, 0, 00, 0, 0, 0h(s) = V = 5
1–2push s→a 5, push s→b 3 (both saturating)5, 0, 0, 0, 05, 3, 0, 0every edge out of s is saturated at the start
3relabel a → 15, 1, 0, 0, 05, 3, 0, 0a's residual neighbours b, c are at 0 (and s at 5): 1 + 0
4–5push a→b 2, push a→c 2 (saturating)5, 1, 0, 0, 01, 5, 2, 0both arcs admissible: h(a) = h(b) + 1 = h(c) + 1

7push a→s 1 (non-saturating)5, 6, 0, 0, 00, 5, 2, 0the unit a cannot place goes back to the source: f(s, a) = 4
8–9relabel b → 1; push b→t 4 (saturating)5, 6, 1, 0, 00, 1, 2, 4b's lowest residual neighbour is t at 0
10–11relabel b → 6; push b→s 1 (non-saturating)5, 6, 6, 0, 00, 0, 2, 4residual arcs out of b: b→a (h 6) and b→s (h 5): 1 + 5; then s is exactly one lower
12–13relabel c → 1; push c→t 2 (non-saturating)5, 6, 6, 1, 00, 0, 0, 6no vertex is overflowing: stop. |f| = e(t) = 6

Five saturating pushes, three non-saturating, five relabels, against the generic bounds 27, 60 and 1 100. Two things in the trace are the algorithm's character. Vertex a receives 5 units it can only pass on 4 of, and the only way to get rid of the fifth is to climb above the source (height 6) and push it back; excess that cannot reach t returns to s by exactly this mechanism, and it is why heights can reach 2V − 1 rather than V. And the final heights h(a) = h(b) = 6 > h(s) and h(c) = 1 separate the graph: {s, a, b} against {c, t}, capacity 6, the minimum cut. The general reading is one-directional. A vertex at height ≥ V cannot reach t in the residual graph (a residual path of ≤ V − 1 arcs would force h(v) ≤ V − 1), so it is certainly on the source side; but a vertex can be stranded below height V and still be unable to reach t, so the cut is read, as in §22, from reachability — here as the set of vertices that cannot reach t in the final residual graph, which is a minimum cut (the largest one; §22's reachable-from-s set is the smallest, and the two coincide here, {s, a, b}, because this instance's minimum cut is unique — the brute force over its 8 cuts finds a single minimiser), and the figure computes that set and its capacity rather than trusting the heights.

Orders, heuristics, and where push-relabel wins

The generic algorithm leaves the order open, and the order determines the bound. Relabel-to-front keeps the internal vertices in a list and repeatedly discharges the current vertex — pushes and relabels until its excess is 0 — moving a relabelled vertex to the front of the list; the list stays topologically sorted with respect to admissible arcs, each pass over the list is O(V) plus the operations it triggers, and a pass that relabels nothing is the last (the list is then fully discharged), so with fewer than 2V² relabels in total there are at most 2V² + 1 passes: O(V³). FIFO selection — discharge active vertices in queue order, as the figure's routine does — is also O(V³). Highest-label first — always discharge the highest active vertex, keeping buckets by height — is O(V²√E), the best of the three in theory and usually in practice. Two heuristics change practical behaviour more than the order: the gap heuristic notices that if no vertex has height k then every vertex with height between k and V can be lifted straight to V + 1 (it can never reach t again), and global relabelling periodically recomputes every height as the exact BFS distance to t in the residual graph, which is the tightest valid labelling. With those two, push-relabel is the algorithm behind most maximum-flow libraries for dense graphs and the graph-cut solvers of §26 (where the competing choice is a specialised augmenting-path algorithm with search-tree reuse), while for sparse graphs with small integer capacities Dinic-style level-graph algorithms remain competitive. The counts on this page are of the generic algorithm with FIFO selection and no heuristics; the bounds quoted for the named orders are stated, not measured.

Push-relabel stepped: vertices drawn at their height, excess as fill, each push (amber arc) and relabel (amber ring) one step vertical position is the height label h (the dashed line is h = V, the source's height); the fill of an internal vertex darkens with its excess; edge labels are f/c; the readout counts saturating and non-saturating pushes and relabels against the generic bounds and checks |f| against Edmonds-Karp and the brute-force minimum cut

25 Maximum bipartite matching as a flow; König and Hall

A matching in a graph is a set of edges no two of which share a vertex; in a bipartite graph — vertices split into L and R with every edge between the sides — a maximum matching is found by one maximum-flow computation on the network that joins a source to every vertex of L, every vertex of R to a sink, and directs every edge from L to R, all with capacity 1. The integrality theorem is what makes the reduction a theorem rather than a relaxation: an integral flow of value k in that network is a matching of size k, and conversely, so the maximum flow is the maximum matching. Ford-Fulkerson on the network needs at most V/2 augmentations of O(V + E) each, O(VE) in all; Hopcroft-Karp augments along many shortest paths per phase and needs only O(√V) phases, O(E√V). The minimum cut of the same network is a minimum vertex cover, which is König's theorem, and König's theorem in turn gives Hall's condition for when every vertex of L can be matched: never more vertices on the left than they have neighbours on the right.

the reductionG = (L ∪ R, E) bipartite ⟶ G′: s → l (cap 1) for l ∈ L; l → r (cap 1) for (l, r) ∈ E; r → t (cap 1) for r ∈ R V′ = V + 2, E′ = E + V a matching M of size k ⟺ an integral flow f of value k: f = 1 on s → l, l → r, r → t for each (l, r) ∈ M, 0 elsewhere (M ⇒ f: capacity 1 and conservation hold because no vertex is in two edges of M; f ⇒ M: unit capacities on s → l and r → t force at most one unit through each l and each r, so the middle edges carrying 1 form a matching) max matching = max flow = the integral maximum flow the method returns (integrality theorem, §22) costsFord-Fulkerson on G′: |f*| ≤ min(|L|, |R|) ≤ V/2 augmentations × O(V′ + E′) = O(V + E) per search = O(V·E) (E ≥ V/2 when no vertex is isolated) Hopcroft-Karp: O(√V) phases × O(E) per phase = O(E·√V) — Dinic on a unit network (§23) weighted (assignment): Hungarian method O(V³); or min-cost flow on G′ with costs on the middle edges (§26) the two theoremsKÖNIG in a bipartite graph, max |M| = min |C| over vertex covers C (a vertex set touching every edge) constructively: Z = vertices reachable from the unmatched vertices of L by M-alternating paths; C = (L ∖ Z) ∪ (R ∩ Z) HALL L can be completely matched ⟺ |N(S)| ≥ |S| for every S ⊆ L (N(S) = the neighbours of S) a violating S — more vertices than neighbours — is a certificate that no such matching exists

Augmenting paths in matching language

An augmenting path in G′_f starts s → l for an unmatched l (the edge s → l still has capacity), alternates between unmatched edges l → r traversed forward and matched edges traversed backward along their reverse arcs r → l (the cancellation of §21 — undoing a matching edge), and ends r → t at an unmatched r. Dropping s and t gives an M-augmenting path in G: an odd-length path from an unmatched L-vertex to an unmatched R-vertex whose edges alternate not-in-M, in-M, …, not-in-M. Augmenting along it — flipping every edge's membership — adds one more edge than it removes, and the max-flow min-cut theorem's (2) ⇒ (1) becomes the statement that a matching with no augmenting path is maximum (this is usually credited as a separate theorem about matchings, and it holds in general graphs too, where the flow reduction does not apply). The routine on this page runs directly on G: for each unmatched l it breadth-first searches alternating paths, and when it reaches an unmatched r it flips the path. That is Ford-Fulkerson on G′ with the source and sink edges made implicit — same augmentations, same result, and the figure confirms the count and the size against the explicit flow network.

Hand-worked: the 4 × 4 instance

L = {l₀, l₁, l₂, l₃}, R = {r₀, r₁, r₂, r₃}, edges l₀: r₀ r₁; l₁: r₀; l₂: r₁ r₂ r₃; l₃: r₂ — seven edges. The flow network has 10 vertices and 4 + 7 + 4 = 15 edges. Processing L in order:

fromalternating path found (⇢ unmatched edge, ⇠ matched edge undone)matching aftersize
l₀l₀ ⇢ r₀{l₀r₀}1
l₁l₁ ⇢ r₀ ⇠ l₀ ⇢ r₁ — r₀ is taken, so the search follows its matching edge back to l₀ and finds l₀ another partner{l₀r₁, l₁r₀}2
l₂l₂ ⇢ r₂ (r₁ is taken; the BFS reaches r₂ first among the free ones in adjacency order after r₁){l₀r₁, l₁r₀, l₂r₂}3
l₃l₃ ⇢ r₂ ⇠ l₂ ⇢ r₃{l₀r₁, l₁r₀, l₂r₃, l₃r₂}4

Four augmentations, a perfect matching. Brute force over all matchings — every subset of the 7 edges with no shared vertex; the routine enumerates 31 of them, the empty one included — confirms 4 is the maximum. The explicit flow network under Edmonds-Karp also makes 4 augmentations and reaches value 4. Hopcroft-Karp does it in 2 phases: the first phase's layering has every L-vertex free and finds three vertex-disjoint length-1 paths, l₀r₀, l₂r₁, l₃r₂ (l₁'s only neighbour r₀ is taken by then); the second phase's shortest augmenting path from l₁ has length 5, l₁ ⇢ r₀ ⇠ l₀ ⇢ r₁ ⇠ l₂ ⇢ r₃, and completes the matching. The figure reports the phase count under its own counter. König's cover: no vertex of L is unmatched, so the set Z reachable by alternating paths from unmatched L-vertices is empty and the cover is L ∖ Z = L itself, size 4 — the minimum, since a cover must touch each of the four disjoint matching edges separately. Brute force over all 2⁸ = 256 vertex subsets confirms that no cover of size 3 exists. Hall's condition holds for all 15 non-empty S ⊆ L, as it must when a perfect matching exists.

The Hall-violating variant. Remove l₂'s edges to r₂ and r₃, leaving l₀: r₀ r₁; l₁: r₀; l₂: r₁; l₃: r₂. Now the three vertices {l₀, l₁, l₂} have only two neighbours, {r₀, r₁}, and cannot all be matched. The routine finds l₀ ⇢ r₀, then l₁ ⇢ r₀ ⇠ l₀ ⇢ r₁, then from l₂ the alternating search reaches r₁, follows the matching to l₀, reaches r₀, follows it to l₁, and has nowhere left to go — no augmenting path, l₂ stays unmatched — then l₃ ⇢ r₂. Maximum matching 3. König's construction: from the unmatched l₂, the alternating paths reach Z = {l₂, r₁, l₀, r₀, l₁}, so the cover is (L ∖ Z) ∪ (R ∩ Z) = {l₃} ∪ {r₀, r₁}, size 3, which touches all five edges; brute force confirms no cover of size 2. And L ∖ C = {l₀, l₁, l₂} with N = {r₀, r₁} is the Hall violator, found by brute force over the 15 subsets as the one with the largest deficiency |S| − |N(S)| = 1.

König from max-flow min-cut

Give the middle edges l → r capacity ∞ instead of 1; the maximum flow is unchanged (each l receives at most 1 from s, so no middle edge ever carries more than 1), and now every finite cut has a clean shape. Let (S, T) be a minimum cut, necessarily finite since S = {s} has capacity |L|. No ∞ edge crosses it, so there is no edge (l, r) with l ∈ S and r ∈ T. Define C = (L ∩ T) ∪ (R ∩ S). C is a vertex cover: an edge (l, r) with l ∉ C has l ∈ S, hence r ∉ T, hence r ∈ S, hence r ∈ C. Its size is the cut's capacity: the crossing edges are s → l for l ∈ L ∩ T (one each) and r → t for r ∈ R ∩ S (one each), so c(S, T) = |L ∩ T| + |R ∩ S| = |C|. Conversely any vertex cover C defines the cut S = {s} ∪ (L ∖ C) ∪ (R ∩ C) of capacity exactly |C| — the crossing edges are s → l for l ∈ L ∩ C and r → t for r ∈ R ∩ C, and no middle edge crosses because an edge from l ∉ C goes to some r ∈ C ⊆ S. So minimum cut capacity = minimum vertex cover size; with maximum flow = maximum matching and the max-flow min-cut theorem in between, max |M| = min |C|. The reachable set of §22 gives the cover explicitly: S = {s} ∪ Z where Z is what s reaches in the residual network, which is precisely the set of vertices reachable from unmatched L-vertices by alternating paths; the formula C = (L ∖ Z) ∪ (R ∩ Z) is (L ∩ T) ∪ (R ∩ S) read off that S. The easy direction, |M| ≤ |C| for any matching and any cover, needs no flow: each vertex of C touches at most one edge of M, and every edge of M must be touched. König's theorem says the bound is tight in bipartite graphs; in general graphs it fails (a triangle has maximum matching 1 and minimum cover 2), which is why the flow reduction needs the two sides.

Hall from König

Necessity is immediate: if every vertex of L is matched, the matching sends S to |S| distinct neighbours, so |N(S)| ≥ |S|. Sufficiency: suppose no matching covers L, so the maximum matching has size < |L| and by König there is a vertex cover C with |C| < |L|. Let S = L ∖ C. Every edge leaving S must be covered by its R-endpoint, so N(S) ⊆ R ∩ C and |N(S)| ≤ |C| − |L ∩ C| < |L| − |L ∩ C| = |S|: Hall's condition fails at S. The proof is constructive in the same way König's is: the violating set is L minus the cover, and the cover is read off the final residual network — which is how the figure lights the violator without searching. A direct flow proof exists too (bound the capacity of any cut in G′ below by |L| using the condition, so the maximum flow is |L|), and the condition generalises: the largest number of L-vertices that can be matched is |L| − max over S ⊆ L of (|S| − |N(S)|), the deficiency version, which the brute-force routine computes and the figure prints.

Hopcroft-Karp, and the weighted problem

Hopcroft-Karp runs phases. A phase does one BFS from all unmatched L-vertices at once, alternating unmatched and matched edges, to layer the graph by shortest alternating distance; then a DFS restricted to the layers finds a maximal set of vertex-disjoint shortest augmenting paths and flips them all. This is Dinic's algorithm on the matching network (§23), and the phase bound is the unit-network argument: after √V phases every remaining augmenting path has length > √V; the symmetric difference of the current matching M with a maximum one M* contains |M*| − |M| vertex-disjoint augmenting paths, each using more than √V vertices, so |M*| − |M| < V/√V = √V, and since each phase adds at least one edge, fewer than √V further phases remain. Each phase is O(E), so the total is O(E√V). On the 4 × 4 instance it takes 2 phases (measured), against the 4 augmentations of the one-path-at-a-time routine. For the weighted problem — the assignment problem, a perfect matching of minimum total weight — the reduction is to min-cost flow on G′ with the weights as costs on the middle edges (§26), solved by successive shortest paths; the Hungarian method is the same successive-shortest-path idea with dual variables (vertex potentials) maintained so that Dijkstra can replace Bellman-Ford, and runs in O(V³). Both are pointers here.

Bipartite matching stepped: alternating paths (amber), the matching (blue), then the König cover (rose rings) and, if it exists, the Hall violator (amber fill) each step is one augmentation from the next unmatched L-vertex; dashed amber arcs on the path are matching edges being undone; the final step rings the vertices of the minimum vertex cover read from the residual reachability and, on the violating instance, fills the Hall set S and its neighbourhood N(S); the readout checks matching = flow = brute force over all matchings, and cover = brute force over all 256 vertex subsets

26 What flow solves: disjoint paths, project selection, image segmentation, circulations

A problem is solved by maximum flow when it can be rewritten as "how much can be routed" and by minimum cut when it can be rewritten as "what is the cheapest way to separate" — and because the two are equal, a reduction to either gives both the optimum and its certificate. This section states the standard reductions precisely: the network built, what a flow or cut in it means in the original problem, and why the correspondence is exact. Edge- and vertex-disjoint paths (unit capacities and vertex splitting; Menger's theorem), project selection (a closure problem as a minimum cut, worked on four projects), image segmentation (a binary labelling energy as a minimum cut, worked on a 3 × 3 grid with a smoothness slider), baseball elimination (sketched), circulations with demands and lower bounds (the two transformations), and the pointers to minimum-cost flow, b-matching and scheduling. Every worked number is recomputed by the figure at load, and the segmentation is checked against brute force over all 2⁹ = 512 labellings.

the reductions, each as (network → what the flow/cut means)EDGE-DISJOINT s ⇝ t PATHS every edge capacity 1; |f*| = the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths; min cut = fewest edges whose removal disconnects t from s (Menger) VERTEX-DISJOINT PATHS split each v ∉ {s, t} into v_in → v_out (cap 1); edges into v enter v_in, edges out of v leave v_out; |f*| = max vertex-disjoint paths; min cut = fewest vertices to delete PROJECT SELECTION (closure) profits p_i (a cost if p_i < 0), "i requires j"; s → i (cap p_i) if p_i > 0, i → t (cap −p_i) if p_i < 0, i → j (cap ∞) per requirement a finite cut (S, T) ⟺ a closed set A = S − {s}; c(S, T) = P − profit(A) with P = Σ positive profits; best profit = P − min cut;
