Disjoint paths and Menger's theorem

Give every edge capacity 1. An integral flow of value k decomposes into k paths from s to t (walk from s along edges carrying flow, removing each edge used; conservation guarantees the walk reaches t, and a cycle in the flow can be discarded without changing the value), and with unit capacities no edge is on two of them: they are edge-disjoint. That is the unit-capacity case of the flow decomposition theorem: every flow is a sum of at most E pieces, each a path flow (along one s ⇝ t path) or a cycle flow, found by peeling — take any path or cycle of edges carrying positive flow, subtract its minimum edge flow, which zeroes at least one edge, and repeat, so at most E pieces — and only the path flows contribute to |f|. It is also why maximum flows are not unique (§21): two maximum flows can differ by a cycle flow, or by which of two paths carries a unit, without differing in value, so "the" maximum flow is a value with many witnesses, exactly as "the" shortest path is a distance with possibly many paths. Conversely k edge-disjoint paths are a flow of value k. So the maximum flow is the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, and the minimum cut — a set of edges leaving S of total capacity c(S, T), i.e. that many edges — is a smallest set of edges whose removal leaves t unreachable from s. The equality of the two is Menger's theorem (edge form). For vertex-disjoint paths, split each internal vertex v into v_in and v_out joined by a single edge of capacity 1, route every edge into v to v_in and every edge out of v from v_out; a flow can pass through v at most once, so integral flows are sets of vertex-disjoint paths, and the minimum cut counts the fewest vertices whose deletion separates s from t (Menger, vertex form; cutting a split edge is deleting the vertex, and cutting an original edge is never cheaper than cutting one of its endpoints' split edges). Undirected graphs reduce by replacing each edge with two antiparallel unit arcs — a maximum flow can be assumed to use at most one direction of each pair, by cancelling. On the six-vertex network the answer is 2 both ways (the source has out-degree 2, so it cannot be more, and s→v₁→v₃→t with s→v₂→v₄→t are vertex-disjoint); the figure's brute force enumerates all 5 simple s ⇝ t paths and checks every subset of them for disjointness.

Project selection: a closure problem as a minimum cut

Projects i have profits p_i, positive for revenue and negative for pure costs, and a prerequisite relation "i requires j"; a feasible selection is a set A closed under prerequisites, and the goal is to maximise Σ_{i ∈ A} p_i. Build the network as in the block: s → i with capacity p_i for each profitable project, i → t with capacity −p_i for each costly one, and an edge i → j of capacity ∞ for each requirement. A cut of finite capacity cannot cut an ∞ edge, so if i ∈ S and i requires j then j ∈ S: the set A = S − {s} is closed. Its capacity is the sum of p_i over profitable projects not in A (their s → i edges cross) plus the costs of the costly projects in A (their i → t edges cross), which is P − profit(A) where P is the sum of all positive profits. Minimising the cut therefore maximises the profit, and the best closed set is the source side of any minimum cut — the reachable set that Ford-Fulkerson returns for free.

Worked. Four projects A = +9, B = +6, C = −4, D = −5; A requires C, B requires C and D. P = 15. The eight closed sets and their profits: ∅: 0, {C}: −4, {D}: −5, {C, D}: −9, {A, C}: 5, {A, C, D}: 0, {B, C, D}: −3, {A, B, C, D}: 6. The best is to do everything, profit 6, even though B alone with its prerequisites loses 3 — because C is paid for once and shared. The network has 6 vertices and 7 edges; Edmonds-Karp finds |f*| = 9 in 2 augmentations, and 15 − 9 = 6 with the reachable set {A, B, C, D}. The cut for {A, C}, for comparison, has capacity 6 (B left out) + 4 (C taken) = 10 = 15 − 5. The same construction solves any "choose a closed set to maximise a sum of vertex weights" problem: open-pit mining (a block requires the blocks above it), module selection with dependencies, and the segmentation below is its cousin with a symmetric penalty in place of ∞.

Image segmentation: a binary labelling energy as a minimum cut

Each pixel p gets a label x_p ∈ {0, 1} (background, foreground); a data term D_p(x_p) says how badly p's intensity fits the region it is assigned to, and a smoothness term charges λ for every pair of 4-neighbours with different labels. The energy is E(x) = Σ_p D_p(x_p) + λ · #{p ~ q : x_p ≠ x_q}, and the minimum-energy labelling is a minimum cut in the network of the block: s → p with capacity D_p(0), p → t with capacity D_p(1), and p → q, q → p both with capacity λ. Put p on the source side when x_p = 1. Then the cut severs s → p exactly for the pixels labelled 0 (paying D_p(0)), severs p → t exactly for the pixels labelled 1 (paying D_p(1)), and severs one of the two arcs between each differently-labelled pair (paying λ once, since only the arc from the source side to the sink side counts); so c(S, T) = E(x) for every labelling, and the minimum cut is the global minimum of the energy. The condition that makes the construction work is that disagreement costs more than agreement — the smoothness term must be non-negative (more generally, submodular) — because a capacity cannot be negative; Image Segmentation, §23 states that condition in full, with the contrast-sensitive weights used in practice and the multi-label extensions that are no longer exact. Here the algorithmic content: the network has N + 2 vertices and up to 2N + 4N edges for N pixels on a grid, the flow is computed by Edmonds-Karp in the figure, and the labelling is the reachable set.

Worked on a 3 × 3 grid. Intensities (row by row) 7, 8, 3 / 8, 4, 7 / 2, 3, 1 on a 0–10 scale; the foreground model is intensity 8 and the background 2, and the data terms are the absolute deviations: D_p(1) = |I_p − 8|, D_p(0) = |I_p − 2|. Pixels numbered 0 … 8 row by row:

pixel012345678
intensity783847231
D_p(1) foreground (cap p → t)105041657
D_p(0) background (cap s → p)561625011
cheaper label alone110101000

At λ = 0 every pixel takes its cheaper label independently, x = 110 101 000, energy 1 + 0 + 1 + 0 + 2 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 1 = 7. At λ = 3 that labelling has 7 disagreeing neighbour pairs — (1,2), (3,4), (4,5), (1,4), (2,5), (3,6), (5,8) — and costs 7 + 21 = 28, while "top two rows foreground", x = 111 111 000, costs data 1 + 0 + 5 + 0 + 4 + 1 for the six foreground pixels plus 0 + 1 + 1 for the three background ones, 13, plus λ for each of the 3 vertical pairs between rows 2 and 3, 9, total 22. The minimum cut finds 22 and brute force over all 512 labellings confirms it is the unique minimum. The network at λ = 3 has 11 vertices and 39 edges — 15 terminal edges (the three zero-capacity ones, p → t for pixels 1 and 3 and s → p for pixel 6, are omitted) plus 24 neighbour arcs — and Edmonds-Karp makes 13 augmentations. Sweeping λ from 0 to 8 the labelling changes three times: at λ = 2 pixel 5 (intensity 7, but with three background neighbours) flips to background; at λ = 3 pixels 2, 4 and 5 become foreground, making the clean two-row split; at λ = 5 the boundary is no longer worth paying for and everything becomes background at energy 27 = Σ D_p(0). Ten flips in all, and pixel 5 goes 1 → 0 → 1 → 0 — the labelling is not monotone in λ pixel by pixel, only the boundary length is non-increasing. At λ = 1 and λ = 2 the minimum is tied (two and three optimal labellings); the labelling the figure draws is then the source side the residual reachability returns, which is the minimum cut with the fewest foreground pixels, a convention and not a property of the energy.

Baseball elimination, sketched

Teams have current wins w_i and remaining games g_{ij} against each other; is team z already unable to finish with the most wins? Suppose z wins all its remaining games, ending with W = w_z + g_z. Build a network with a node per remaining pairing {i, j} among the other teams and a node per other team: s → {i, j} with capacity g_{ij}, {i, j} → i and {i, j} → j with capacity ∞, and i → t with capacity W − w_i (the wins team i may still collect without passing z; if some w_i > W the answer is already no). A flow assigns each remaining game's wins to its two teams; every game can be played out without anyone overtaking z iff the maximum flow saturates all the s → {i, j} edges, i.e. equals the total number of remaining games among the others. When it does not, the minimum cut names a set of teams T* whose current wins plus the games among themselves exceed W · |T*| — a certificate anyone can check by arithmetic: those teams cannot all stay at or below W. The reduction is a template for "can these demands all be met" questions: sources of supply on the left, sinks of capacity on the right, and a check that the flow saturates the supply.

Circulations with demands and lower bounds

A circulation has no source or sink; instead each vertex has a demand d(v) (negative for a supplier), the demands sum to zero, and the flow must satisfy capacity on every edge and in(v) − out(v) = d(v) at every vertex. Feasibility reduces to maximum flow: add s* with an edge s* → v of capacity −d(v) to each supplier and t* with an edge v → t* of capacity d(v) from each demander; a circulation exists iff the maximum s* ⇝ t* flow saturates every new edge, i.e. has value D = Σ_{d(v) > 0} d(v), and then the flow restricted to the original edges is the circulation. If the answer is no, the minimum cut gives the reason: a set of vertices whose net demand exceeds the capacity of the edges into it. With lower bounds ℓ(e) ≤ f(e) ≤ c(e), pre-send ℓ(e) on every edge: that consumes ℓ(e) of the capacity, leaving c(e) − ℓ(e), and it changes each vertex's balance by ℓ_in(v) − ℓ_out(v), so the residual demand is d′(v) = d(v) − ℓ_in(v) + ℓ_out(v); solve the lower-bound-free problem with capacities c − ℓ and demands d′, and add the ℓ(e) back. Both transformations are exact — they are bijections between feasible solutions — and together they cover survey design, scheduling with minimum staffing, and flows that must use certain edges.

Hand-worked: lower bounds on three vertices

Vertices a, b, c with demands d(a) = −3 (a supplier of 3), d(b) = 1, d(c) = 2, summing to zero, and three edges with lower bound and capacity [ℓ, c]: a→b [1, 3], b→c [2, 4], a→c [0, 2]. Pre-sending the lower bounds delivers 1 into b from a→b and 2 into c from b→c, and takes 1 out of a and 2 out of b, so with d′(v) = d(v) − ℓ_in(v) + ℓ_out(v):

vertexd(v)ℓ_in(v)ℓ_out(v)d′(v)reading
a−301−3 − 0 + 1 = −2still a supplier, of the 2 units the lower bounds did not already take out
b1121 − 1 + 2 = 2it received its 1 but had to send 2 on, so it now needs 2 more in
c2202 − 2 + 0 = 0the lower bounds alone satisfy it

The residual demands sum to zero, as they must (Σ ℓ_in = Σ ℓ_out over all vertices). The lower-bound-free problem has capacities c − ℓ: a→b 2, b→c 2, a→c 2, with s* → a of capacity 2 and b → t* of capacity 2, and D′ = 2. The page's augmenting-path routine on that network finds one path, s* → a → b → t*, of bottleneck 2: value 2 = D′, so a circulation exists, with f′(a→b) = 2, f′(b→c) = 0, f′(a→c) = 0. Adding the lower bounds back gives f(a→b) = 3, f(b→c) = 2, f(a→c) = 0, and checking the original problem: a has in − out = 0 − 3 = −3, b has 3 − 2 = 1, c has 2 − 0 = 2, every edge within its [ℓ, c] — and it is in fact the only circulation, since b→c ≥ 2 forces a→b ≥ 3. The sign convention is the part worth checking rather than remembering: with the signs of ℓ_in and ℓ_out swapped the residual demands would read (−4, 0, 4), the reduced network would happily route 4 units a → c (2 direct, 2 via b), and adding ℓ back would give a an outflow of 5 against its supply of 3 — a "solution" that violates the demands, which is why the routine's answer is always re-checked against the original d before it is believed.

Pointers: minimum-cost flow, b-matching, scheduling

Minimum-cost flow adds a cost per unit on each edge and asks for the cheapest flow of a given value (or the cheapest maximum flow). The residual network gets costs too — the reverse arc of an edge with cost k costs −k, since it refunds — and the successive shortest paths algorithm augments along a cheapest augmenting path each time, found by Bellman-Ford (§07) because of the negative reverse costs, or by Dijkstra after Johnson-style reweighting with vertex potentials (§14), which is the practical version; the correctness invariant is that a flow is minimum-cost for its value iff the residual network has no negative-cost cycle, which is also the basis of the alternative cycle-cancelling algorithm. The assignment problem of §25 is its special case. b-matching gives each vertex a capacity b(v) on how many matching edges may touch it — jobs to machines that can run several, students to courses with quotas — and is the matching network with s → l of capacity b(l) and r → t of capacity b(r). Scheduling problems with "each of n tasks needs one of its allowed slots, each slot holds at most k" are the same network; with time windows and machine capacities they are circulations with demands. In each case the reduction is the whole algorithm, and the two things to state precisely are what the capacities mean and what an integral flow of maximum value corresponds to in the original problem.

Minimum-cut segmentation of the 3 × 3 grid: the labelling, the cut in the network, and the energy across λ — checked against all 512 labellings left: the pixels with their intensities, foreground (source side) filled blue, with the cut drawn as rose bars between differently-labelled neighbours; middle: the network with s above and t below, a pixel's terminal capacities printed as D₀ / D₁ and the severed terminal edges in rose; right: the optimal energy for every λ from 0 to 8, the current λ marked, with a tick wherever the labelling changes; the readout also re-derives the disjoint-path and project-selection answers of this section

27 Weighted-graph algorithms in machine learning

A weighted-graph algorithm enters machine learning in one of four roles: as an exact solver for a combinatorial subproblem (a minimum cut that is the global optimum of a labelling energy, an assignment that is a maximum-weight matching, a transport plan that is a minimum-cost flow); as an exact inference engine whose recurrence is literally a shortest path on a DAG (Viterbi, and every trellis decoder built like it); as a geometric primitive that turns a point cloud into distances or clusters (geodesics on a nearest-neighbour graph, single-linkage trees); and as the shape that other graph computations are mistaken for — PageRank, message passing, greedy routing in a vector index — which are iterations on a graph but not shortest paths, and whose guarantees are of a different kind. Each correspondence below is stated exactly: which graph is built, which algorithm on this page runs on it, under which hypothesis of that algorithm, and at what cost; every claim of exactness is a theorem from §02–§26 applied to a particular network, and every claim of "not this" says what the computation is instead. The links go to the page in these notes where the learning side is treated.

the correspondences, one line eachMANIFOLD LEARNING (Isomap) points → k-NN graph, w = Euclidean length; all-pairs shortest paths (V × Dijkstra, §12; Floyd-Warshall, §13) → geodesic distances → classical MDS SINGLE-LINKAGE CLUSTERING points → complete graph, w = d(p, q); Kruskal stopped after n − k acceptances (§17, §20) = the k clusters of maximum spacing, exactly CHOW–LIU TREES variables → complete graph, w = mutual information; MAXIMUM spanning tree (§20, negate) = the best tree-structured factorisation GRAPH-CUT SEGMENTATION / MAP pixels → s-t network, D_p on terminal edges, λ (submodular V_pq) between neighbours; min cut (§22–§24, §26) = the global minimum of the energy VITERBI DECODING states × time → trellis DAG, w = −log p; the one-pass DAG algorithm (§09) = the most probable state sequence; E ≈ T·S² relaxations, one per arc DETERMINISTIC PLANNING states → graph, w = step cost ≥ 0, γ = 1; value iteration = Bellman-Ford's rounds (§08), Dijkstra = value iteration in distance order (§04) OPTIMAL TRANSPORT (EMD, W₁) two histograms → bipartite transportation network, supplies and demands = masses, cost = ground distance; MIN-COST FLOW (§26 pointer) ASSIGNMENT / DATA ASSOCIATION detections × tracks → bipartite graph, w = matching cost; maximum-weight perfect matching (Hungarian O(V³), or min-cost flow on §25's network) NOT shortest paths: PageRank / TextRank = the stationary vector of a random walk (power iteration); GNN message passing = L learned aggregation rounds (Bellman-Ford's shape with min, + replaced); HNSW search = greedy descent, no optimality guarantee

Geodesics on a neighbourhood graph: manifold learning

The linear methods on the Dimensionality Reduction page flatten a point cloud by a rotation, and the table there of nonlinear and neighbour-embedding alternatives begins with the observation that a curved manifold — a spiral, a Swiss roll — is not flat in Euclidean distance but is flat in distance measured along it. Isomap makes that distance computable with two objects from this page. First a k-nearest-neighbour graph: each point is joined to its k nearest points (or to every point within radius ε), each edge weighted by the straight-line length, giving an undirected graph with n vertices and about nk edges whose weights are all non-negative. Second, all-pairs shortest paths on that graph (§12–§14): with weights ≥ 0 the choice is n runs of Dijkstra, O(n · nk log n) = O(n² k log n) with a binary heap, against Floyd-Warshall's Θ(n³), and the sparse choice wins by a factor of n / (k log n) — the decision procedure of §28 applied verbatim. The resulting n × n matrix of graph distances approximates the geodesic distances on the manifold, and classical multidimensional scaling — the eigendecomposition of the double-centred squared-distance matrix, the same computation as PCA on a Gram matrix — recovers coordinates in which those distances are Euclidean. Three facts from this page govern when it works. The graph must be connected: a component unreachable from another has δ = ∞ (the no-path property, §02), and an implementation must either raise k, keep the largest component, or embed components separately. The weights must not short-circuit: an edge that cuts across a fold makes the graph distance shorter than the geodesic, and no amount of shortest-path computation notices, because the algorithm answers exactly the question asked of the graph it is given. And the cost is dominated by the shortest paths, not the top-d eigendecomposition (computed iteratively; a full Θ(n³) eigendecomposition would dominate), for the sizes where k log n ≪ n. Spectral embeddings, t-SNE and UMAP begin from the same neighbourhood graph and never compute a shortest path: they work from the graph's Laplacian or from local affinities, which is why they preserve neighbourhoods rather than geodesic distances.

Minimum spanning trees: single linkage, spacing, and the tree that factorises a distribution

§20 proved that the k clusters produced by single-linkage agglomeration — merge the two closest clusters, distance between clusters being the minimum over cross pairs — are exactly the components left when Kruskal's algorithm stops after n − k acceptances on the complete distance graph, and that this partition maximises the spacing (the minimum inter-cluster distance) over all k-partitions. The Clustering page presents the dendrogram and the linkage choices; the fact recorded here is what it costs and what it cannot do. The complete graph has n(n − 1)/2 edges, so Kruskal costs Θ(n² log n) for the sort and Prim's array version (§19) costs Θ(n²) — either is the O(n³) naive agglomerative loop with a log factor or more removed, and both compute the entire dendrogram at once, since the merge sequence is the acceptance sequence. The audit at the foot of this page re-checks the equivalence on 120 random graphs: cutting the k − 1 heaviest tree edges gives k clusters whose spacing is the lightest cut edge. What single linkage cannot do is resist chaining: the MST is willing to connect two dense groups through a thin bridge of points, because the tree minimises total edge weight and never asks how far apart the endpoints of a path are — the MST-against-SPT contrast of §20 (a path of 13 for a distance of 10 on six vertices, a factor approaching V − 1 in general) is the same phenomenon. Complete and average linkage do not reduce to a spanning-tree computation for that reason: their inter-cluster distance depends on all pairs, not the closest, and the cut property's exchange argument no longer applies. Two more uses of the tree are exact. The maximum spanning tree (§20, by negation) of the complete graph whose edge weights are pairwise mutual informations is the tree-structured joint distribution closest in KL divergence to the data — the Chow–Liu construction — because the log-likelihood of a tree factorisation decomposes into a sum of edge mutual informations plus a constant, so maximising it is a maximum-weight spanning tree, solved by Kruskal on −I(X; Y). And the minimum-bottleneck property (§16) makes the MST path between two points the minimax path — the route whose largest jump is smallest — which is the distance density-based methods want and the reason single-linkage clusters coincide with the connected components of the ε-neighbourhood graph at every threshold ε at once. Spectral clustering, which the clustering page lists among the density- and model-based methods, is often described as "a graph cut", and it is worth being precise: the normalised and ratio cuts it approximates are balanced partitions with no designated source and sink, which are NP-hard, and the eigenvector computation is a relaxation of them; the s–t minimum cut of §22 is a different problem, solvable exactly, and it is the next item.

Minimum cut as exact MAP inference: graph cuts

§26 built the network whose minimum cut is the global minimum of a binary labelling energy E(x) = Σ_p D_p(x_p) + λ · #{p ~ q : x_p ≠ x_q}, and checked it against all 512 labellings of a 3 × 3 grid. The Image Segmentation page states the general condition and its consequences; the algorithmic content is this. A pairwise term V_pq(x_p, x_q) can be represented by capacities if and only if it is submodular, V_pq(0, 0) + V_pq(1, 1) ≤ V_pq(0, 1) + V_pq(1, 0), because after the standard rearrangement the smoothness capacity between p and q is V_pq(0, 1) + V_pq(1, 0) − V_pq(0, 0) − V_pq(1, 1) and a capacity cannot be negative (§21); the Potts term λ·[x_p ≠ x_q] with λ ≥ 0 is the simplest submodular term, and contrast-sensitive weights λ_pq = λ · exp(−(I_p − I_q)²/2σ²) are submodular for the same reason. Under that condition the minimum cut is the exact MAP labelling of the corresponding Markov random field — the pairwise energy is −log of an Ising-type prior, the data term −log of a likelihood — and the max-flow min-cut theorem (§22) is the certificate: the flow value proves no labelling is cheaper. For more than two labels the reduction is no longer exact; α-expansion solves a sequence of binary problems, each a minimum cut that asks "which pixels should switch to label α", and is guaranteed only to reach a local minimum, within a known constant factor of the optimum when the smoothness term is a metric. The solvers matter because the networks are large and structured — a megapixel image is a grid of a million vertices and four million neighbour arcs — and §24 named the choice: push-relabel with the gap and global-relabelling heuristics, or the augmenting-path algorithm that reuses its search trees between augmentations, which on grids with short augmenting paths beats the general-purpose bounds; Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) (§23) is the wrong tool at that scale and the right one on the page's 3 × 3 instance, where it makes 13 augmentations. Conditional random fields with the same binary submodular structure — foreground/background, in/out of a region, before/after a boundary — are solved the same way, and the segmentation page's λ sweep is a picture of the regularisation path: energies non-decreasing in λ, boundary length non-increasing, individual pixels not monotone (pixel 5 flips 1 → 0 → 1 → 0 on this page's grid).

Viterbi is the DAG algorithm, and beam search is not

The Viterbi decoder finds the most probable state sequence of a hidden Markov model or a linear-chain CRF; Dynamic Programming, §26 derives it as a table. Here it is a graph: the trellis has a vertex for every (state, time) pair plus a source and a sink, an arc from (i, t − 1) to (j, t) of weight −log a_ij − log b_j(o_t), and the most probable sequence is the shortest source-to-sink path, because a product of probabilities is a sum of negative log-probabilities. The trellis is a DAG — every arc advances time — so §09 applies with no condition on the weights: one pass in topological order (time-major), each of the (T − 1)·S² + 2S arcs relaxed once, Θ(T·S²) — the DP page's count of (T − 1)·S² + T·S = 63 multiplications for S = 3, T = 6 is the 45 inter-layer arcs relaxed once each plus one emission factor per (state, time). The weights happen to be non-negative, so Dijkstra (§04) would also be correct — and would be slower, because it pays a log per queue operation for an order the topological sort gives for free; the typical situation in which Dijkstra is the better decoder is a lattice that is not a trellis, such as a word graph with arcs of varying length, where the goal is reached long before most of the graph has been expanded and A* with an admissible completion estimate (§15) expands fewer vertices still. Beam search is neither: it keeps the B best partial paths at each time and discards the rest, so it is a heuristic pruning of the DAG pass that can discard the prefix of the optimal path — the DP page counted a width-1 beam missing the optimum on 33 of the 64 observation strings of its model — and it has no counterpart on this page because nothing on this page discards a vertex before its estimate is final. CTC's forward recursion, the forward algorithm, and the sum-product versions of all of these are the same trellis with (min, +) replaced by (+, ×), the semiring change of §12, and they compute a marginal rather than a path.

Planning: value iteration is Bellman-Ford, and Dijkstra is its priority order

The Bellman optimality equation of Reinforcement Learning, V*(s) = min_a [c(s, a) + Σ_s′ P(s′ | s, a) V*(s′)] for costs, becomes a shortest-path problem exactly when the transitions are deterministic and undiscounted: then V*(s) = min_a [c(s, a) + V*(next(s, a))] is the triangle-inequality fixed point of §02 read backwards, V*(s) = δ(s, goal), and the value-iteration sweep "update every state from its successors" is one round of Bellman-Ford on the reversed graph — the DP view of §08, in which round k is exact for every state whose optimal path has at most k steps. That correspondence gives value iteration its convergence count on such problems (at most V − 1 sweeps from the initialisation "0 at the goal, ∞ elsewhere", not the log(1/ε)/log(1/γ) of the discounted case) and explains why negative step costs are dangerous there and harmless here: a negative cycle is an unbounded reward, and the test round of §07 is the detector. When step costs are non-negative, Dijkstra's order — settle the state with the smallest current value, never revisit it — is exactly the priority that prioritised sweeping and Dijkstra-style planners use, and §05 says what it needs: w ≥ 0, no more and no less. With stochastic transitions the problem is a stochastic shortest path, the expectation replaces the single successor, the greedy order is no longer valid in general, and value iteration's rounds remain the safe algorithm — the same relationship, one level up, as Bellman-Ford's to Dijkstra's on this page. Temporal-difference learning is the same backup sampled from experience, and A* with a learned heuristic is Dijkstra with a potential (§15), which is why an inconsistent learned heuristic must be run with reopening or accepted as approximate.

Optimal transport and assignment: minimum-cost flow and maximum-weight matching

The earth-mover distance between two discrete distributions — the minimum total work, mass times distance, to reshape one histogram into the other — is a transportation problem: a bipartite network with a vertex per bin on each side, supplies equal to the source masses, demands equal to the target masses, an arc from every source bin to every target bin with cost equal to the ground distance and unbounded capacity, and the answer is a minimum-cost flow of value equal to the total mass — the pointer at the end of §26, solved by successive shortest paths with Bellman-Ford or, with Johnson-style potentials (§14) keeping the reduced costs non-negative, with Dijkstra. That is the Wasserstein-1 distance the Wasserstein GAN page introduces through the same dirt-moving picture; the GAN uses its dual form (a supremum over 1-Lipschitz critics) rather than solving the flow, because the distributions are continuous and represented by samples. In one dimension the flow has a closed form — sort both samples and pair them in order, which is the reason the drift-monitoring distances can be computed per feature in O(n log n) — and the entropically regularised version solved by Sinkhorn iterations is a matrix-scaling relaxation, not a flow algorithm, which trades exactness for parallelism. Two special cases collapse further. When both sides have n points of equal mass, the transport plan can be taken to be a permutation (the integrality of §22 applied to the transportation network), and the problem is the assignment problem: a minimum-cost perfect matching in a complete bipartite graph, §25's matching with costs, solved by the Hungarian method in O(n³) or as a min-cost flow of value n. That is the data-association step of multi-object tracking — detections on one side, tracks on the other, cost a distance between predicted and observed positions, with a dummy vertex per side so that unmatched detections can start tracks and unmatched tracks can end — for which these notes have no page; the nearest treatment is the feature-matching strategies of the vision notes, where the mutual-nearest-neighbour rule with a ratio test is a greedy approximation to the same assignment, fast and without the optimality guarantee a matching gives. And when the costs are 0/1 — "compatible or not" — the assignment is the unweighted maximum bipartite matching of §25 with Hall's condition as the feasibility test, which is how the audit at the foot of this page checks it.

What is not a shortest path, and what it is instead

Three graph computations that machine learning uses constantly resemble the algorithms of this page and are not them. PageRank, and its sentence-graph form TextRank in extractive summarisation, is the stationary distribution of a random walk with restarts: the dominant eigenvector of a stochastic matrix, computed by power iteration in O(E) per iteration, converging geometrically at the damping rate. Nothing is minimised along a path; the quantity is a fixed point of averaging, and the relevant theory is spectral (the Perron–Frobenius eigenvector), not relaxation. Message passing in graph neural networks repeats L rounds of "aggregate the neighbours' vectors, update your own", and its L-hop receptive field has exactly the shape of Bellman-Ford's rounds (§08), in which round k reaches every vertex within k edges; the difference is that the aggregation and update are learned functions rather than min and +. The correspondence is more than a metaphor in one direction: a message-passing layer whose message is d[u] + w(u, v) and whose aggregation is min is one round of Bellman-Ford, so a network with that inductive bias can represent shortest paths exactly, and the observation that architectures aligned with a target algorithm's recurrence learn it from fewer examples is the reason "neural algorithmic reasoning" benchmarks use Bellman-Ford, Dijkstra, Prim and the like as targets; a network with sum or mean aggregation cannot represent the min and only approximates. Greedy search in a vector index such as HNSW, in the vector-database notes, hops from a vertex to whichever neighbour is closest to the query and stops when none is closer: it is a descent on a navigable graph with no optimality guarantee, tuned by the beam width ef, and it answers "an approximate nearest neighbour" rather than "the shortest path"; the theory behind it is about the graph's construction (long-range links that make greedy routing succeed), not about relaxation. Knowing which of the four roles a graph computation plays is the practical skill: an exact solver can be trusted to the last unit and checked by its certificate (a cut, a cover, a dual), an inference engine is exact for its model and only as good as the model, a geometric primitive is exact for the graph it is given and only as good as the graph, and an iteration that resembles a shortest path should not be given a shortest path's guarantees.

28 Choosing the algorithm: the decision procedure

The algorithm for a weighted-graph problem is decided by four questions asked in order — what is being asked (one source, all pairs, one target, a tree, a flow), what the weights are (unit, small integers, non-negative, arbitrary, capacities), what structure the graph has (a DAG, sparse or dense, implicit or explicit, bipartite), and how often the question will be asked — and every bound quoted in the answer carries the hypothesis it was proved under. The procedure below is the page's contents rearranged as a decision tree, with the cost of each branch stated as "bound, under hypothesis" rather than as a bare Big-O, because most of the wrong choices on this page are a correct bound applied outside its hypothesis: Dijkstra's O((V + E) log V) without w ≥ 0, Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) for a depth-first path rule, the array's Θ(V²) on a sparse graph, union-find's α(n) with one heuristic, Ford-Fulkerson's termination on irrational capacities. The table that follows is the same procedure as a lookup; §30 collapses it further to one row per algorithm.

the decision procedure — ask in this order1. WHAT IS ASKED? one source (δ(s, ·)) → 2; one source, one target → 2 then 7; all pairs → 8; a spanning tree → 9; "how much can be routed" or "cheapest separation" → 10 2. ARE THE WEIGHTS UNIT? all equal → BFS, Θ(V + E) (Graph Representations & Traversal §12) — a heap is a log factor wasted (§04, §11) in {0, 1} → 0-1 BFS, O(V + E) (§11); integers in 0 … C with C < log V → Dial's buckets, O(E + V·C) (§11); otherwise → 3 3. IS THE GRAPH A DAG? yes → one pass in topological order, Θ(V + E), ANY weights, longest paths for free (§09); not known → topological sort decides it in Θ(V + E); no → 4 4. ARE ALL WEIGHTS ≥ 0? yes → Dijkstra (§04–§05), and choose the queue by density (§06): E ≲ V²/log V → binary heap, O((V + E) log V); E ≈ V² → array, Θ(V²); Fibonacci heap, O(E + V log V) amortised, only when E ≫ V log V and the constants are measured to pay; lazy deletion when the library heap has no decrease-key no → 5 5. NEGATIVE EDGES, ONE SOURCE? Bellman-Ford, O(VE) with early exit, and the V-th round as the negative-cycle test (§07–§08); the constant-shift "fix" is wrong (§03); a few known negative edges, no negative cycle → k + 1 alternating Dijkstra phases, O(k·(V + E) log V) (§05); many queries → Johnson's potentials once, then Dijkstra per query (§14) 6. IS IT A CONSTRAINT SYSTEM? xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ b → the constraint graph with a zero-edge source, Bellman-Ford, O(n·m); a negative cycle is the infeasibility certificate (§10) 7. ONE TARGET, A HEURISTIC? admissible h → A* returns the optimum (with reopening if h is inconsistent); consistent h → each vertex expanded once, Dijkstra's bound, fewer expansions (§15); no heuristic but one target → Dijkstra stopped when t is extracted, or bidirectional search 8. ALL PAIRS: w ≥ 0 → V × Dijkstra, O(V·(V + E) log V) (§12); negative edges, sparse → Johnson, O(V² log V + VE) Fibonacci / O(VE log V) binary (§14); dense (E ≈ V²) → Floyd-Warshall, Θ(V³), in place, with the diagonal as the negative-cycle test (§13); reachability only → transitive closure, or V traversals if sparse 9. MINIMUM SPANNING TREE: edges arrive sorted, or are small integers, or the graph is an edge list / a stream → Kruskal, O(E·α(V)) after the sort (§17–§18); adjacency structure, dense → Prim with an array, Θ(V²); sparse → Prim with a heap, O(E log V) (§19); parallel rounds, no sort, no heap → Borůvka, O(E log V) (§20) k clusters → Kruskal stopped early; bottleneck path → any MST; second-best → one swap, O(V²) (§20) 10. MAXIMUM FLOW / MIN CUT: small integer capacities and a tiny graph → any Ford-Fulkerson, O(E·|f*|) (§22); general → Edmonds-Karp O(VE²) (§23) up to a few thousand vertices; integer capacities with a wide range, E small → capacity scaling O(E² log U), weakly polynomial (§22); larger, or unit capacities → Dinic O(V²E), O(E√V) on unit networks (§23); dense or grid-structured (segmentation) → push-relabel with gap + global relabelling (§24) bipartite matching → Hopcroft-Karp O(E√V) (§25); costs on the arcs (assignment, transport) → min-cost flow, successive shortest paths with potentials (§26); several commodities, or side constraints → linear programming

Shortest paths: the sign of the weights first, then the structure, then the query pattern

The first question after "one source?" is not the algorithm but the weights, because the weights decide which invariants are available. Non-negative weights license the greedy invariant — the closest tentative vertex is final — and with it every Dijkstra variant, 0-1 BFS, Dial and A*; a single negative edge withdraws the licence, and §05's slider shows the first failure at w = −2 on a four-vertex DAG that has a perfectly well-defined δ. The second question is structure, and it can overrule the first: a DAG has an evaluation order that makes the sign irrelevant (§09), so the topological sort should be attempted before Bellman-Ford is reached for — it costs Θ(V + E), the same as the sweep that follows it, and it either succeeds or reports a cycle. The third question is what the weights are made of when they are non-negative: equal weights are BFS and a heap is waste; weights in {0, 1} are a deque; integers below log V are buckets; and only real or large-integer weights need a comparison-based queue, at which point density chooses between the heap and the array on the terms §06 measured — the array's Θ(V²) beats the heap's O((V + E) log V) on the bound's accounting from the first sampled density above the algebraic threshold, 0.15 at V = 128 (the threshold itself is E > 2,213, density ≈ 0.135), and never on the measured sift steps for random weights, so the honest advice is the heap unless the array is measured to win on the actual inputs. The fourth question is the query pattern. One target with a heuristic is A*, and a consistent heuristic is a potential (§15) that costs nothing in correctness; one target without one is Dijkstra stopped at the target's extraction, and bidirectional search halves the radius. Many sources, or all pairs, is §12–§14's table: V Dijkstras for non-negative sparse graphs, Johnson for negative sparse ones, Floyd-Warshall for dense ones, and the boolean closure when only reachability is wanted. And a source that is really a constraint system — inequalities xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ b — is Bellman-Ford from the zero-edge source with the negative cycle read as the answer (§10), which is the one case where a negative cycle is an output rather than an error.

All pairs: density decides, and the negative-cycle test comes free

The three all-pairs strategies differ by a factor of V/log V in either direction depending on density, so the choice is arithmetic: E log V against V². Below that crossover — road networks, meshes, most graphs that arise from data — run a single-source algorithm from every vertex: Dijkstra if the weights are non-negative, Johnson's reweighting first if they are not, and never V Bellman-Fords, which Johnson dominates at the cost of one Bellman-Ford. Above it, Floyd-Warshall's three loops with k outermost are hard to beat: no heap, no adjacency structure, a contiguous matrix, and a negative-cycle test that is one glance at the diagonal. Repeated squaring is the algorithm to know rather than run — a log factor slower than Floyd-Warshall, and the reason (§12) that no Strassen-type shortcut exists for distances while one does for reachability. Two practical notes. The output is Θ(V²) whatever the method, so at V = 10⁵ the question is moot and the answer is "queries on demand, cached", i.e. Dijkstra per query with potentials or landmarks. And when the graph will be queried many times but not for all pairs, Johnson's step 2 — one Bellman-Ford to get potentials — is worth doing once even for a handful of queries, since it converts every later query from O(VE) to O((V + E) log V).

Minimum spanning trees: how the edges arrive

All three MST algorithms are O(E log V) in their standard forms and all three are correct by the same lemma, so the choice is about representation and side effects. Kruskal wants an edge list and pays for a sort: if the edges are already sorted, or integer-weighted (counting sort), or arrive as a stream, the loop's O(E·α(V)) is the whole cost and nothing else comes close; it also produces the spanning forest of a disconnected graph for free and, stopped early, the single-linkage clustering (§20). Prim wants an adjacency structure and a queue: with an array it is Θ(V²), which beats every sort-based method on dense graphs, and it grows a connected tree from a root, which is what an implicit graph (edges generated on demand from a vertex) or an early-stopped partial tree needs. Borůvka wants neither a sort nor a heap, does its work in ⌈log₂ V⌉ rounds each of which is a parallel scan of the edges, and is the base of the randomised linear-time algorithm; it needs distinct weights or the (w, index) tie-break, and §20 shows the cycle that ties can otherwise close. The union-find inside Kruskal must have both heuristics for the α(n) bound — §18 measured 104 hops per operation with neither, 2.3 with rank alone, 2.7 with compression alone and 1.3 with both on the random workload, and 799 against 1.0 on the chain — and the "bottleneck", "second-best" and "clustering" questions are all answered from the tree in O(V²) or less rather than by a new algorithm.

Flow: size and structure, and when to leave the combinatorial world

Every flow algorithm on the page is Ford-Fulkerson with a rule, and the rule is chosen by size. On a graph small enough to enumerate its cuts — the page's instances — any rule works and the brute-force cut is the check. On thousands of vertices Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²) is fine in practice because the augmentation count sits far below VE (3 against 54 on six vertices; at most 8 against 128 over 300 random eight-vertex networks); on larger sparse graphs and on all unit-capacity networks Dinic's level graphs win, with O(E√V) on matching networks; and on dense or grid-structured networks — the segmentation problems of §26–§27 — push-relabel with the gap and global-relabelling heuristics, or a search-tree-reusing augmenting-path solver, is what the libraries run. Bipartite matching is Hopcroft-Karp, which is Dinic on the unit network, and the cover and the Hall violator come from the final residual reachability at no extra cost (§25). The step outside this page is taken when the problem acquires costs (assignment, transportation, the earth-mover distance of §27): minimum-cost flow, whose successive-shortest-path algorithm reuses Bellman-Ford or potential-reweighted Dijkstra from §07 and §14. And the step outside combinatorial optimisation is taken when the problem acquires several commodities sharing capacities, or side constraints that are not flow conservation, or when a fractional answer is acceptable: then the max-flow LP of §21 generalises and the flow algorithms do not — the integrality theorem is a theorem about one commodity, and a two-commodity instance can have no integral optimum at all. Knowing where that boundary lies is the last decision, and it is the one the NP-completeness notes take up from the other side.

The one-paragraph version. Ask what is wanted; then look at the weights (unit → BFS, 0/1 → deque, small integers → buckets, non-negative → Dijkstra with the queue chosen by density, negative → Bellman-Ford, a DAG → one pass whatever the weights); then at the query pattern (a heuristic → A*, all pairs → Johnson when sparse and Floyd-Warshall when dense). For a spanning tree, ask how the edges arrive (sorted or streamed → Kruskal, adjacency and dense → Prim's array, parallel → Borůvka), and build the union-find with both heuristics. For a flow, ask how big (Edmonds-Karp, then Dinic, then push-relabel) and whether there are costs (min-cost flow) or several commodities (linear programming). Attach the hypothesis to every bound you quote, and check the answer on a small instance against brute force before trusting the routine — which is what every figure on this page does.

29 Pitfalls

Every pitfall below was either exhibited by a routine on this page and caught by its brute-force comparison, or measured by running a deliberately wrong variant of a routine against the correct one on the page's own instances — the wrong answers are numbers, not hypotheticals. They fall into three kinds. A hypothesis was dropped: a bound or an invariant proved under w ≥ 0, "bipartite", "both heuristics", "integer capacities" or "shortest augmenting path" was applied without it, and the algorithm returned a plausible wrong number or ran for far longer than the quoted bound. A certificate was misread: a vertex was taken to be on the cycle when it was merely downstream of it, a finite estimate was taken for a distance, a tie was taken for a property of the problem. Or a step was skipped: the test round, the reverse arc, the stale check, the outermost loop. The cure is the habit the figures practise — run the routine beside an enumeration on an instance small enough to enumerate, and do not trust it until they agree — and the load-time audit of §30 does that for every family on the page.

  • A negative edge into S makes Dijkstra finalise a wrong estimate, and no implementation variant repairs it (§05). On the four-vertex graph s→a (2), s→b (1), a→b (−3), b→c (1), Dijkstra extracts b at key 1 and finalises it; the truth is δ(s, b) = −1. The variant that skips edges into S returns d = ⟨0, 2, 1, 2⟩ against δ = ⟨0, 2, −1, 0⟩, wrong at b and c; the variant that relaxes the edge anyway but never re-extracts fixes b by accident and still returns d[c] = 2. The slider's sweep finds the first failing weight at −2 for both variants — at −1 the route ties the direct edge and survives — which is exactly the boundary the proof's step (2) draws: a stretch of weight zero past a finalised vertex is harmless, a strictly negative one is not. The errors are always too high (upper-bound property), which is why "Dijkstra ran without complaint" proves nothing: nothing in its state records that a finalised vertex was later improved.
  • Adding a constant to every weight does not repair negative edges; adding a potential does (§03). On s→t (1), s→a (2), a→t (−2) the true distance to t is 0 by the two-edge route; shifting every weight by +2 makes that route cost 4 and the direct edge 3, and Dijkstra on the shifted graph returns the direct edge — original weight 1 — and does so for every shift c ≥ 2, because a path with k edges gains k·c and the shift is a per-edge toll. Johnson's reweighting ŵ = w + h(u) − h(v) is a per-endpoint toll that every s ⇝ v path pays equally: with h = ⟨0, 0, −2⟩ the three edges become 3, 2, 0, Dijkstra returns ⟨0, 2, 2⟩, and un-reweighting gives ⟨0, 2, 0⟩ = δ. The difference between the fallacy and the theorem is the telescoping sum of §14, and nothing else.
  • Bellman-Ford without the V-th round returns finite numbers where δ = −∞ (§07). On the five-vertex instance with 4→1 = −6, four rounds end with d = ⟨0, 1, −2, 5, 3⟩; a routine that stops there hands those back as distances. The fifth round lowers three more estimates, to ⟨0, −3, −6, 2, 3⟩, and that is the report: the true values for 1, 2, 3, 4 are −∞, the brute force over the 3 simple cycles reachable from s finds the lightest at −7, and the finite numbers are the state of a process that would descend forever. The early exit is the reason the omission survives: on every instance without a negative cycle the extra round changes nothing and looks redundant.
  • The vertex whose estimate fell in the test round need not be on the cycle, and the −∞ set is not just the cycle (§07–§08). The witness vertex is on the cycle or downstream of it, which is why the routine walks π back V times before collecting the cycle — on §07's instance the walk 1 → 4 → 3 → 2 → 1 → 4 lands on 4 and the cycle is read from there. And the set of vertices with δ = −∞ is everything reachable from a reachable negative cycle, computed as the forward closure of the vertices improved in the test round. On §07's graph the cycle's vertices and the −∞ set happen to coincide — {1, 2, 3, 4} both — so that instance cannot distinguish the right rule from the wrong one, and a test suite built from it would pass a routine that reports only the cycle's vertices; the audit's 36 random negative-cycle instances, where the two sets differ whenever a vertex hangs off the cycle, are what check the rule against brute force.
  • Floyd-Warshall's k must be the outermost loop (§13). The invariant is "after stage k, intermediates are drawn from {1 … k}", and it is the induction; with k innermost the cell (i, j) is finished before later intermediates are available to it. Measured by the checking script with the loops reordered: 2 of the 25 cells wrong on the §13 graph and 1 of 16 on the §12 graph, and a wrong matrix on 35 of 165 random graphs without negative cycles — never on the diagonal, never for row 0 of those two instances, which is why a spot check of one source can miss it. The order of i and j inside k is free (§13 proves the in-place update is safe because row and column k do not change during stage k); the order of k is not.
  • Johnson's potential must come from an added source that reaches everything, not from an existing vertex (§14). Any h satisfying h(v) ≤ h(u) + w(u, v) on every edge works, and the distances from an ordinary vertex s satisfy it wherever they are finite: on the §13 graph, which is strongly connected, h = δ(0, ·) = ⟨0, 1, −2, 2, 0⟩ gives ŵ = ⟨5, 0, 0, 0, 0, 9, 3, 1⟩, all non-negative, and the algorithm runs correctly — which is how the shortcut earns trust it does not deserve. The moment some vertex is unreachable from s its h is ∞, ŵ on its edges is ∞ − ∞, and the reweighted graph is not a graph. The added source with a zero-weight edge to every vertex is what makes every h finite, and it costs one vertex and V edges.
  • An inadmissible heuristic makes A* fast and wrong, and not reliably wrong (§15). Doubling the Manhattan distance on the 9 × 14 grid expands 49 cells instead of Manhattan's 50 and returns a 25-step path where BFS finds 21; on the second grid the same heuristic expands 43 cells and happens to return the optimal 19. A heuristic that fails only sometimes cannot be caught by a demo; the guarantee that survives is weighted A*'s cost ≤ ε·δ, here 25 ≤ 42, which is a different promise from optimality. Consistency is the property that lets the closed set be final; an admissible but inconsistent heuristic needs reopening, and the figure's routine counts reopenings — 0 for all three admissible heuristics on both grids — because that count is the test.
  • Union-find's α(n) needs both heuristics; either alone has a different bound, and neither alone is "almost constant" on the wrong workload (§18). On the random workload of 5 000 operations over 1 000 elements the measured hops per operation are 104.47 with neither heuristic, 2.27 with rank only, 2.65 with compression only and 1.28 with both; on the chain adversary — 999 sequential unions, then 4 001 finds of the deepest element — naive linking costs 799.40 hops per operation and every heuristic combination 1.00. The theorems are: rank alone Θ(m log n) worst case, compression alone Θ(m·log1+m/n n), both O(m·α(n)); the "as good as constant" reading rests on the table α(n) ≤ 4 for n ≤ A₄(1), which is a statement about both heuristics together. Kruskal on the shared instance cannot show the difference — 9 hops with both against 12 with neither over 18 finds — so a routine tested only on toy graphs will not reveal that its union-find has one heuristic.
  • Kruskal's test is find(u) ≠ find(v), not "is either endpoint already in the forest" (§17). The tempting shortcut — reject an edge whose endpoints are both already touched — rejects AB (4) at the fourth step on the shared instance, because A was touched by AD and B by BE, though they are in different trees. The script that runs that rule returns three edges {CF, AD, BE} of weight 6 and stops: a forest, not a spanning tree. The opposite shortcut, no test at all, accepts all nine edges (weight 41) and builds cycles. Only the union-find question "same tree?" is the cut property's condition, and it is asked 2E = 18 times on the instance.
  • Borůvka with tied weights and a per-vertex tie-break can add a cycle (§20). On a triangle with all weights 1, vertex x may choose xy, y choose yz and z choose zx — three edges, one cycle, and A is no longer a forest. The routine compares (w, index) lexicographically, which makes every comparison behave as if weights were distinct, and returns 2 edges on that triangle; the audit runs it on 57 tied-weight random graphs and finds a spanning tree of minimum weight every time. The tie-break is not a nicety: the proof that the chosen edges are acyclic uses "the heaviest edge on a cycle of chosen edges was chosen by a component that had a lighter option", which needs a strict order.
  • The minimum spanning tree is not the shortest-path tree, and from a different root it is a different comparison (§16, §20). From root A on the shared instance the MST is {AB, AD, BE, EF, CF} (15) and the shortest-path tree is {AB, AD, BE, BC, CF} (16); the MST's path to C is A–B–E–F–C = 13 against δ(A, C) = 10, a stretch of 1.30. From root C the SPT weighs 21; the largest excess is at D — 15 along the MST against δ = 11, 4 units — while the largest stretch ratio is at B, 9 against 6 (1.50): the two "worst" vertices need not coincide, and the figure's readout reports the largest excess. Prim and Dijkstra are one loop with one line changed (w(u, v) against d[u] + w(u, v)), and a program that uses the wrong key returns a spanning tree with a plausible weight — the SPT weighs 16, one more than the minimum — and no error. Compare against brute force over the 55 trees, or at least check the tree's weight against a second MST algorithm, as every MST figure does.
  • Forgetting the reverse residual arcs turns Ford-Fulkerson into a greedy heuristic (§21). Without cancellation the depth-first rule on the six-vertex network stops at value 19 after three augmentations, because its first path sent 4 units the long way through v₃→v₂ and only the reverse arc v₂→v₃ can take them back; with the reverse arcs it reaches 23 in four. On the pathology it stops at 199 of 200, and on the five-vertex push-relabel instance it happens to reach the optimum — three instances, three different behaviours, and the last is the dangerous one. The max-flow min-cut theorem's (2) ⇒ (3) is about the residual network with both kinds of arc; "no forward path left" is not "no augmenting path left".
  • Ford-Fulkerson's termination is a theorem about integer capacities, and its cost is a theorem about the path rule (§22). With M = 100 the "worst" rule needs 200 augmentations and 2 402 arc scans on four vertices to move 200 units, one at a time across the middle edge and back; depth-first needs 4 and BFS 2. The bound O(E·|f*|) is not pessimism — the figure attains it — and with irrational capacities the method can fail to terminate at all, converging to a value below the maximum. Every count on this page is for integer capacities, and the bound that does not depend on them is Edmonds-Karp's O(VE²), which needs the BFS rule and nothing else.
  • Antiparallel edges must each own their residual pair; a residual capacity stored per unordered pair conflates room with cancellation (§21, §26). The definition's convention subdivides one of an antiparallel pair; the routine gives every input edge its own forward and reverse arc, so c_f(u, v) on the forward arc of (u, v) is "room left" and on the reverse arc of (v, u) is "flow to cancel", and they are never added. The segmentation network of §26 is made of such pairs — 24 neighbour arcs on the 3 × 3 grid, λ each way — and its minimum cut of 22 at λ = 3 agrees with brute force over 512 labellings because each direction's capacity is severed separately. An implementation that keys residual capacity on {u, v} would count λ once for a cut that must pay it in one direction and would silently mis-price every boundary.
  • Kruskal and Prim are for undirected graphs; run on a directed graph they answer a question nobody asked (§16). Treating the arcs as edges and running either algorithm returns a spanning tree of the underlying undirected graph, which as a set of arcs is in general not an arborescence: some vertices have two incoming arcs and others none, so it is not a rooted out-tree from any root, and its weight is a lower bound on the arborescence weight that need not be attained (three vertices r, a, b with arcs r→a (10), a→b (1), b→r (1): Kruskal on the arcs as edges returns {a→b, b→r}, weight 2, in which a has no incoming arc and r has one — not an out-tree from r; the cheapest arborescence rooted at r is {r→a, a→b}, weight 11, found by brute force over the two candidate arc sets). The directed problem is the minimum spanning arborescence, solved by the contraction algorithm of §16's opening; the cut property, which every MST algorithm on this page rests on, is a theorem about undirected cuts.
  • König's theorem is for bipartite graphs only (§25). A triangle has a maximum matching of size 1 and a minimum vertex cover of size 2; max |M| = min |C| fails, and so does the flow reduction, which needs the two sides to place the source and sink. The augmenting-path characterisation of a maximum matching does hold in general graphs, but finding the paths there needs blossom handling that this page does not build. The routine's inputs are typed as {nL, nR, adj} for that reason, and Hall's condition — derived from König — inherits the restriction.
  • A tied minimum makes the returned labelling a convention, not a property of the energy (§26). On the 3 × 3 grid the minimum energy is unique at λ = 0, 3, 4, 5 but attained by two labellings at λ = 1 and three at λ = 2; the figure draws the source side of the final residual reachability, which is the minimum cut with the fewest foreground pixels, and a different max-flow implementation would legitimately draw another. Pixel 5 flips 1 → 0 → 1 → 0 as λ rises, so per-pixel labels are not monotone in the smoothness weight even though the energy and the boundary length are. Compare energies across implementations, or fix the tie rule; comparing labellings will fail on every tie.
  • Lazy deletion without the stale check keeps the distances and loses everything else (§06, §19). A popped pair whose key exceeds the vertex's current estimate, or whose vertex is already done, must be discarded. With weights ≥ 0 the distances survive its omission — a stale entry cannot lower an estimate below the truth — which is why the bug is invisible in a distance check; what it costs is work and correctness downstream. On the six-vertex instance the omission makes 17 relaxations instead of 10; on the dense random graphs of §06 it makes 2 241 relaxations against 1 024 at V = 64 and 10 449 against 4 096 at V = 128, a factor that grows with the stale count. And in Prim's lazy version the same omission adds an edge for a vertex already in the tree: on the shared instance it returns 10 edges of weight 47, with BC twice, instead of 5 edges of weight 15. The check is one comparison per pop; the figures count the stale pops (4 of 10 on §04's graph, 203 of 331 at V = 128) so that its necessity is visible.
  • The DAG figure's "shortest" toggle is not a scheduling quantity (§09). The sweep with min returns 10 for the task DAG — the length of the shortest chain from S to T, A–C–E–G or B–D–E–G — and no project finishes in 10 time units: the earliest finish is the longest chain, 12, and every task's earliest start is a longest path. The toggle exists to show that the code path is identical and only the comparison changes; reading its output as a schedule is the mistake, and it is easy to make because both numbers come from the same routine with the same relaxation count of 11 per sweep (22 for the two sweeps §09 runs).
  • Bellman-Ford's edge order changes the work, not the answer or the worst case (§07–§08). On the five-vertex instance the listed order — the shortest paths' edges in reverse — needs 5 rounds and 40 relaxations; the reversed list needs 2 rounds and 16. Both end at ⟨0, 4, 1, 5, 3⟩. A benchmark that happens to meet the shortest paths in order will report a near-linear algorithm; the DP view says the two-array recurrence takes exactly V − 1 rounds regardless (32 relaxations either way on that instance), and the queue-based variant's typical speed carries no guarantee. Quote O(VE), and measure the early exit as a bonus.
  • A bound for one queue is not "Dijkstra's bound", and the bound's own accounting is not a measurement (§06). Θ(V²), O((V + E) log V), O((V + E) log E) and O(E + V log V) amortised are four different theorems about four data structures. On the bound's accounting the array should win from the first sampled density above the algebraic threshold, 0.15 at V = 128 (the threshold itself is E > 2 213, density ≈ 0.135); counting the sift steps the heap actually took, it never does on random weights — 9 760 steps to the array's 24 512 at full density, with only 366 of 16 256 edges producing a decrease-key. The number the Fibonacci heap's bound charges for, E decrease-keys, was 708 of 16 384 on the dense family at V = 256. Recommendations that rest on the accounting are not wrong, but they are statements about adversarial inputs, and the page says so where it makes them.

Where this goes next in the series. The three problems this page named and did not solve — the Steiner tree of §20, the shortest simple path with negative cycles of §03, and the integral multi-commodity flow of §28 (its fractional form is the linear program §28 recommends, and is polynomial), all NP-hard where their single-commodity or all-vertex cousins are polynomial — are the subject of the NP-completeness and approximation part, together with the travelling salesman and the flow linear program's role in approximation by rounding; it is reached through the series map. Upstream, the representations and traversals every algorithm here is built on, BFS's shortest-path theorem for unit weights and the topological sort of §09, are Graph Representations & Traversal; the priority queues that make Dijkstra's and Prim's schedules cheap, with the index map that decrease-key needs and the Fibonacci heap's amortised cut, are Heaps & Priority Queues; the cut property is stated as the greedy-choice lemma at Greedy Algorithms, §20; and Bellman-Ford and Floyd-Warshall as dynamic programs with an extra parameter, and the DAG pass as the table-filling order every DP walks, are Dynamic Programming, §21.
Where it goes sideways, out of this domain. §27 gave the correspondences with their hypotheses; the pages are: geodesic distances on a neighbourhood graph among the nonlinear alternatives on Dimensionality Reduction, built from the k-nearest-neighbour graph; single-linkage clustering as Kruskal stopped early on Clustering, where spectral methods are the relaxation of a balanced cut, not an s–t cut; the minimum cut as exact MAP inference for binary submodular energies at Image Segmentation, §23; Viterbi as the DAG algorithm on the trellis at Hidden Markov Models & CRFs; value iteration as Bellman-Ford's rounds and Dijkstra as its priority order on Reinforcement Learning; the earth-mover distance as a minimum-cost flow behind the Wasserstein GAN and the one-dimensional closed form in drift monitoring; and the three things that are not shortest paths — PageRank in extractive summarisation, message passing in Graph Neural Networks, greedy routing in vector databases.
